# John

## 1:1 Argument
[Philipp Melanchthon, In Evangelium Ioannis Annotationes (Basel, 1523). English: machine translation, The Faith Received (Mere Orthodoxy); the original follows each comment.]
IN THE BEGINNING &c.

THE TRINITY'S mystery and the incarnation of the Son of God, the Scripture of the Old Testament indicated here and there, but the Scripture of the New Testament explained it more clearly. Indeed, this is the confession of the New Testament, Matthew 16. You are the Son of the living God: and lastly: Baptize every nation in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. But you should understand the confession not of history only, but that which confesses Christ to be the Son of God for this reason, that it may acknowledge the propitiator. And he who is baptized in the name of the Father and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, is baptized for the remission of sins. And just as this is the one confession of the New Testament, so there is one blasphemy, to deny that Christ is the Son of God, that is, not to believe that sin is remitted to oneself through Christ.

It is of no use to recount the persons of the divinity. Confession is of use, that is, faith, by which we believe that we are baptized in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, by which power we are reborn and are made children of God through baptism. And this is the glory of baptism, that we have already been made powerful by the virtue of God, etc. Furthermore, we cannot distinguish the persons with better words than those with which Scripture sounds. They call Him the Father, from whom the Son and the Holy Spirit shine forth. The Son is called the Word by John, and the image of God by Paul, Coloss. 1. and Hebraeos 1. the brightness of His glory and the express image of His substance, from which it can be gathered in some way why He is called the Word by John. For it is a word by which something is represented, and the Father, contemplating Himself, conceives the image of Himself, which is called the Word: and because it is a perfect image, the whole substance of the Father shines back in it: so that that image in which the Father shines is no less God by nature than He who makes it. In creatures there is no similar example: for when the images of bodies shine back in mirrors, they return imperfectly. The image of the Father is perfect, and because He conceives this image by the consideration of Himself, the Father is called the Word. For a word is a cognition representing something. And every consideration of the power of the Father is this Word of His, therefore through the Word as if through the counsel of the Father all things are created, in the Word all things consist. And because the Word is the counsel of the Father, therefore it was fitting for the Word to be incarnated, that He might preach the sentence. and the decree of the Father concerning the remission of sins. Thus John says: He who is in the bosom of the Father, he has declared him to us. And Isaiah calls him the angel of the great counsel. And this is the word of which Numbers 9. By the word of the Lord they pitched their tents: because Paul says that it was Christ who followed Israel in the desert. 1. Corinth. 10.

The Holy Spirit is the very agitation by which all things are quickened, Genesis 1. And the spirit of the Lord was borne over the waters. Item: He breathed into his face the breath of life. The actions of the persons cannot be separated. The Father is he from whom every action arises. The Son is the counsel of acting. The Holy Spirit is the agitation itself. Thus Hilary distinguishes the infinite persons in the eternal, book 2. Species in the image, use in the gift.

It could be added here, that in the renewal of the creature we are illuminated by the living word, and by the Holy Spirit agitating and changing, we are transformed from glory to glory. So that just as Christ is the image of the Father, so we may become his image, as at Coloss. 3. Paul says: That we are renewed by the knowledge of God to his image. But we shall leave these things to the spiritual. Let us foolish ones seize the confession of the Trinity, and glory in it.

Latin:

IN PRINCIPIO &c.

TRINITATIS MYsterium & incarnationem filii dei, scriptura veteris testamenti passim indicavit, sed exposuit clarius novi testamenti scriptura. Immo haec est confessio novi testamenti, Matthaei 16. Tues filius dei vivi: & ultimo: Baptizate omnem gentem in nomine patris, & filii, & spiritussancti. Sed intelligas confessionem non historiae tantum, sed eam quae Christum ideo filium dei confitetur, ut agnoscat placatorem. Et qui baptizatur in nomine patris & filii, & spiritus sancti, in remissionem peccatorum baptizatur. Et sicut haec una est confessio novi testamenti, ita una blasphemia est, negare Christum filium dei esse, hoc est non credere peccatum sibi remitti per Christum.

Nihil prodest personas divinitatis recensere. Confessio prodest, hoc est fides, qua credimus nos in nomine patris, & filii, & spiritussancti baptizatos esse, qua potentia renascimur & efficimur filii dei per baptismum. Atque haec est baptismi gloria, iam nos factos esse potentes virtute del &c. Porrò personas verbis melioribus discernere non possumus quàm quibus scriptura sonat. Patrem vocant, ex quo emicant filius & spiritussanctus. Filius à Ioanne verbum dicitur, à Paulo imago dei, Coloss. 1. & Hebraeos 1. Deradiatio gloriae & character substantiae eius, ex quibus utcunque colligi potest cur à Ioanne verbum dicatur. Est enim verbum quo repraesentatur aliquid, & pater sese intuens concipit sui imaginem, quae verbum dicitur: & quia perfecta imago est, tota substantia patris in ea relucet: ita ut non minus sit natura deus imago illa in qua relucet pater, quam ipse facit. In creaturis nullum simile exemplum est: nam cum in speculis relucent corporum imagines, imperfecte redeunt. Imago patris perfecta est, & quia hanc imaginem consyderatione sui concipit, pater verbum dicitur. Nam verbum est cognitio repraesentans aliquid. Et omnis consyderatio potentiae patris hoc ipsius verbum est, ideo per verbum tanquam per consilium patris omnia creantur, in verbo omnia consistunt. Et quia verbum est patris consilium, ideo oportuit verbum incarnari, quod praedicaret sententiam & decretum patris de remissione peccatorum. Sic Ioannes ait: Qui est in sinu patris, ipse narravit nobis. Et Esaias vocat eum magni consilii angelum. Et hoc verbum est de quo Numerorum 9. Per verbum domini erigebant tentoria: quia Paulus dicit Christum fuisse, qui Israël in deserto secutus sit. 1. Corinth. 10.

Spiritussanctus est ipsissima agitatio, qua omnes res vegetantur, Genesis 1. Et spiritus domini ferebatur super aquas. Item: Spiravit in faciem eius spiraculum vitae. Non possunt separari actiones personarum. Pater est, ex quo omnis actio oritur. Filius consilium agendi. Spiritussanctus ipsa agitatio. Sic Hilarius discernit personas infinitas in aeterno, lib. 2. Species in imagine, usus in munere.

Possit hic adiici, ut in renovatione creaturae per vivum verbum illuminamur, & spiritusancto agitante, & mutante transformamur de claritate in claritatem. Ut sicut Christus est imago patris, ita nos fiamus imago eius, ut ad Coloss. 3. Paulus ait: Renovari nos agnitione dei ad imaginem eius. Sed haec spiritualibus relinquemus. Nos stulti arripiamus confessionem Trinitatis, & in illa gloriemur.

## 1:1
In the beginning was the word etc.

Paul distinguishes two creatures in Christ thus Romans 1. Concerning his son, who was born of the seed of David according to the flesh, who was declared to be the son of God in power according to the spirit of sanctification. In which place he attributes weakness to the flesh, and power and the spirit of sanctification to the divinity. Wherefore, since John begins from the divinity, faith must be raised up, so that we may believe that the power of God is in Christ, than which no creature is superior. And let us not doubt that by the will of God sins are thus remitted. Because Christ, who is God, announces the remission of sins.

Finally, that we may believe that the saving forms are, the forms of the cross, if indeed they are of God. Nor indeed is John content to have indicated the divinity of Christ with brief words, but he describes it through many circumstances. And so that it might be understood that the son is God not by a communication of the name, as the Arians thought, but by nature. And so that the distinction of the persons might be known, and the nature or manner of this one operating upon the creatures.

Latin:

In principio erat verbum &c.

Duas creaturas in Christo sic discernit Paulus Romanos 1. De filio suo, qui natus est ex semine David secundum carnem, qui declaratus est esse filius dei in potentia secundum spiritum sanctificationis. Quo loco infirmitatem carni, potentiam & spiritum sanctificationis divinitati tribuit. Quare cum à divinitate orditur Ioannes, erigenda fides est, ut credamus dei potentiam esse in Christo, qua nulla creatura sit superior. Néve dubitemus quin voluntate dei sic remittantur peccata. Quia Christus qui deus est, annunciat remissionem peccatorum.

Postremo, ut credamus salutares formas esse, formas crucis, si quidem dei sint. Neque vero Ioannes contentus est brevibus verbis indicasse divinitatem Christi, sed per multa circumstantia describit. Et ut intelligeretur, quod filius non communicatione nominis, sicut Arriani senserunt, sed natura deus est. Et ut cognosceretur discrimen personarum, & huius natura vel ratio operandi in creaturas.

## 1:1
In the beginning was the word,

Namely, when the creatures began to be created, according to Gen. 1. In the beginning God created heaven and earth. In that beginning he was, that is, he was then, when the creatures began to be created: therefore it is necessary that he was God before every beginning of the creatures. Now since concerning this beginning it is thought, one goes into the abysses of eternity, here by faith one must enter into the darkness, which feels that God is before all things, after all things, in all things. Just as also in death where one must go out beyond all creatures, reason does not see where one is to go, what is to be. But faith stands immovable, doubting nothing but that it is entering into God. Now this is more powerful, that here Christ and that person who was made flesh, who is ours, is that God into whom one must enter.

Latin:

In principio erat verbum,

Scilicet quando condi creaturae coeperunt, iuxta Gen. 1. In principio creavit deus caelum & terram. In illo principio erat, id est tunc erat, quando creaturae condi inciperent: ergo ante omne principium creaturarum fuerit deus, necesse est. Iam cum de hoc principio cogitatur, itur in abyssos aeternitatis, hic fide in tenebras ingrediendum est, quae sentit, ante omnia, post omnia, in omnibus esse deum. Sicut & in morte ubi exeundum est extra omnes creaturas, non videt ratio quò concedendum, quid futurum sit. Sed fides stat immobilis, nihil dubitans quin ingrediatur in deum. Iam hoc potentius est, quod hic Christus & ea persona quae caro facta est, quae nostra est, is deus est in quem ingrediendum sit.

## 1:1
And the word was with God,

Namely the image conceived by the consideration of the father, which John calls the word, the Apostle calls the image. The image of the son is the very image of the father.

That he says with God, not in God, signifies that the person is distinguished, and other than the father. And in Greek the preposition in is of dissimilarity, so that he seems to say: And it was like God. But if the word is like, it is necessary that it be other than God. But if it is other, and yet is before all creatures, and before every beginning: therefore it is necessary that it be God.

Latin:

Et verbum erat apud deum,

Scilicet imago concepta consyderatione patris, quod Ioannes verbum, Apostolus imaginem vocat. Imago filii est ipsa imago patris.

Quod apud deum dicit, non in deo, significat discriminatam esse personam, & aliam à patre. Et Graecis in praepositio dissimilitudinis est, ut videatur dicere: Et erat simile dei. Quod si simile est verbum, aliud à deo sit necesse est. Quod si aliud est, & tamen ante omnes creaturas est, & ante omne principium: ergo deus sit, necesse est.

## 1:1
And the word was God.

This is as it were an epilogue of the things above. For if in the beginning was the word, if it was with God before all creatures, it is necessary that he be God. But by the word was, he suppresses Arius with one word: for Arius used to say that Christ was called God by a communication of the title, not that he had attained the glorification of the Father by nature and the title of God. Now, since John says that the Word was God from eternity, the fiction of Arius is worth nothing.

Latin:

Et deus erat verbum.

Hic velut epilogus est superiorum. Nam si in principio fuit verbum, si apud deum fuit ante omnes creaturas, necesse est ut sit deus. Quod autem erat dixit, uno verbo Arrium reprimit: nam Arrius dicebat Christum deum communicatione appellationis vocari, non natura & appellatione dei consecutum glorificationem patris. Nunc cum Ioannes dicit ab aeterno verbum fuisse deum, nihil valet Arrii commentum.

## 1:1
This was in the beginning with God.

He had finished the first syllogism concerning the divinity of the Son, and when he had placed in the conclusion, the Word was God, immediately, lest anyone should indicate that the Word is one person, or nothing distinct from the Father, he adds: This was with God. This Word, namely, which is God, was with the Father, that is, it was the Father himself: but it was with the Father, and it was the very image of the Father. And in the beginning it was with God, that is, the Word is coeternal with the Father. And he has encompassed three things in this brief sentence. First, in that which he says: This, he refers to the preceding sentence, that the Word is God, and he indicates the divinity of the Word. That which he says: In the beginning it was with God, makes the Word coeternal with the Father. That which he says, with God, notes the distinction of the persons.

Latin:

Hoc erat in principio apud deum.

Finierat syllogismum primum de divinitate filii, & cum in conclusione posuisset, deus erat verbum, statim ne quis unam personam, aut nihil à patre distinctum esse verbum indicaret, addit: Hoc erat apud deum. Hoc scilicet verbum, quod est deus, erat apud patrem, hoc est erat ipse pater: sed apud patrem erat, & ipsa patris imago. Et in principio erat apud deum, id est coaeternum verbum est pater. Atque tria hac brevi sententia complexus est. Primum in eo quod dicit: Hoc, refert proximam sententiam, verbum deum esse, & verbi divinitatem indicat. Quod dicit: In principio erat apud deum, facit patri coaeternum verbum. Quod dicit, apud deum, notat discrimen personarum.

## 1:3
All things were made through him, and without him was made nothing that was made.

And this is an argument for the divinity of the Son. For if all things were made through the Word: therefore he himself was not made. And lest anyone should cavil that all other things could have been made through the Son, so that he himself might also be made, he added an objection. Without him was made nothing that was made, that is, whatever was made was made through him. And this is indeed the opinion of John concerning the divinity. Now whoever wishes to weigh more broadly how all things were made through the Word, let him consult also other places of Scripture, Coloss. 1. In him were created all things that are in heaven, that are on earth, visible and invisible, etc. All things were created through him and in him: and he is before all things, and all things consist in him. Thus Paul: And he says that all things were created through him, and that all things consist in him. And to Heb. 1. Upholding all things by the word of his power. But these things, unless they are understood by the Spirit, will never be able to be understood. For the narrowness of reason does not grasp, but the Spirit grasps, that all things are in the hand of God, and consist, etc. God works sensibly in the hearts of the saints through the Word in all things, in the ungodly by a hidden counsel, which is also the Word of God itself. Furthermore, the series and composition of the discourse indicates that John followed the first chapter of Genesis. Because there the text openly testifies that all things were created through the Word.

Latin:

Omnia per ipsum facta sunt, & sine ipso factum est nihil, quod factum est.

Et hoc argumentum divinitatis filii est. Nam si omnia per verbum facta sunt: ergo ipse non est factus. Et ne quis calumnietur, potuisse per filium omnia alia fieri, ut maxime & ipse sit factus, subiecit occupationem. Sine ipso factum est nihil quod factum est, id est quicquid factum per ipsum factum est. Et haec est quidem Ioannis de divinitate sententia. Nunc qui volet expendere latius, ut per verbum omnia facta sint, is consulat & alios scripturae locos, Coloss. 1. In ipso condita sunt omnia, quae sunt in caelis, quae sunt super terram, visibilia & invisibilia &c. Omnia per ipsum, & in ipso condita sunt: & ipse est ante omnia, & omnia in ipso consistunt. Ita Paulus: Et per ipsum ait omnia esse condita, & in ipso omnia consistere. Et ad Heb. 1. Portans omnia verbo virtutis suae. Sed haec, nisi spiritu intelligantur, intelligi numquam poterunt. Non enim capit angustia rationis, sed spiritus capit, omnia in manu dei esse, & consistere &c. In sanctorum cordibus operatur sensibiliter deus per verbum omnia, in impiis abscondito consilio, quod & ipsum verbum dei est. Caeterum orationis series & compositio indicat Ioannem esse sequutum primum caput Genesis. Quia illic textus palàm testatur omnia per verbum esse condita.

## 1:4
In him was life.

Because through him all things were made, and in him all things consist, and all things are carried by his power. Thus also Deuteronomy 8. Man does not live by bread alone, but by every word etc. And because there is life in the word, therefore the word came into the world, that he might vivify the dead. The punishment of sin, eternal death, was established, Genesis 2. And because there is life in the word, therefore death could not be abolished except by the word. Therefore he begins here to accommodate his speech to the Gospel and human renewal. For because he is the life of all, therefore he is the light to man, and he adds: The life was the light of men, that is, that very living word was the light. Just as in us there is no life, except the life of the flesh, so in us there is no light, that is, intellect, judgment, affection, except of the flesh. 1. Corinthians 2. The animal man does not perceive the things that are of the spirit of God, therefore the flesh neither understands God, nor fears, nor loves, but is ignorant and despises. But when we are touched by that living word of God, then we are illuminated, that we may know God.

Latin:

In ipso vita erat.

Quia per ipsum facta sunt omnia, & in eo omnia consistunt, & omnia portantur virtute eius. Sic & Deuteronomii 8. Non in solo pane vivit homo, sed in omni verbo &c. Et quia in verbo vita est, ideo in mundum venit verbum, ut vivificaret mortuos. Poena peccati, mors aeterna constituta est, Genesis 2. Et quia in verbo vita est, ideo non potuit mors aboleri nisi verbo. Ideo hic incipit suam orationem accommodare ad Evangelium & renovationem humanam. Quia enim vita est omnium, ideo est lux homini, et subiicit: Vita erat lux hominum, id est illud ipsum vivum verbum erat lux. Sicut in nobis non est vita, nisi carnis vita, ita in nobis non est lux, id est intellectus, iudicium, affectus, nisi carnis. 1. Corinth. 2. Animalis homo non percipit ea quae sunt spiritus dei, ideo nec intelligit deum, nec metuit, nec diligit caro, sed ignorat & contemnit. Quando autem illo vivo verbo dei tangimur, tum illuminamur, ut cognoscamus deum.

## 1:5
And the light shines in the darkness.

In this text there is this obscurity. Whether he speaks of Christ insofar as he has already appeared in the flesh, or of the divine nature even before he appeared in the flesh. However, the sum of the sentence returns to the same thing. For the divine nature alone, just as it is life, is light. They have almost referred it to this, that Christ appearing in the flesh was not known by the world to be God: therefore this, light, that is the eternal word of God, or the son of God. It shines, that is, it is shown by preaching in preaching from the created world to this day. For for light to shine is for light to be shown and to appear, as if you were to say the sun shines, so that it may be a preaching or a ray of the eternal light, by which that light is apprehended. Thus Psalm 18 In the sun he has set his tabernacle, and he himself as a bridegroom coming out of his chamber. Likewise Psalm 25 With you is the fountain of life, and in your name we shall see light. What else is this than what John says, with you is life? To this pertain all things which are about light among the prophets, Psalm 88 And Isaiah 2 Let us walk in the light of our God, that is, illuminated through the living word by the knowledge of God.

Latin:

Et lux in tenebris lucet.

In hoc textu haec est obscuritas. Utrum de Christo quatenus iam apparuerit in carne loquatur, an de natura divina etiam priusquam in carne apparuit. Caeterum eòdem redit summa sententiae. Nam sola divina natura sicut vita lux est. Ferè eò retulerunt, quod Christus in carne apparens, non est cognitus à mundo deus esse: ergo hic, lux, id est verbum dei aeternum, seu filius dei. Lucet, id est ostenditur praedicatione in praedicatione à condito mundo in hunc diem. Nam lucem lucere, est lucem ostendi & apparere, ut si dicas solem lucere, ut sit praedicatio seu radius lucis aeternae, quibus illa lux apprehenditur. Sic Psalmo 18. In sole posuit tabernaculum suum, & ipse tanquam sponsus procedens de thalamo suo. Item Psalmo 25. Apud te est fons vitae, & in nomine tuo videbimus lumen. Quod quid aliud est, quàm quod ait Ioannes, apud te vita est? Huc pertinent omnia quae de lumine sunt apud prophetas, Psalmo 88. Et Esaiae 2. Ambulemus in lumine dei nostri, id est illuminati per vivum verbum cognitione dei.

## 1:5
And the darkness did not comprehend him.

In darkness, that is, in human reason, which is darkness. And thus it is the subject: And the darkness did not comprehend him, as if he were saying, he is never comprehended by any man as long as he remains in darkness.

Furthermore, there is nothing for us to dispute about reason and free will: because John here once calls darkness whatever is not illuminated by the light of God. For John does not call only those darkness who do not hear the word of God at all, but those who do hear. For he says, the light shines. The darkness understands neither the light, nor the rays of the light, that is, of the preaching. To this pertains the song of Zechariah: To give light to those who were in darkness, etc. And to Ephesians 5. You too were once darkness.

Latin:

Et tenebrae eum non comprehenderunt.

In tenebris, id est in ratione humana, quae tenebrae sunt. Atque ita subiectum est: Et tenebrae eum non comprehenderunt, quasi diceret, nunquam comprehendi ab ullo homine quandiu maneat in tenebris.

Nihil est porrò, quod disputemus de ratione & libero arbitrio: quia semel hic Ioannes tenebras vocat, quicquid non est illuminatum luce dei. Nam Ioannes non eos tantum tenebras vocat, qui prorsus non audiunt verbum dei, sed eos qui audiunt. Dicit enim, lux lucet. Nec lucem, nec radios lucis, id est praedicationis intelligunt tenebrae. Huc pertinet canticum Zachariae: Illuminare his qui in tenebris erant &c. Et ad Ephesios 5. Eratis & vos aliquando tenebrae.

## 1:6
There was a man sent from God, etc.

Thus far concerning the divinity of the Word, hereafter he will speak concerning the incarnation. And because Christ was about to come in forms of the cross and alien to the grasp, sense, and estimation of the flesh, about to die before he is glorified, about to mortify before he gives life. And because a completely new preaching of the remission of sins was about to go forth gratuitously. For thus Isaiah says: Lord, who will believe our report? And in the Gospel Christ: Blessed is he who will not have been scandalized in me. Therefore it was necessary for a witness to go before him, by whom the consciences of the elect might be confirmed, so that they might believe this one to be truly the Christ, who had been promised in the prophets. You see therefore that this is the office of John, to bear witness concerning Christ. And thus it had been predicted that a witness was about to come, in Malachi 3. Behold I send my messenger, and he will prepare the way before my face, and immediately the Lord whom you seek will come to his temple, and the messenger of the covenant whom you desire. Concerning the true witness of John and his baptism, more widely below. Here the Evangelist is content, and

to indicate the office of John, and to show that John is lesser than Christ, that Christ is the light, and that John is not the light. But if to be the light is nothing other than to be a teacher, certainly John also was a light. It is necessary, therefore, that to be the light is to be that which is the illuminator, the source, and the author of light. The sophists of free will deny that Christ is the light.

Latin:

Fuit homo missus à deo &c.

Hactenus de divinitate verbi, deinceps de incarnatione dicet. Et quia Christus venturus erat in formis crucis & alienis à captu, sensu, & aestimatione carnis, antea moriturus quàm glorificetur, antea mortificaturus quàm vivificet. Et quia praedicatio omnino nova remissionis peccatorum gratuite exitura erat. Sic enim Esaias ait: Domine, quis credet auditui nostro? Et in Evangelio Christus: Beatus, qui non fuerit in me scandalizatus. Ideo oportuit ei praeire testem, quo conscientiae electorum confirmarentur, ut crederent hunc vere esse Christum, qui promissus fuerat in prophetis. Vides ergo hoc esse officium Ioannis, testimonium perhibere de Christo. Atque ita praedictum erat venturum testem, Malachiae. 3. Ecce ego mitto angelum meum, & praeparabit viam ante faciem meam, & statim veniet ad templum suum dominator quem vos quaeritis, & angelus testamenti quem vos vultis. De testimonio vero Ioannis & baptismo infra latius. Hic contentus Evangelista, &

indicare Ioannis officium, & ostendere Ioannem minorem esse Christo, Christum lucem esse, Ioannem non lucem esse. Atqui si lucem esse nihil aliud est, nisi doctorem, certe et Ioannes lux fuit. Necesse est ergo, lucem esse, id èsse, quod illuminatorem, fontem, & autorem luminis. Sophistae liberi arbitrii negant Christum lucem esse.

## 1:6
He was not that light.

It is a great question why he denies that John was the light, when he says elsewhere: You are the light of the world. I answer: The Evangelist explains himself when he says afterwards: He was the true light, which illuminates every man coming &c. and above: The life was the light of men &c. For he institutes a comparison of John and Christ, and in John of all the prophets. For there has not arisen a greater than John. So that he may teach that Christ alone, as the substantial and life-giving light, is thus the justifier &c. Other prophets and John are witnesses of that light. I say therefore, as the text itself demands, that light here signifies the life-giving light, as he says: The life was the light of men: and that light which illuminates, that is, a new light, or as Paul calls it, he creates a new mind in the hearts of men, as he says: He was the true, that is, substance and nature light, which illuminates, that is, creates a new light. Thus Christ alone is light, no one among men: furthermore, no one of the prophets is light. For they are light not by nature, but by participation. As Peter says: You have been made partakers of the divine nature light. These things pertain to this, that we may know what difference there is between the living word and the dead word, between the letter and the spirit, between Christ and Moses. By the dead word men are indeed taught, however much the law teaches that God is to be loved. But meanwhile the heart of man is ignorant of God, and despises Him, even if it simulates the law to the greatest degree. And this is what the Evangelist says: He was the true light, which illuminates etc. For whatever dead doctrines there may be do not illuminate, even if they seem to teach, nor do they even teach: because the same sense, the same judgment of reason, the same ignorance of God always remains, until they are taught by the living word and the spirit. Here you see that, since Christ alone is light, and since with any new illumination we have a mere lie, there is mere ignorance of God, the fiction concerning free will. Christ is not a teacher, but the effector of salvation: because if He were only a teacher, both John and all the other prophets would be light. Now since John does not illuminate, who nevertheless teaches, we certainly have need of a new light, which is not doctrine, but the creation of a new heart. Observe therefore most accurately the comparison of John and Christ, that you may understand that a new light must be created in our hearts.

Latin:

Non erat ille lux.

Magna quaestio est, cur Ioannem neget lucem fuisse, cum aliâs dicat: Vos estis lux mundi. Respondeo: Evangelista ipse se exponit, cum postea dicit: Erat lux vera, quae illuminat omnem hominem venientem &c. & supra: Vita erat lux hominum &c. Instituit enim collationem Ioannis & Christi, & in Ioanne omnium prophetarum. Non enim surrexit maior Ioanne. Ut doceat, solum Christum, ut substantialem et vivificam lucem, ita iustificatorem esse &c. Alii prophetae & Ioannes sunt testes illius lucis. Dico itaque, sicut textus ipse postulat, lucem hic significare vivificam lucem, sicut ait: Vita erat lux hominum: & eam lucem quae illuminat, id est novum lumen, seu ut Paulus vocat, novam mentem creat in hominum cordibus, sicut ait: Erat vera, id est substantia & natura lux, quae illuminat, id est creat novum lumen. Ita solus Christus lux est, nemo hominum: praeterea nemo prophetarum lux est. Nam ii non natura, sed participatione lux sunt. Sicut Petrus ait: Consortes facti estis divini lucis naturae. Haec eò pertinent, ut sciamus quid intersit inter verbum vivum, & verbum mortuum, intra literam & spiritum, inter Christum & Moysen. Mortuo verbo docentur quidem homines, utcunque lex docet deum esse diligendum. Sed interim ignorat cor hominis deum, & contemnit, ut maxime simulet legem. Et hoc est quod ait Evangelista: Erat lux vera, quae illuminat &c. Non enim illuminant qua lescunque mortuae doctrinae, tametsi docere videantur, nec etiam docent: quia semper manet idem sensus, idem iudicium rationis, eadem ignorantia dei, donec verbo vivo & spiritu doceantur. Hic vides, quod cum Christus solus lux sit, & cum nova illuminatione habeamus merum mendacium, mera ignorantia sit dei, commentum de libero arbitrio. Non doctor, sed effector salutis est Christus: quod si doctor tantum esset, & Ioannes & omnes alii prophetae lux essent. Nunc cum Ioannes non illuminet, qui tamen docet, novo certe lumine opus habemus, quod non doctrina, sed creatio novi cordis sit. Observa itaque accuratissime Ioannis & Christi collationem, ut intelligas novum lumen in cordibus nostris crearioportere.

## 1:7
That all might believe.

We have said that the office of John is to testify concerning Christ. But the Evangelist answers why he might testify, that all might believe: although how Christ came, he is to explain later, yet the Evangelist noted that here most significantly with one word. For if Christ had come only to teach the law, there would have been no need for a witness: for Moses testifies sufficiently concerning the law, whom the people believed, as is said below: We are disciples of Moses. Thus there was no need for faith. Second: therefore Christ was about to announce something new, which it was necessary to believe, and that is the Gospel, or the remission of sin.

Third. Furthermore, that Gospel is apprehended not by reason, but by faith. Fourth: Christ was also about to come covered in such a form that the flesh would not be held for Christ. Fifth. Now this also teaches, if John testified for no other reason than that we might believe: therefore this sum of sums of justice is to believe in Christ, so that the Evangelist here intends nothing else than what Scripture says elsewhere, The just man lives by faith. Christ came so that He might justify. But how do we attain that justice? John answers, by believing. The sixth, through him, is to be referred to John, so that it may be, through him, that is, with John testifying. For the same figure of speech is below chapter 17 Those who are to believe through the word in me.

Latin:

Ut omnes crederent.

Diximus Ioannis officium esse testificari de Christo. Sed cur testificetur respondet Evangelista, ut omnes crederent: quanquam ut venerit Christus, postea expositurus est, tamen id hic uno verbo significantissime notavit Evangelista. Nam si venisset Christus legem tantum docturus, nihil opus fuisset teste: Nam satis de lege Moyses testatur, cui credebat populus, ut infra dicitur: Nos discipuli Moysi sumus. Ita nec fide fuit opus. Secundum: ergo erat Christus novum quiddam nunciaturus, quod credi oportuit, id autem Evangelium est, seu remissio peccati.

Tertium. Porrò id Evangelium non ratione, sed fide apprehenditur. Quartum: erat & venturus Christus tali tectus forma, ut caro pro Christo non haberetur. Quintum. Iam hoc quoque docet, si non ob aliud testificatus Ioannes, nisi ut credamus: ergo haec summa summarum est iustitiae, credere in Christum, ut nihil aliud hic Evangelista velit, quàm quod aliâs scriptura, Iustus ex fide vivit. Venit Christus, ut iustificet. At qui consequimur illam iustitiam? Respondet Ioannes, credendo. Sextum, per ipsum, referendum est ad Ioannem, ut sit, per ipsum, id est Ioanne testificante. Nam eadem loquendi figura infra est capite 17. Qui credituri sunt per verbum in me.

## 1:9
Every man.

Firstly, it is clear that He does not illuminate everyone; what, therefore, is the meaning of this passage? Some have escaped in this way, so as to understand that just as the sun rises over everyone and pours out its rays, although the blind do not see, so the Gospel rises even over the impious, although they do not grasp it: according to that: Hear by hearing, and do not understand. But in this way you would make Christ a teacher, which is repugnant to the order of the sentence in this place. For here He certainly accepts illumination as the condition of a new light. Secondly, it is clear that to illuminate here ought not to be accepted as to teach, as the Sophists understood: for otherwise John and all the prophets would also have been a light. Thirdly, that the Evangelist did not think that all are illuminated appears from the fact that he says: And the darkness did not comprehend it. Item: The world did not know Him. Item: His own did not receive Him: they would certainly have received Him, if they had been illuminated: according to that 1, Corinth. 2. If they had known, they would by no means have crucified the Lord of glory. Fourthly, let us simply say that which Augustine thinks, and the order of this text proves, that the Evangelist wished Christ alone to be the light, who alone illuminates as many as are illuminated.

As if you were to say, in a single city where someone is teaching, this person teaches everyone in this city, that is, as many as are taught in this city, they are taught by his work. As Romans 5 Paul, when he wishes to teach that Christ alone justifies, says: As the righteousness of one unto all. That is, no one is justified except through the one Christ. And below: Of his fullness we have all received. So here John, in the comparison of the prophets with Christ (for they too could appear to be light), says: He was not the light, but Christ is the light, who illuminates both John and everyone. Thus, unless he himself illuminates, no one is illuminated. Briefly, if the Sophists cite this passage to me in favor of free will, I shall attack them with no stronger argument than this very beginning of this Gospel. And you will learn most especially from this that Christ is the light which illuminates, that is, creates a new light in the hearts of the elect, without which new light, the light of nature is mere darkness, mere ignorance. And as the light of Christ is life, so the light of nature is clearly death. For those who believe in Christ will not taste death, because they are illuminated, and therefore also vivified, and they will pass through death without any sense of death.

Latin:

Omnem hominem.

Primo, Non omnes illuminare palàm est, quae ergo huius loci sententia est? Quidam elapsi sunt hoc modo, ut intelligerent, quod sicut sol super omnes exoritur, & effundit radios suos, quanquam caeci non videant, ita Evangelium exoriatur etiam supra impios, quanquam non capiant: Iuxta illud: Audite audientes, & nolite intelligere. Sed ita ex Christo doctorem feceris, quod repugnat ordini sententiae hoc loco. Nam hic certe illuminationem pro novi luminis conditione accipit. Secundo, Illuminare hic non debere accipi pro docere, sicut Sophistae intellexerunt, palàm est: nam alioquin etiam Ioannes & omnes prophètae lux fuerint. Tertio, Non sensisse Evangelistam, quod omnes illuminentur, ex eo apparet quod ait: Et tenebrae non comprehenderunt. Item: Mundus non cognovit eum. Item, Sui non receperunt eum: certe recepturi, si fuissent illuminati: Iuxta illud 1, Corinth. 2. Si cognovissent haud quaquam dominum gloriae crucifixissent. Quarto, Simpliciter dicamus id quod sentit Augustinus, & huius textus ordo evincit, voluisse Evangelistam solum Christum esse lucem, qui solus illuminat quotquot illuminantur.

Ut si dicas, in unica urbe aliquo docente, hic omnes docet in hac urbe, id est quotquot in hac urbe docentur, huius opera docentur. Ut Romanos 5. Paulus, ubi docere vult, quod solus Christus iustificet, dicit: Sicut unius iustitia in omnes. id est nemo iustificatur, nisi per unum Christum. Et infra: De plenitudine eius accepimus omnes. Ita hic Ioannes in collatione prophetarum cum Christo (nam & illi lux videri poterant) ait: Non erat ille lux, sed Christus lux est, qui cum Ioannem tum omnes illuminat. Ita nisi & ipse illuminet, nemo illuminetur. Breviter, si mihi pro libero arbitrio citent hunc locum Sophistae, nullo fortiore eos oppugnaturus sum, quàm hoc ipso huius Evangelii principio. Atque potissimum hinc disces Christum lucem esse, quae illuminet, id est novum lumen creet in cordibus electorum, sine quo novo lumine, merae tenebrae, mera ignorantia lumen naturae est. Et ut Christi lumen vita est, ita lumen naturae planè mors est. Nam qui credunt in Christum, mortem non gustabunt, quia illuminati, ideoque etiam vivificati, & pertransibunt mortem sine ullo mortis sensu.

## 1:9
Coming into this world.

Ἐρχόμενον in the Greek, that participle can be referred either to the light or to the man. Cyril therefore referred it ad hominem, because concerning the light it follows afterwards: He was in the world, etc. and that very reason moved me, so that I might be able to refer it to the light. As if he were saying, he creates a new light, because he came into the world, that is because that light was made flesh. But because I see that the other reading has pleased everyone, let us follow it also. So that the sum may be, that Christ the light illuminates by faith in this life, afterwards he delivers the kingdom to the father, where we shall know the father face to face. And through that very light the father is known, which Christ created in us. Understand it concerning the incarnate word, and what he said before, coming into the world, and afterwards, he came into his own. For certainly to come is to appear. And now he says significantly, He was in the world: which does not seem to be aptly said concerning the incarnate word, if you look at the phrase John chapter 9. As long as I am in this world, I am the light of the world. And 13. He might pass from this world. Therefore after he had spoken above concerning the divinity of the word, and his power, namely that he is both life, and light, that is a vivifying light: now he begins, I do not say to speak only of the flesh, but of the word incarnate and humiliated. He treats that again at length, and through circumstances. And first indeed here you will observe an excellent antithesis. The words are simple, but the things are greatest.

Latin:

Venientem in hunc mundum.

Ἐρχόμενον in Graecis participium illud referri potest vel ad lumen, vel ad hominem. Cyrillus ideo ad hominem retulit, quia de luce postea sequitur: In mundo erat, &c. atque me ea ipsa ratio moverit, ut ad lumen referre possim. Quasi diceret, novum lumen creat, quia in mundum venit, id est quia illa lux caro facta est. Sed quia video omnibus placuisse alteram lectionem, sequamur eam & nos. Ut sit summa, lucem Christum illuminare fide in hac vita, postea tradere regnum patri, ubi coràm cognoscemus patrem. Atque per illud ipsum lumen cognoscitur pater, quod in nobis Christus creavit. De verbo incarnato intellige, & quod ante dixerit, veniens in mundum, et postea, in propria venit. Certe enim venire, apparere est. Et nunc dicit significanter, In mundo erat: quod apte de verbo incarnato dici non videtur, si spectes phrasim Ioannis capite 9. Quandiu sum in hoc mundo, lux sum mundi. Et 13. Transeat ex hoc mundo. Proinde posteaquam supra de divinitate verbi, & potentia eius dixerat, scilicet quod & vita, & lumen sit, id est vivificum lumen: nunc incipit, non dico de carne tantum, sed verbo incarnato & humiliato loqui. Id iterum prolixe, & per circumstantias tractat. Et primum quidem hic observabis egregiam antithesim. Verba simplicia, sed res maximae sunt.

## 1:9
He was in the world.

Thus far concerning the power of the word, now concerning his entry into the world he speaks. To come into the world is to be humbled, etc. To be in the world is that which is said in Philip. 2.: Being made in the likeness of man, and found in fashion as a man. And Hebr. 4.: Tempted in all things by likeness, etc. This humiliation of the Son commends to us the greatness of mercy, and brings it about that we may approach the throne of grace with confidence, because we know that we have a high priest who can sympathize with our infirmities. Hebr 4. And immediately, lest he should seem to have detracted anything from the divinity, he adds, The world was made by him. And thus he makes an antithesis of the humbled Word, and also of his power, so that he may signify at the same time that Christ is to reign in the power of God.

Latin:

In mundo erat.

Hactenus de verbi potentia, nunc de ingressu eius in mundum loquitur. Venire in mundum, est humiliari &c. In mundo esse, est id quod Philip. 2. dicitur: In similitudine hominis factus, & habitu inventus ut homo. Et Hebr. 4. Tentatum per omnia pro similitudine &c. Haec humiliatio filii, magnitudinem misericordiae nobis commendat, & efficit, ut cum fiducia thronum gratiae accedamus, quod scimus nobis esse pontificem, qui possit nobis compati nostris infirmitatibus. Hebr 4. Et statim, ne quid videretur divinitati derogasse, subiicit, Mundus per ipsum factus est. Atque ita facit anithesim humiliati verbi, & etiam potentiae eius, ut simul significet Christum regnaturum in potentia dei.

## 1:10
And the world knew him not.

It is the same as what he said above: And the darkness did not comprehend him. The world is darkness, and it savors and understands nothing but carnal things. Wherefore it recognizes neither the righteousness of the spirit, which is faith, nor Christ appearing in the forms of the cross. Therefore it is said in Matthaei 16.: Blessed art thou, Simon, because flesh and blood hath not revealed it to thee.

Latin:

Et mundus eum non cognovit.

Idem est quod supra ait: Et tenebrae cum non comprehenderunt. Mundus tenebrae est, & nihil nisi carnalia sapit & intelligit. Quare neque iustitiam spiritus quae fides est, neque Christum apparentem in formis crucis agnoscit. Ideo dicitur Matthaei 16. Beatus es Simon, quia caro & sanguis tibi non revelavit.

## 1:11
He came unto his own.

That is, to the Jews, to whom he was promised. Thus Paul calls Christ a minister of the circumcision, etc. And here again see how John looks back into the histories of the Old Testament, and the promises concerning Christ, through which he wishes his Gospel to be expounded and confirmed.

Latin:

In propria venit.

Id est, ad Iudaeos, quibus erat promissus. Sic Paulus Christum vocat ministrum circumcisionis &c. Atque hic iterum vide quomodo retrospiciat Ioannes in historias veteris testamenti, & promissiones de Christo, per quas Evangelium suum vult exponi & confirmari.

## 1:11
And his own did not receive him.

Although he says nothing other than what he said before: The darkness did not comprehend him. Item, The world did not know him. Yet this is more terrible, that he says, his own. Just as he says Romans 10. from Isaiah 65. I was found by those not seeking me &c. All day long I have stretched out my hands to a disbelieving people. Where Isaiah explains the cause, what it is for his own not to have received him, namely nothing other than to have been unwilling to believe, and to have wished to be saved through works. As Paul says in the same place. Israel, pursuing the law of righteousness, did not arrive at the law of righteousness. And Christ: Harlots and publicans shall go before you &c. Thus you see John urging this, so that he may teach that one must believe in Christ with no regard for works. And these things concerning that he has entered into the world.

Latin:

Et sui eum non receperunt.

Quanquam non aliud dicit, quàm quod antea: Tenebrae eum non comprehenderunt. Item, Mundus eum non cognovit. Tamen hoc terribilius est, quod dicit, sui. Sicut dicit Romanos 10. ex Esaiae 65. Inventus sum à non quaerentibus me &c. Tota die expandi manus meas ad populum non credentem. Ubi Esaias causam explicat, quid sit suos non recepisse, scilicet non aliud nisi noluisse credere, & per opera voluisse salvari. Ut ibidem Paulus dicit. Israël sectando legem iustitiae, ad legem iustitiae non pervenit. Et Christus: Meretrices & publicani praecedent vos &c. Ita vides hoc urgere Ioannem, ut doceat nullo operum respectu credendum esse in Christum. Et haec de eo quod sit ingressus in mundum.

## 1:12
But as many as received him.

Here he explains at once the sum of the whole Gospel, expounding why Christ came into the world, namely that he might give authority to become children of God. Now he also adds how they become children of God, namely if they receive Christ, that is if they believe in Christ. In the appellation many things are comprehended. First, just as children are born in the flesh, they do not merit to be children by any work of their own: So here they do not become children by any merit of their own. As will be explained more broadly a little later. Galatians 4. it is written concerning the heir. Second, to be children is to be in the grace of the father. Therefore it is opposed to children of wrath. And every right of children contains the inheritance of Christ, the priesthood & the kingdom. Just as Matthew 17. the children are free. And this is the inheritance of which it is said at Romans 4. That he might be heir of the world. Third, to be children is more than to be a people. Just as also Paul at Romans 8. distinguishes the spirit of the adoption of children from the spirit of bondage. In the Old Testament he did not call them children, but a people, and therefore it was a people and not a child: because it served in the righteousnesses of the flesh, and its own works. In the New Testament they are children, because they were received into grace freely without any exaction of works, so that John appears to have spoken in antithesis: His own received him not. And afterwards: He gave them power to become the children of God. That is, Christ did not come to make a people, to impose a law, but to make children. Just as at Hosea first it is said: It shall be in the place where it shall be said to them, Ye are not my people, it shall be said to them, Ye are the children of the living God. Likewise because his own are not children, he openly signifies a people of works not to be sons of God.

Latin:

Quotquot autem receperunt.

Hic explicat semel totius Evangelii summam, exponens cur Christus in mundum venerit, scilicet ut autoritatem daret fieri filios dei. Iam & adiicit quomodo fiant filii dei, nempe si recipiant Christum, id est si credant in Christum. In appellatione multa comprehenduntur. Primum sicut filii in carne nascuntur, non merentur filii esse suo aliquo opere: Ita hic filii non fiunt aliquo suo merito. Ut paulo post latius exponetur. Ad Gala. 4. de haerede scriptum est. Se cundo, filios esse, est in gratia patris esse. Proin de filiis irae opponitur. Et omne ius filiorum continet haereditatem Christi, sacerdotium & regnum. Sicut Matthaei 17. Liberi sunt filii. Et haec est haereditas de qua ad Romanos 4. Ut esset haeres mundi. Tertio, Filios esse, plus est, quàm populum esse. Sicut & Paulus ad Romanos 8. à spiritu filiorum adoptionis discernit spiritum servitutis. Veteri testamento non filios, sed populum appellabat, & ideo populus erat & non filius: quia serviebat in iustitiis carnis, & operibus suis. Novo testamento filii sunt, quia gratis sine ulla operum exactione recepti sunt in gratiam, ut videatur Ioannes in antithesi locutus: Sui eum non receperunt. Et postea: Dedit eis potestatem filios dei fieri. Id est, Non venit Christus, ut populum faceret, ut legem imponeret, sed ut filios faceret. Sicut Osee primo dicitur: Erit in loco ubi dicetur eis. Non populus meus vos, dicetur eis, filii dei viventis. Item quia sui non sunt filii, aperte significat populum operum non esse filios dei.

## 1:12
Who believe in his name.

How they become sons of God, he himself explains, when he adds: To those who believe in his name. Where he teaches that we do not become sons by human merit, nor by any work of ours, but by faith alone. Nor does he simply say, who believes in him, but in his name, that is, who believe that Christ is truly the justifier, truly the savior.

Latin:

Qui credunt in nomine eius.

Quomodo fiant filii dei, ipse exponit, cum adiicit: His qui credunt in nomine eius. Ubi docet, non humano merito, non opere aliquo nostro filios fieri, sed sola fide. Neque simpliciter dicit, qui credit in eum, sed in nomine eius, hoc est qui credunt Christum vere iustificatorem, vere salvatorem esse.

## 1:13
Who not of bloods.

Nor is the calling of sons vain, nor is faith ineffectual, therefore it follows: Who not of bloods. For he teaches that, because they are sons who have believed, it is necessary also to be born: and because they are sons of God, therefore it is necessary that regeneration take place. Therefore, you will observe here most importantly that through faith they are reborn as sons. When I say reborn, I signify first to mortify the flesh with its light, and then to renew the new man by faith, that is, by the knowledge of the glory of God, of the justice, and of the mercy of God given in Christ. Thus, as often as scripture speaks of being reborn, the old birth is condemned with all its powers and works.

He calls the natural propagation of the flesh blood. And when he says that the sons of God are not born of bloods, he condemns the old birth in its entirety, and consequently also all the powers of nature, which are transfused into offspring by natural propagation. Therefore, the light of nature, free will, are nothing but impious terms.

Latin:

Qui non ex sanguinibus.

Neque vana appellatio filiorum est, nec fides inefficax est, ideo sequitur: Qui non ex sanguinibus. Docet enim, quia filii sunt qui crediderunt, oportere & nasci: & quia filii dei sunt, ideo necesse est fieri regenerationem. Potissimum itaque hic observabis, per fidem renasci filios. Cum dico renasci, significo prius mortificare carnem cum suo lumine, & deinde novum hominem renovare fide, id est cognitione gloriae dei, iustitiae, & misoricordiae dei donatae in Christo. Sic quoties de renascendo loquitur scriptura, damnatur vetus nativitas cum omnibus viribus & operibus suis.

Sanguinem vocat propagationem naturalem carnis. Et cum dicit, non ex sanguinibus nasci filios dei, damnat in totum veterem nativitatem, adeoque & omnes vires naturae, quae naturali propagatione transfunduntur in sobolem. Proinde lumen naturae, liberum arbitrium, nihil nisi impia vocabula sunt.

## 1:13
Nor of the will of the flesh.

But what he said, not of bloods, condemns nature generally, and teaches that we are all born children of wrath. Now he adds: Not of the will of the flesh: Which although it pertains to this, that we are not born children of God carnally, yet it seems to me to pertain properly to those who were born from fathers to whom the promise was made, as if he were saying thus: First, I deny that they are children, although born according to nature. Then, I also deny that these are children who, although they were born from fathers whose promise it is, yet were born by the will of the flesh. Finally, he adds: Nor of the will of man: That is, not of the will, or choice of man. This third generation is arbitrary, by which proselytes betook themselves to the law of Moses, and to the people of Israel, by which Jews or Turks today betake themselves to the faithful people: they, reborn by human will, not by the spirit, commonly using the external observation of the law with the faithful people, are not children of God. You see therefore that nature, and all its pursuits, all its counsels are condemned. First, they are not children of God who in any way are born according to nature. Second, not even those indeed who are born from the fathers. Thirdly, neither those who learn, hear, and observe the same law and the same Gospel in external habit. He said all these things in one word Psalm 15 I will not gather their conventicles of blood. And Romans 9 Not all who are the seed of Abraham are children.

Latin:

Neque ex voluntate carnis.

Quod autem dixit, non ex sanguinibus, generaliter damnat naturam, & docet nos omnes nasci filios irae. Nunc adiicit: Non ex voluntate carnis: Quod quamquam eò pertineat, non nasci filios dei carnaliter, tamen proprie mihi pertinere videtur ad eos, qui ex patribus, quibus promissio facta est, nati sunt, quasi sic dicat: Primum nego filios esse, quanquam natos secundum naturam. Deinde hos etiam nego filios esse, qui quamquam è patribus, quorum promissio est, nati sunt, tamen voluntate carnis nati sunt. Postremo addit: Nec ex voluntate viri: Id est non ex voluntate, seu arbitrio hominis. Haec tertia generatio arbitraria est, qua proselyti se ad legem Moysi, & ad populum Israël conferebant, qua se ad populum fidelem conferunt hodie Iudaei, vel Turci: ii voluntate humana, non spiritu renati, externa observatione legis communiter utentes cum populo fideli, non sunt filii dei. Vides ergo naturam, & omnia studia, omnia consilia eius damnari. Primum filii dei non sunt, qui quocunque modo secundum naturam nascuntur. Secundo, ne illi quidem qui è patribus nati sunt. Tertio, nec illi qui eandem legem, idem Evangelium discunt, audiunt, observant externo habitu. Haec omnia uno verbo dixit Psalm. 15. Non congregabo conventicula eorum de sanguinibus. Et Romanos 9. Non qui semen sunt Abrahae, omnes filii sunt.

## 1:13
But are born of God.

Already before it was spoken about being born, here he adds of God: because the renewal of the creature is the work of God alone. Just as therefore he said before: In him was life. Likewise: He was the true light, which illuminates every man. So now he says: They are born of God. Now this is even more significant, that he says of God, not from God. For to be born of God is to become a participant of his nature, that is, to be transformed into the same image, beholding the glory of the Lord. 2 Corinthians 3 To become participants of the light, wisdom, truth, justice of God, etc. These things must be understood by the spirit, lest anyone judge such regeneration to be human thoughts. For he who has only human thoughts, he is a son, born of the will of man, not of God.

Latin:

Sed ex deo nati sunt.

Iam ante de nascendo dictum est, hic addit ex deo: quia solius dei opus est renovatio creaturae. Sicut ergo antea dixit: In ipso erat vita. Item: Erat lux vera, quae illuminat omnem hominem. Ita nunc ait: Ex deo nati sunt. Iam hoc etiam significantius est, quod ait ex deo, non à deo. Nam ex deo nasci, est participem fieri naturae eius, hoc est in eandem imaginem transformari speculantes gloriam domini. 2. Corinth. 3. Participes fieri lucis, sapientiae, veritatis, iustitiae dei &c. Haec necesse est spiritu intelligi, ne quis iudicet talem regenerationem esse humanas cogitationes. Nam qui pro cogitationibus humanis tantum habet, is filius est, ex voluntate viri, non ex deo natus.

## 1:14
And the Word was made flesh.

You may wonder why, when he had spoken before about the entry of the Word into the world, he did not also speak earlier about the incarnation he may have spoken. I answer, although he spoke concerning the entry into the world, nevertheless all things which have been said concerning him ought to have been referred to the power of the Word, not to the infirmity of the flesh of Christ: because the flesh of Christ does not illuminate, does not vivify, does not renew, etc. Therefore now at last he speaks concerning the incarnation, but this also is subjected in an apt order. Because just as in Christ the Word was made flesh, so in us it is necessary for it to be made flesh: that finally is to be reborn. As far as the word is concerned, it is only human thought, not of the sons of God. Furthermore, omitting philosophical disputes concerning the incarnation, I thus briefly feel that John takes ταπεινώσει, to be born, as when he says: The Word was made flesh, that is what Isaiah says, the lowest of men. And Paul 2. Corinth. 5. He was made sin for us. And Galat. 3. Made a curse for us. Wherefore when he calls it flesh, understand the extreme humiliation of Christ, in the extreme humiliation the remission of sin, or satisfaction, and thus the most effusive charity of the Father, who delivered up the Son for us. Romans 8. In Christ are seen the treasures of wisdom, the wrath of God against sin, the ineffable mercy of the Lord in giving the Son, so that he justifies with no respect to our works, etc. This knowledge is the righteousness of Christ, not food, or drink.

Latin:

Et verbum caro factum est.

Mirari queas, cur cum de ingressu verbi in mundum antea dixerit, non prius etiam de incarnatione locutus sit. Respondeo, quanquam de ingressu in mundum locutus est, tamen omnia quae de eo dicta sunt, ad verbi potentiam, non ad infirmitatem carnis Christi referri debuerunt: quia caro Christi non illuminat, non vivificat, non renovat &c. Ideo nunc tandem de incarnatione loquitur, sed & hoc apto ordine subiectum est. Quia sicut in Christo verbum caro factum est, ita in nobis carnem fieri oportet: Id demum est renasci. Quoad verbum cogitatio humana tantum est, non filii dei. Porrò omissis philosophicis disputationibus de incarnatione, ita breviter sentio Ioannem ταπεινώσει capere, nasci, uti cum ait: Verbum caro factum est, id est quod Esaias ait, novissimus hominum. Et Paulus 2. Corinth. 5. Pro nobis peccatum factus est. Et Galat. 3. Factus pro nobis maledictum. Quare cum carnem vocat, intellige extremam humiliationem Christi, in extrema humiliatione remissionem peccati, velsatis factionem adeoque effusissimam charitatem patris, qui pro nobis filium tradidit. Romanos 8. Videntur in Christo thesauri sapientiae, ira dei adversus peccatum, ineffabilis misericordia donintis filium, adeo ut nullo respectu operum nostrorum iustificet &c. Haec cognitio iustitia Christi est, non esca, vel potus.

## 1:14
And he dwelt among us.

To be made flesh is to be made a curse and sin, so that the curse might be taken away in our flesh. Thus what he says: He dwelt among us: signifies here what is said in Philip. 2.: Being made in the likeness of men, and found in habit as a man. Ad Hebraeos 4. We have not a high priest, who cannot have compassion on our infirmities, tempted in all things for the likeness without sin. Likewise, because he himself also suffered, and that in such a way that he is called by the prophet the last of men, he rejoices to suffer with us. Likewise, because he himself suffered, the forms of the cross cannot but be salutary: namely those which he himself sanctified.

Latin:

Et habitavit in nobis.

Carnem fieri, est maledictum & peccatum fieri, ut tolleretur maledictio in carne nostra. Ita quod ait: Habitavit in nobis: Significat hic, quod Philip. 2. dicitur: In similitudinem hominum factus, habitu inventus ut homo. Ad Hebraeos 4. Non habemus pontificem, qui non possit compati infirmitatibus nostris, tentatum per omnia pro similitudine absque peccato. Item, quia & ipse passus est, idque ita ut novissimus hominum à prompheta vocetur, gaudet compati nobiscum. Item, quod ipse passus est, non possunt non esse salutares formae crucis: nempe quas ipse sanctificavit.

## 1:14
And we saw his glory.

John makes an antithesis: for before he spoke of the humiliation of Christ, here he speaks again of his glorification. The glory beyond all the miracles of Christ is his resurrection from death. Ephesios 1. Raising him from the dead, and setting him at his right hand. Nor is any other miracle, or work of Christ, salutary to us except this, as Matthaei 16. There shall not be given to them a sign but the sign of Jonas the prophet. And Romanos 1. Item 10. If thou shalt believe in thy heart that God hath raised him up from the dead, thou shalt be saved. But what is it to believe that he is raised from the dead? I answer, it is to believe Christ to reign, to have conquered death, and to have broken the gates of hell, to have taken away sin, to protect us, to be about to judge the unbelievers, etc. This is the glory of the resurrection, by which Christ glorifies all who believe in him, etc. We have seen not only with the eyes of the flesh, but believing by faith, and perceiving, that sin and death have been destroyed.

Latin:

Et vidimus gloriam eius.

Antithesim facit Ioannes: nam ante de humiliatione Christi, hic rursum de glorificatione loquitur. Gloria praeter omnia miracula Christi, est resurrectio eius à morte. Ephesios 1. Suscitans illum à mortuis, & constituens illum ad dexteram suam. Neque aliud miraculum, aut opus Christi salutare nobis est praeter hoc, ut Matthaei 16. Non dabitur eis signum nisi signum Ionae prophetae. Et Romanos 1. Item 10. Si in corde tuo credideris, quod deus suscitavit illum à mortuis, salvus eris. Quid est autem credere resuscitatum à mortuis? Respondeo, est Christum credere regnare, vicisse mortem, & fregisse portas inferi, sustulisse peccatum, tueri nos, iudicaturum incredulos &c. Haec gloria resurrectionis est, qua glorificat Christus omnes credentes sibi &c. Vidimus non oculis tantum carnis, sed fide credentes, & sentientes, peccatum & mortem deletam esse.

## 1:14
The Only-Begotten.

He calls him the Only-Begotten, so that he may signify that he is the son of God by nature. For otherwise all the saints are sons of God, so that he could not be called the only-begotten, unless he were the son of God by nature and God.

Latin:

Unigeniti.

Unigenitum vocat, ut significet natura filium dei esse. Nam alioquin omnes sancti filii dei sunt, ut non possit unigenitus dici, nisi natura filius dei esset & deus.

## 1:14
Full of grace and truth.

Here he explains what he called glory, namely grace and truth. Grace is that by which all things are acceptable to God in Christ. Truth is that by which all things are true, good, and holy, which he works. And just as John calls the glory of Christ the fullness of grace and truth, so Isaiah calls the kingdom of Christ: A throne shall be prepared in mercy, and he shall sit upon it in truth. Likewise, the kingdom of Christ is a kingdom of mercy, or of grace and truth. Of grace, because he reconciles the Father to us. Of truth, because he makes believers truthful and holy. Furthermore, if indeed the kingdom of Christ is only a kingdom of grace and truth: therefore outside of Christ all things are sins and lies.

Latin:

Plenus gratia & veritate.

Hic exponit quid vocaverit gloriam, nempe gratiam, & veritatem. Gratia est, qua omnia Christodeo accepta sunt. Veritas est, qua omnia vera, bonae & sancta sunt, quae operatur. Et sicut Ioannes gloriam Christi plenitudinem gratiae & veritatis vocat, ita Esaias regnum Christi: Praeparabitur in misericordia solium, & sedebit super ipsum in veritate. Item Christi regnum, est regnum misericordiae, seu gratiae & veritatis. Gratiae, quia nobis reconciliat patrem. Veritatis, quia veraces & sanctos facit credentes. Porrò siquidem Christi regnum tantum gratiae & veritatis regnum est: ergo extra Christum omnia sunt peccata & mendacia.

## 1:14
And of his fullness.

Because in him dwells the fullness of the Godhead bodily. For all things are subject to Christ, because he himself is God.

Latin:

Et de plenitudine eius.

Quia in ea habitat plenitudo divinitatis corporaliter. Omnia enim Christo subiecta sunt, quia deus ipse est.

## 1:15
John testifies concerning him.

Above he distinguished Christ from John, and consequently from all the prophets, so that he might indicate that Christ is not merely a teacher, as the prophets are, but the effector of salvation. This passage is a notable argument against those who preach human powers. Here he returns to John, and continues the history: for because Christ appeared in a viler form, namely that of the cross, than reason would wish, and because the preaching of the remission of sins gratuitously resists every sense of the flesh: Therefore it was necessary for a witness to be sent before Christ, and for his testimony to be cast about by the Evangelists.

Latin:

Ioannes testatur de ipso.

Supra discrevit Christum à Ioanne, adeoque ab omnibus prophetis, ut indicaret Christum non doctorem tantum esse, ut sunt prophetae, sed effectorem salutis. Qui locus insigne argumentum est adversus eos, qui vires humanas praedicant. Hic ad Ioannem redit, & prosequitur historiam: quia enim Christus apparuit viliore forma, crucis scilicet, quàm velletratio, & quia praedicatio remissionis peccatorum gratuite, refragatur omni sensui carnis: Ideo oportuit praemitti testem Christo, & ab Evangelistis iactari illius testimonium.

## 1:15
This was he.

Whom I indicate, of what sort does he seem to you? that is, the flesh indicates that this one cannot be Christ.

Latin:

Hic erat.

Quem indico, qualis qualis vobis videtur? id est caro indicat hunc non posse esse Christum.

## 1:15
Of whom I was speaking.

That is, before I prophesied obscurely about him.

Who when he followed me.

That is, not only a preacher, but a savior, because he is God, whose face he precedes in the last times, the angel sent. As in Malachi 3.

Latin:

De quo dicebam.

Id est, ante sum obscure vaticinatus de eo.

Qui cum me sequeretur.

Id est non modo praedicator, sed salvator, quia deus est, cuius faciem praecedit novissimis temporibus angelus missus. ut Malachiae 3.

## 1:15
He went before me.

That is, he is above me, or he is greater than me. For he aims everywhere at this, to teach that Christ is not only a teacher, but light and life.

Latin:

Antecessit me.

Id est super me, vel maior me est. Nam eò ubique tendit, ut doceat, Christum non esse doctorem tantum, sed lumen & vitam.

## 1:15
Because he was before me.

That is, he was sent, or ordained, to be sent before I was sent. That is, this is he whom you expect, who was promised to you in the law and the prophets. I am only promised in the place of his minister and witness. For he himself is that principal one whom you expect, and who was sent before I began to preach.

Latin:

Quia prior me erat.

Id est, missus est, vel ordinatus, qui mitteretur priusquam ego mitterer. Id est hic est quem expectatis, qui vobis in lege & prophetis promissus est. Ego nis per ei ministri & testis vice promissus sum. Etenim ipse principalis ille est quem expectatis, & qui missus est priusquam ego inciperem praedicare.

## 1:16
We have all received.

That is, the Holy Spirit, and the grace by which the Father is appeased toward us, for the grace by which the Father is appeased toward him. Because Christ is set as a propitiatory. ad Roman. 3.

No one can conceive in words this magnificent of the Gospel, or the description of Christ, when he says: Of his fullness we have all received. For first, because he attributes fullness to Christ, he attributes divinity. And because he says that we receive from that, he signifies the power of God, which is the Holy Spirit. Then this is more, that he says we have obtained grace for grace, grace, that is, the remission of sins, for the grace by which the Father loves Christ. As Ephesians 1 he says: He has graced us in his beloved Son. Therefore, by gracing the Son openly, the Father testified that he loves the Son. Thus let us not doubt at all that we are loved as he himself is. Secondly, but again he describes Christ most amply, where he compares him with Moses. The law was given through Moses, grace and truth came through Jesus Christ. He opposes grace and truth to the law. For the law effects two things without Christ. First, when reason hears it, it simulates and feigns the work of the external law, while meanwhile the heart is ignorant of God, and alien to charity, chastity, etc. And thus John opposes truth to the lie, as if to the hypocrisy and simulation of the law. Secondly, the law confuses and terrifies consciences, and does not console: Romans 4 The law works wrath. And John opposes this wrath to grace. But see with how much difference human reason and the Holy Spirit judge concerning the law. Human reason attributes to the law the power of justifying, Scripture the power of sin. calling. Furthermore, if the law works nothing but falsehood and wrath, then it is plain that free will is nothing, and that impossible things are commanded by the law, and that all our righteousnesses are sins and falsehoods, according to that of the Psalm: Every man is a liar. Likewise, all vanity is every man. Thirdly, what then does Christ effect? Grace and truth. Grace, because through him we have obtained the remission of sins, and in him we are loved. As it was said before, grace for grace. As the son is loved, so are we also loved, who are in the son through faith. Grace is therefore the favor of God, by which he loves and accepts us and all our things, even though they are sins, no differently than he loves the son. Truth is the truthful righteousness of the heart, by which he sanctifies our hearts through his spirit, and makes them truthful, founding a new life and a new light. From these, gather the distinction between law and Gospel, Moses and Christ.

Latin:

Accepimus omnes.

Id est spiritunsanctum, & gratiam qua nobis placatus est pater, pro gratia qua ei placatus est pater. quia Christus positus propitiatorium. ad Roman. 3.

Nemo verbis concipi queat magnificam hanc Evangelii, seu Christi descriptionem, cum inquit: Ex plenitudine eius accepimus nos omnes. Primum enim, quod Christo plenitudinem tribuit, divinitatem tribuit. Et quod ex illa nos accipere dicit, significat potentiam dei, esse spiritum sanctum. Deinde hoc amplius est quod ait, Gratiam pro gratia nos consecutos esse, gratiam, id est remissionem peccatorum, pro gratia qua pater Christum amat. Sicut Ephesios 1. ait: Gratificatuit nos in dilecto filio suo. Ergo gratificato palàm filio, testatus est pater diligere se filium. Ita nos nihil dubitemus quin diligamur ut ipse. Secundo, sed iterum amplissime describit Christum, ubi cum Moy se confert. Lex per Moysen data est, gratia & veritas per Iesum Christum facta est. Legi opponit gratiam & veritatem. Duo enim lex efficit sine Christo. Primo quando audit eam ratio, simulat & mentitur opus legis externae, cum interim corsit ignorans dei, & alienum à charitate, castitate, &c. Atque ita mendacium ceu hypocrisim, & simulationem legis opponit Ioannes veritati. Secundo, lex confundit & pavefacit conscientias, nec consolatur: Romanos 4. Lex iram operatur. Et hanc iram Ioannes opponit gratiae. Vide autem quanto discrimine de lege iudicent ratio humana & spiritussanctus. Ratio humana legi iustificandi vim tribuit, scriptura virtutem peccati vocat. Porrò si lex non nisi mendacium & iram operatur: iam palàm est nihil esse liberum arbitrium, & impossibilia à lege praecipi, & omnes iustitias nostras esse peccata & mendacia, iuxta illud Psalmi: Omnis homo mendax. Item, universa vanitas omnis homo. Tertio, quid autem Christus efficit? gratiam & veritatem. Gratiam, quia per ipsum consecuti sumus remissionem peccatorum, & in ipso diligimur. Sicut ante dictum est, gratiam pro gratia. Ut filius diligitur, ita diligimur & nos, qui in filio sumus per fidem. Est itaque gratia favor dei, quo non aliter atque filium diligit, & acceptat nos & nostra omnia, quamquam peccata. Veritas est verax iustitia cordis, qua per spiritum suum sanctificat corda nostra, & facit ea veracia, condens novam vitam & novum lumen. Ex his collige discrimen legis & Evangelii, Moysi & Christi.

## 1:18
No man hath seen God at any time.

This is the reason for the above, why grace and truth were made through Christ. For because grace is the divine will remitting sin: No one will acknowledge grace, except through him who is the counsel of the father, the son is the knowledge of the father: Wherefore his will cannot be known, except in the son. Likewise, because truth is our justification: Truth but it is the knowledge of God. And every knowledge of the Father is the Son. Therefore no one knows the Father, except through the Word, and in the Word which is the Son. Thus you see the reason of the superiors held in these words: No one has seen God at any time. etc. For if you ask, why was grace made through Jesus Christ? John answers: Therefore through Jesus Christ, because no one knows the Father except the Son. Nor can the opinion of the Father be known, except in the Word of Jesus Christ. Likewise why was truth made through Jesus Christ? He answers, because since justification is nothing other than the knowledge of God: it cannot be known, except through the Word and in the Word founding a new life, and a new light. The same things are handed down 2. Corinth. 3. & 4. And in the first of Peter, chapter one. To me it seems most simply that nothing else is said, than what Matthaei 11. is said: No one knows the Son, except the Father: nor does anyone know the Father, except the Son, and he to whom the Son shall wish to reveal him. For thus the Son knows the Father, so that also the substance of the Father is propagated into him, so that he is the universal knowledge of the Father, such knowledge, not only not to Moses, not to Jacob, but not even to any creature. And just as from this, no one knows the Father except the Son, it detracts nothing that Moses and Jacob saw God. So neither does this, no one has seen God at any time.

I rightly think the bosom is called a secret, as in the Psalm. My prayer shall turn into my bosom. And here he opens John [explains] what the power of the word is, and what our justification is. For the Son was sent, that he might declare the word of grace and truth: therefore the hearing of that word is justification. And the power of that word is to save, to make men truthful, to create a new light, to illuminate the darkness.

Latin:

Deum nemo vidit unquam.

Haec ratio superioris est, quare per Christum gratia & veritas facta sit. Quia enim gratia est voluntatis divinae remittentis peccatum: Nemo agnoscet gratiam, nisi per eum qui patris consilium est, filius scientia patris est: Quare sciri voluntas eius, nisi in filio non potest. Item quia veritas est iustificatio nostri: Veritas autem cognitio dei est. Et omnis scientia patris, filius est. Ideo patrem nemo cognoscit, nisi per verbum, & in verbo quod filius est. Ita vides rationem superiorum teneri in his verbis: Deum nemo vidit unquam. &c. Nam si quaeras, quare per Iesum Christum gratia facta est? Respondet Ioannes: Ideo per Iesum Christum, quia nemo cognoscit patrem praeter filium. Nec potest patris sententia, nisi in verbo Iesu Christi cognosci. Item quare per Iesum Christum veritas facta est? Respondet, quia cum iustificatio non sit aliud, nisi dei cognitio: cognosci, nisi per verbum & in verbo condenti novam vitam, & novum lumen, non potest. Eadem traduntur 2. Corinth. 3. & 4. Et primae Petri primo. Mihi simplicissime non aliud dici videtur, quàm quod Matthaei 11. dictum est: Nemo novit filium, nisi pater: neque patrem quis novit, nisi filius, & cui voluerit filius revelare. Sic enim filius patrem novit, ut & substantia patris in ipsum propagetur, ut sit universa scientia patris, qualis cognitio, non modo non Moysi, non Iacob, sed ne ullius quidem creaturae. Et sicut hinc, patrem nemo novit nisi filius, nihil derogat quod Moyses & Iacob viderunt deum. Ita neque hunc deum nemo vidit unquam.

Sinum recte puto secretum vocari, ut in Psalmo. Oratio mea in sinis meo convertetur. Atque hic aperit Ioannes quae sit verbi potentia, quae sit iustificatio nostri. Filius enim missus est, ut enarret verbum gratiae & veritatis: ergo auditus illius verbi, iustificatio est. Et verbi illius potentia est salvare, veraces efficere, novum lumen condere, illuminare tenebras.

## 1:19
And this is the testimony.

He repeats the testimony of John, which is cited twice above, for because John was sent by God, that he might testify concerning Christ, it was necessary that testimony be given concerning him. And why it was necessary for a witness to be sent beforehand, has been said above. And because the doctrine of Christ is new, namely the gratuitous remission of sins, and because Christ appeared in such forms as the flesh abominates: John was arguing against the Pharisees, as appears in Matthaei 3.. Therefore they send to him, questioning him about his authority, etc. Thus they questioned Christ, Matthaei 21.. In what power do you do these things? They ought to have looked at the word, and also to have estimated the authority of the person by the word. Now because they hate the word, and cannot condemn it, they therefore dispute about the authority of the person. John and Christ appear in other forms than the Pharisees wanted, therefore they condemn them. Thus in general the impious condemn, because Christ appears otherwise than they would wish. The flesh condemns him, it does not want to be mortified: therefore the living Christ he shudders. And this is the testimony of John, both that he himself is not the Christ, and who he is.

Latin:

Et hoc est testimonium.

Repetit Ioannis testimonium, quod supra bis citatur, quia enim Ioannes à deo missus fuit, ut testificaretur de Christo necessario, ut de hoc testimonium detur. Et cur oportuerit praemitti testem, supra dictum est. Et quia doctrina Christi nova est, scilicet gratuita remissio peccatorum, & quia apparuit Christus in huiusmodi formis, quas caro abominatur: arguebat Ioannes pharisaeos, sicut Matthaei 3. apparet. Ideo ad eum mittunt, interrogantes de autoritate &c. Sic Christum interrogabant, Matthaei 21. In qua potestate haec facis? Verbum spectare debebant, & etiam verbo autoritatem personae aestimare.
