Nunc quia verbum oderunt, & damnare non possunt, ideo de autoritate personae disputant. Aliis formis apparent Ioannes & Christus quàm volebant pharisaei, ideo damnant eos. Sic in universum damnant impii, quia aliter apparet Christus quàm vellent. Damnat eum caro, non vult mortificari: ideo viventem Christum exhorret. Et hoc est testimonium Ioannis, & quod ipse non sit Christus, & quis sit.

## 1:19
Priests and Levites.

The most powerful and most wise of the people, that they might interrogate him, that is, concerning his authority, why he was baptizing and preaching against the princes of the people.

Latin:

Sacerdotes & levitas.

Potentissimos & sapientissimos populi, ut interrogarent eum, id est de eius autoritate, cur baptizaret & praedicaret contra principes populi.

## 1:20
He confessed, and did not deny.

He said affirmatively he confessed, and did not deny, when it seems it could have been said more openly, he denied that he was the Christ. But this is more significant, because the Pharisees want him to retract his entire preaching. Therefore he says: He confessed, and did not deny, that is, he steadfastly confessed his word.

Are you Elijah? And he says, I am not.

Malachi 4. Elijah is promised to come, and Christ says that this John is he. How therefore does John here deny that he is Elijah? I answer, that Malachi is explained by the angel, Luke 1. In the spirit and power of Elijah. And so John was not Elijah, although the spirit and power of Elijah were in him.

Latin:

Confessus est, & non negavit.

Affirmative dixit confessus est, & non negavit, cum videatur apertius dici potuisse, negavit se esse Christum. Sed hoc significantius est, quia volunt pharisaei, ut universam praedicationem retractet. Ideo ait: Confessus est, & non negavit, id est constanter confessus est verbum suum.

Helias es tu? & dicit non.

Malachiae 4. promittitur venturus Helias, & hunc Christus dicit Ioannem esse. Quomodo igitur hic negat se Ioannes Heliam esse? Respondeo, Malachiam esse expositum per angelum, Lucae 1. In spiritu & veritate Heliae. Itaque non fuit Helias Ioannes, quanquam in eo spiritus & virtus Heliae fuerit.

## 1:21
Are you that prophet?

Some think that the prophet is to be understood here, who was promised Deutero. 18. But the Jews also understood that prophet to be Christ. Therefore, this passage does not seem to be applicable here. Why then does he deny that he is a prophet? I answer, the office of John is one thing, the office of the prophets is another: therefore John denies that he is a prophet. The prophets pertain to the Old Testament, John to the New, Matthaei 11. All the prophets and the law until John. And the prophecies were already fulfilled. The prophets prophesy about the Christ who is to come. John testifies that he is present and that the prophecies are fulfilled, therefore he denies that he is a prophet.

Latin:

Es tu propheta ille?

Prophetam hic quidam intelligendum esse putant, qui promissus Deutero. 18. Sed & eum prophetam intelligebant Iudaei Christum. Ideo non videtur eò conferendum esse hic locus. Quare ergo negat se esse prophetam? Respondeo, aliud esse Ioannis, aliud esse prophetarum officium: ideo Ioannes se negat esse prophetam. Prophetae ad vetus testamentum pertinent, Ioannes ad novum, Matthaei 11. Omnes prophetae & lex usque ad Ioannem. Et erant iam consummatae prophetiae. Prophetae de venturo Christo vaticinantur. Ioannes praesentem & consummatas esse prophetias testificatur, ideo se negat prophetam esse.

## 1:23
I am the voice of one crying.

What the office of John is, and what his baptism is, appears from this passage etc. And first indeed he cites the word of his calling, and the testimony from the prophet Esaias, so that he may indicate that his calling was made divinely. Also that his word is the word of God. I am the voice of one crying, that is, a crying voice or a preacher. I am a voice, not a light. Also a voice, because I do not now invent or teach a foreign word, nor a new word.

Latin:

Ego vox clamantis.

Quod sit Ioannis officium, & qui sit eius baptismus, ex hoc loco apparet &c. Et primum quidem vocationis suae verbum citat, & testimonium ex Esaia propheta, ut indicet vocationem sui divinitus esse factam. Item verbum suum, verbum dei esse. Ego sum vox clamantis, id est vox clamans seu praedicator. Vox sum, non lumen. Item vox, quia alienum verbum nunc, non novum verbum invenio seu doceo.

## 1:23
In the desert.

A voice in the desert, this pertains to history, because John was about to preach in the desert, and thus by the prophet It has been predicted for the sake of confirming the authority of John, as it pertains to the allegory. The desert figures his preaching. For John commands to prepare the way of the Lord.

Latin:

In deserto.

Vox in deserto, hoc ad historiam pertinet, quia Ioannes in deserto praedicaturus erat, & sic à propheta praedictum est propter confirmandam autoritatem Ioannis, quod ad allegoriam pertinet. Desertum figurat praedicationem eius. Iubet enim Ioannes parare viam domini.

## 1:23
Make straight the way of the Lord.

That is, prepare the way, level it, remove the obstacles from the middle of the way. And what this is, Isaiah indicates in the same place: All flesh is grass, and all its glory is as the flower of the field. This is the meaning: it is necessary to mortify the flesh and all its glory before Christ comes and vivifies the new creature: that is, before we receive the spirit of Christ. It is therefore the office of John to preach repentance, as the other Evangelists say. But true repentance is the true mortification of the flesh with all its concupiscences and with all its glory. And so John condemns whatever is in the flesh, so that it may become a way for Christ, that is, for the Gospel or consolation and vivification. Because before the flesh is mortified, it is not vivified by Christ. And as long as we think we can justify ourselves by our own powers, we acknowledge that we have no need of Christ, as it is said in Galat. 2.: If justice is through the law, then Christ died in vain. In the same place Isaiah adds the reason why the way must be leveled, and all high things cast down. Because, he says, the glory of the Lord shall be revealed. For John bound us to this, when he cited the prophet: To reveal the glory of the Lord, is to reveal the righteousness of God, and to exhibit the righteousness of God justifying us. Just as Paul says to the Romans: All are empty of the glory of God. But the righteousness of God cannot be revealed, unless first the righteousness and all the glory of the flesh is confounded. And from this it can be understood, why John baptizes. For baptism is a sign of mortification. Roman. 6. We are buried together with him through baptism into death. This baptism prepares the way for Christ, that is, vivification through Christ. Thus Matthaei 3. it is said: I baptize you in water unto repentance etc. It was necessary therefore for baptism to begin from John before Christ, because mortification precedes vivification, confusion precedes glorification, as Matthaei 3. it is said: He who is to come after me, will baptize you with the Holy Spirit and with fire.

The other part of the office of John is to indicate Christ, as he says here: That he might be manifested in Israel, therefore I came baptizing in water. And Isaiah says: Prepare the way of the Lord etc. because the glory of the Lord shall be revealed. John does not remit sins, but he shows him, who is about to remit them. Thus baptism is not a type of death, but a sign that we pass through death to life: a sign that death may not absorb, that it is the beginning of a new creature etc. Just as we see the children of Israel baptized in the Red Sea, to have entered into life through death. But there is this difference between the baptism of Christ and that of John, that the remission of sin was not yet expressed when John was baptizing, but it is expressed when Christ is baptizing.

Latin:

Dirigite viam domini.

Id est, apparate viam, complanate, tollite è medio viae impedimenta. Et quid hoc sit, indicat ibidem Esaias: Omnis caro soenum, et omnis gloria eius quasi flos agri. Est autem haec sententia: mortificare carnem, & omnem gloriam eius oportet, priusquam Christus veniat, & vivificet novam creaturam: id est priusquam accipiamus spiritum Christi. Est ergo Ioannis officium praedicare paenitentiam, sicut alii Evangelistae dicunt. Est autem vera paenitentia vera mortificatio carnis cum omnibus suis concupiscentiis, & cum omni gloria sua. Itaque Ioannes damnat quicquid in carne est, ut fiat via Christo, id est Evangelio seu consolationi, & vivificationi. Quia priusquam caro mortificata est, non vivificatur à Christo. Et quoad nostris viribus iustificare nos posse putamus, mundum agnoscimus Christo opus esse, ut Galat. 2. dicitur: Si per legem iustitia, ergo Christus frustra mortuus est. Ibidem Esaias causam addit, quare via complananda sit, deiicienda omnia excelsa. Quia, inquit, revelabitur gloria domini. Nam eo nos obligavit Ioannes, cum prophetam citavit: Revelari gloriam domini, est revelari iustitiam dei, & exhiberi iustitiam dei iustificantem nos. Sicut ad Romanos Paulus ait: Omnes sunt vacui gloria dei. Non potest autem iustitia dei revelari, nisi prius iustitia & omni gloria carnis confusa. Et ex hoc potest intelligi, cur Ioannes baptizet. Est enim baptismus signum mortificationis. Roman. 6. Consepulti sumus cum illo per baptismum in mortem. Hic baptismus praeparat viam Christo, id est vivificationem per Christum. Sic Matthaei 3. dicitur: Baptizo vos in aqua in paenitentiam &c. Oportuit itaque baptismum incipi à Ioanne ante Christum, quia mortificatio praecedit vivificationem, confusio glorificationem, ut Matthaei 3. dicitur: Qui post me venturus est, baptizabit vos spiritusancto & igni.

Altera pars officii Ioannis est, indicare Christum, sicut hic ait: Ut manifestetur in Israël, propterea veni ego in aqua baptizare. Et Esaias ait: Parate viam domino &c. quia gloria domini revelabitur. Non remittit Ioannes peccata, sed eum ostendit, qui remissurus est. Sic baptismus non mortis typus est, sed signum quod per morten ad vitam transeamus: signum quod mors non absorbeat, quod sit initium novae creaturae &c. Sicut filios Israël videmus mari rubro baptizatos, per mortem in vitam ingressos esse. Sed inter Christi baptismum & Ioannis hoc interest, quod remissio peccati nondum expressa fuit Ioanne baptizante, expressa est Christo baptizante.

## 1:24
And those who had been sent.

See an example of Pharisaical blindness. John had already answered who he was, by whom he had been sent, by whose authority he was doing what he was doing: yet they understood none of these things, not knowing the scriptures and the power of God, with the testimony of his calling, preaching, and baptism having been produced: Then they still ask by whose authority he baptizes. And the Evangelist expressed the circumstances beautifully when he says: Those who had been sent were of the Pharisees, and they asked etc. as if he were saying: Although they were of the Pharisees, that is, of the wisest and holiest, yet they did not understand the answer of John. Thus the Evangelist sketches for us the blindness and darkness of the flesh. The Pharisees here ask, as if they had sensed that Christ was about to baptize. For concerning the baptism of Christ it is in Isaiah 44. I will pour waters upon the thirsty. And Ezekiel 36. I will pour clean water upon you, and you shall be cleansed from all your iniquities.

Latin:

Et qui missi fuerant.

Vide exemplum pharisaicae caecitatis. Iam responderat Ioannes, quis esset, à quo missus esset, cuius auntoritate faceret quae faciebat: cum ipsi nihil horum intellexerunt, nescientes scripturas & virtutem dei, producto testimonio vocationis, praedicationis, & baptismi: Tum adhuc interrogant, cuius autoritate baptizet. Et Evangelista circumstantias pulchre expressit, cum inquit: Qui missi fuerant, erant ex pharisaeis, & interrogaverunt &c. quasi diceret: Quanquam ex pharisaeis, id est ex sapientissimis & sanctissimis essent, tamen responsum Ioannis non intellexerunt. Sic nobis Evangelista adumbrat caecitatem, & tenebras carnis. Pharisaei hic interrogant, quasi senserint Christum baptizaturum esse. Nam de baptismo Christi est apud Esaiam 44. Effundam aquas super sitientes. Et Ezechielis 36. Effundam super vos aquam mundam, & mundabimini ab omnibus iniquitatibus vestris.

## 1:26
I baptize in water.

John does not answer the question proposed, why he was baptizing. For he had answered before, when he says: I am the voice of one crying in the wilderness, etc. Here he distinguishes his baptism and his ministry from Christ's baptism and office, as if he were saying: I baptize in water unto repentance, so that you may be mortified. I do not remit sins, I do not sanctify. I am only a voice and a messenger of the word. He is the power of the word. And as was said above, he is the light, not I, etc. There has stood in the midst of you: That is, among you, whom you do not know, although you are Pharisees. He who is to come after me, namely, to remit sins. He who was before me, that is, who is greater than I, or has been made so that he is greater than I, etc.

Latin:

Ego baptizo in aqua.

Non respondet Ioannes ad quaestionem propositam, cur baptizaret. Nam antea responderat, cum inquit: Ego vox clamantis in deserto &c. Hic discernit suum baptismum, & suum ministerium à Christi baptismo & officio, quasi diceret: Ego baptizo in aqua ad paenitentiam, ut mortificemini. Non remitto peccata, non sanctifico. Ego vox tantum sum, & nuncius verbi. Ille est verbi potentia. Et ut supra dictum est, ille lux est, non ego &c. Medio vestrum stetit: Id est inter vos, quem vos nescitis, quamvis pharisaei. Qui post me venturus est, scilicet remissurus peccata. Qui ante me fuit, id est qui est maior me, vel factus est ut sit maior me &c.

## 1:27
Whose I am not worthy, etc.

In many ways John prefers Christ to himself, so that it may be understood that Christ is not only a prophet, but is the savior. And because he was understood to be the savior, it followed that they should acknowledge him to be God. This is the sum of John's testimony, therefore he casts himself down below him, saying that he is not worthy to unloose the latchet of his shoe for him, that is, that he is not worthy to serve him in even any part. In which sentiment, however simple, he first confesses his divinity. For a man is certainly worthy to serve a man, because we were created for mutual ministries. Genesis 1. & 2. Then all the glory of all he condemns our works, in that he confesses that not even he himself is worthy to serve. Therefore it is mere grace, mere mercy, to have received the ability to serve. Finally, he reproves the blindness, pride, and swelling of the Pharisees, as if he were saying: You wise men, you holy men, despise him whom I preach to you. You hold the kingdom, you hold the priesthood, when he has been ordained by God as king and priest, to whom it was fitting that you be subject. Now if you seek an allegory of the shoe: it signifies the word or the Gospel: because we ought to walk in the word, not in the light of nature. Just as it is said Numbers 9.: By the word of the Lord the tents were made, and by his word they set out. Thus Ephesians 6.: Shod your feet in the preparation of the Gospel of peace: That is, that you may be prepared by the Gospel of peace. And Ezekiel 16.: I shod you with hyacinth. John therefore says Matthew 3.: Whose shoes I am not worthy to carry: That is, to carry the word, or the Gospel. And here: I am not worthy to loosen the thong of his sandals: That is, to unfold the Gospel. In whatever way you explain it, it remains that John acknowledges himself to be unworthy to minister to Christ, so that all flesh may not boast.

Latin:

Cuius ego non sum dignus &c.

Multipliciter Ioannes sibi Christum praefert, ut intelligatur Christus non propheta tantum, sed salvator esse. Et quia salvator esse intelligebatur, consequebatur ut deum esse agnoscerent. Haec summa testimonii Ioannis est, ideo se deiicit infra eum, inquiens se non esse dignum, qui illi solvat corrigiam calciamenti, id est se non esse dignum, ne ulla quidem parte servire. In qua sententia quantumvis simplici, primum confitetur divinitatem. Nam homo certe dignus est, ut homini serviat, quia ad mutua ministeria conditi sumus. Genesis 1. & 2. Deinde omnem gloriam omnium operum nostrorum damnat, in eo quod confitetur, ne se quidem dignum esse qui serviat. Ergo mera gratia, mera misericordia est, accepisse ut servias. Postremo pharisaeorum caecitatem, superbiam, & tumorem reprehendit, quasi diceret: Vos sapientes, vos sancti contemnitis eum, quem vobis praedico. Vos regnum, vos sacerdotium tenetis, cum ille sit ordinatus à deo rex & sacerdos, cui vos subiici oportuit. Iam si quaeras allegoriam calcei: verbum seu Evangelium significat: quia in verbo debemus ingredi, non in lumine naturae. Sicut Numerorum 9. dicitur: Per verbum domini fiebant tentoria, & per verbum illius proficiscebantur. Sic Ephes. 6. Calciate pedes in praeparationem Evangelii pacis: Id est ut sitis praeparati Evangelio pacis. Et Ezechielis 16. Calciavi te hyacintho. Ait ergo Ioannes Matthaei 3. Cuius non sum dignus portare calceos: Id est portare verbum, seu Evangelium. Et hic: Non sum dignus solvere calciamentorum corrigiam: Id est pandere Evangelium. Quo cunque modo exponas, manet quod Ioannes agnoscit se indignum esse, qui ministret Christo, ut non glorietur omnis caro.

## 1:29
The next day he saw &c.

The next day, that is on another day: because it is a Grecism, lest urge that it be, on the following day.

Latin:

Altera die vidit &c.

Altera die, id est alio die: quia Graecismus est, ne urgeat esse, sequenti die.

## 1:29
Behold the lamb of God.

John repeats the testimony concerning Christ, because the conscience has need of many witnesses, etc. Again John testifies, and more clearly concerning Christ than before. For in these words is the sum of the Gospel. Behold, as if he were saying, this is that savior, appearing in so vile a form, and to be reckoned among the wicked, etc. Made sin by God, that he might justify us, etc. whom you Pharisees think will come with great glory before the world, etc. Lamb, he looks back to the type in the law, the paschal lamb, whose blood was a sign to the angel, so that he would not kill the Israelites. Thus the blood of this lamb is a pledge, so that those who are sprinkled with it may not be killed. The typical lamb of God in the law was offered by the people, taken from the flock. This is the lamb of God, that is, not taken from the flock, but given by God for our sins. And thus you see grace being commended. We did not obtain that he should be offered, but he offered himself of his own accord, etc.

Latin:

Ecce agnus dei.

Repetit Ioannes testimonium de Christo, quia conscientia multis indiget testibus &c. Rursum Ioannes testificatur, & clarius de Christo quàm ante. Nam in iis verbis est summa Evangelii. Ecce, quasi diceret, hic est ille savator, apparens tam vili forma, & inter iniquos computandus &c. Factus peccatum à deo, ut nos iustificet &c. quem vos pharisaei venturum putatis cum magna gloria coram mundo &c. Agnus, respicit typum in lege, agnum paschalem, cuius sanguis signum erat angelo, ne Israëlitas occideret. Ita huius agni sanguis pignus est, ne qui sunt eo aspersi, occidantur. Dei agnus typicus in lege offerebatur à populo, acceptus ex grege. Hic agnus dei, id est non acceptus ex grege, sed datus à deo pro peccatis nostris. Atque ita vides gratiam commendaeri. Non nos impetravimus ut offerretur, sed ipse se ultro obtulit &c.

## 1:29
Who takes away the sins, etc.

Here is the first expressed preaching of the remission of sins. And here is explained what kind of kingdom Christ's will be, that is, not carnal, but spiritual, of righteousness, of truth, and of peace, etc.

Latin:

Qui tollit peccata &c.

Hic prima est expressa praedicatio remissionis peccatorum. Atque hic exponitur quale futurum regnum Christi, id est, non carnale, sed spirituale, iustitiae, veritatis, & pacis &c.

## 1:29
Of the world.

Not only of the Jews, but also of the Gentiles. This is indeed such a one, since you expect a much different one, according to Malachi.

Latin:

Mundi.

Non tantum Iudaeorum, sed et Gentium. Hic est scilicet talis, cum vos multo alium expectetis, iuxta Malachiam.

## 1:30
He comes after me.

What the force of this testimony is, can be gathered from Malachi. First, what he says: He comes after me: He looks to the words of Malachi: Behold I will send my messenger, and he shall prepare the way before my face, and presently the Lord shall come to his temple, etc.

Man.

Here John says that Malachi speaks of the ruler. Likewise John signifies that he is to come who was promised to the fathers, because Malachi says: The angel of the testament whom you desire. He signifies also that Christ is God, because Malachi says: Before my face, etc.

Latin:

Post me venit.

Quae sit huius testimonii vis, ex Malachia colligi potest. Primum, quod ait: Post me venit: Respicit ad verba Malachiae: Ecce ego mittam angelum meum, & praeparabit viam ante faciem meam, & statim veniet ad templum &c.

Vir.

Hic dicit Ioannes, quod Malachias dominatorem. Item significat Ioannes, eum venturum qui sit promissus patribus, quia dicit Malachias: Angelus testamenti quem vos vultis. Significat & deum esse Christum, quia Malachias ait: Ante faciem meam &c.

## 1:30
He was made before me.

Before I was sent, he himself was sent. And because he is already present in the flesh, and was present before I was sent, and began to preach: and because he is the one who was ordained first of all before all the prophets, and for whose sake all the prophets were sent, who bear witness (For thus he explains himself, because he was before me, that is, greater. For I am his minister, etc.)

Therefore he is not a prophet, but the savior.

Latin:

Ante me factus est.

Priusquam ego mitterer, ipse missus est. Et quia iam adest in carne, & adfuit priusquam ego mitterer, & inciperem praedicare: & quia ipse est, qui primus omnium ordinatus ante omnes prophetas, & propter quem omnes prophetae missi sunt, qui perhibent testimonium (Sic enim se exponit, quia prior me erat, id est maior. Nam ego eius minister sum &c.)

Itaque ille non propheta, sed salvator est.

## 1:31
And I knew him not.

That is, I did not know him, but he was shown by divine revelation. Here John confirms his authority by his testimony, in that he says he did not know him. He alleges the divine revelation by which Christ was shown to him. For flesh cannot know Christ, as Matthaei 16. Blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jonah, because flesh and blood has not revealed it to you, etc.

Latin:

Et ego nesciebam eum.

Id est non noram eum, sed ostensus est divina revelatione. Hic Ioannes testimonio suo comprobat autoritatem in eo, quod ait se non novisse. Allegat divinam revelationem qua sibi sit ostensus Christus. Non enim potest Christum novisse caro, sicut Matthaei 16. Beatus es Simon Bariona, quia caro & sanguis non revelavit tibi &c.

## 1:31
But that he might be manifested.

Namely, because you all were expecting a carnal kingdom and a carnal redemption: no one was going to take this man for Christ, therefore I was sent divinely, that I might show him.

Latin:

Sed ut manifestetur.

Scilicet quia omnes expectatis carnale regnum, & carnalem redemptionem: nemo pro Christo habiturus erat hunc, ideo ego divinitus missus sum, ut ostendam eum.

## 1:31
Therefore I came baptizing in water.

To preach repentance, so that they might believe they would receive the remission of sin through him who is to come. From this you will gather the distinction between the baptism of John and of Christ. It is disputed by Augustine in this place, what is the fruit of the baptism of John, and why it endured in the church. But I answer, the baptism of John and of Christ is represented by one sign, indeed the sign itself was instituted by John not, as if from a preacher of repentance. For it is a sign of mortification, which it is necessary to be done before the remission of sins. And when we are baptized, we are baptized with both the baptism of John and of Christ, that is, we are mortified, and to those who are mortified sins are remitted. Furthermore, the office of John is properly to preach repentance, that of Christ is properly to remit sins. When this history was performed, it can be understood from the other Evangelists: namely, after Christ was baptized by John. And there John testifies concerning Christ, when he says: I ought to be baptized by you: That is, I have need of being justified by you. Christ answered: Suffer it now: That is, it is not yet the time of my glorification. It becomes us to fulfill all righteousness. That is, it behooves me to be subjected to the law, to be baptized, to be humbled, to be mortified, that I may satisfy the law, that I may pay the law in full. Afterwards, as Christ was ascending, the heavens were opened, and John saw the spirit of God descending.

Latin:

Propterea veni ego in aqua baptizans.

Praedicare paenitentiam, ut credant, accepturos remissionem peccati per eum qui venturus est. Hinc discrimen baptismi Ioannis & Christi colliges. Ab Augustino hoc loco disputatur, quis sit fructus baptismi Ioannis, & cur in ecclesia duraverit. Ego vero respondeo, uno signo repraesentari baptismum Ioannis & Christi, immo signum ipsum institutum est à Ioan ne, tanquam à praedicatore paenitentiae. Est enim signum mortificationis, quam necesse est fieri ante remissionem peccatorum. Et cum baptizamur, & Ioannis & Christi baptismo baptizamur, id est mortificamur, & mortificatis remittuntur peccata. Caeterum Ioannis officium proprie est, paenitentiam praedicare, Christi proprie est peccata remittere. Quando haec historia gesta sit, ex aliis Evangelistis intelligi potest: nempe posteaquam à Ioanne Christus baptizatus est. Atque ibi Ioannes testificatur de Christo, cum ait: Ego à te debeo baptizari: Id est ego te iustificante opus habeo. Respondit Christus: Sine modo: Id est nondum est tempus glorificationis meae. Decet nos implere omnem iustitiam. Id est oportet me subiici lege, baptizari, humiliari, mortificari, ut legi satisfaciam, ut legem persolvam. Postea ascendente Christo, aperti sunt caeli, & vidit Ioannes spiritum dei descendentem.

## 1:32
Because I saw the spirit descending.

The testimony by which the Father testifies concerning the Son is the spirit resting upon Christ, as it is said below cap. 6.: For him has God the Father sealed. And Esaiae 61. The spirit of the Lord is upon me, because he has anointed me. And Psalmo 44. God, your God, has anointed you. This anointing is that which The anointing of the priests in the law prefigured this. This distills from the head of Christ into the beard of Aaron, that is, into the whole generation of Christ. This is the spirit which bears witness to our heart that we are sons of God, crying, Abba Father. Here you see how great a difference there is between the generation of Christ and the generation of the flesh. This passage has tortured many, why John says he did not know Christ before he saw the dove descending from heaven. Moreover, the spirit descended after baptism. Furthermore, even before baptism John seems to have known Christ, when he says: I ought to be baptized by you. There are those who think John only then learned the divinity of Christ, when previously he had known his humanity, and knew he was the savior, although he did not know he was God. My reasoning is thus, that John alleges this sign, by which the Father bore witness to Christ the Son. Even though he himself recognized Christ from a prior revelation: nevertheless John does not allege a private revelation, he alleges this sign, which the prophets said would be the sign of Christ, Esaiae 61. The spirit of the Lord is upon me. So that this is the meaning: I, John, did not know Christ, but God revealing it I learned, and the Father bore this witness of his revelation. He showed the spirit descending upon Christ, which sign I allege to you. For this is the sign by which the Father sealed the Son. This is he who baptizes, that is he makes a new creature, a new light, a new heart, with the old one mortified. Therefore, the first thing to be observed here is that Christ is not known by the flesh, as Matthew 16. Blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jonah, etc. Secondly, Christ was sealed and anointed with the Holy Spirit, so that we too might be anointed and sealed, and his anointing might drip down upon the beard of Aaron. And just as the Father testifies concerning the Son by sending the Holy Spirit, so Paul calls the Spirit in us the earnest of grace. And in turn, the Spirit testifies to us that we are children of God, crying out: Abba, Father.

Latin:

Quia vidi spiritum descendentem.

Testimonium quo de filio testificatur pater, est spiritus incumbens super Christum, sicut infra cap. 6. dicitur: Hunc enim pater signavit deus. Et Esaiae 61. Spiritus domini super me, eo quod unxit me. Et Psalmo 44. Unxit te deus, deus tuus. Haec unxio est, quam figuravit in lege sacerdotum unctio. Haec distillat à capite Christi in barbam Aaron, hoc est, in totam generationem Christi. Hic spiritus est, qui testimonium perhibet cordi nostro, quod sumus filii dei, clamans, abba pater. Hic vides quantum intersit inter generationem Christi, & carnis generationem. Multos torsit hic locus, cur Ioannes dicat se non novisse Christum, priusquam vidit descendentem columbam de caelo. Descendit autem spiritus post baptismum. Porrò & ante baptismum videtur Ioannes novisse Christum, cum inquit: Ego à te debeo baptizari. Sunt qui putant Ioannem tum demum didicisse divinitatem Christi, cum antea nosset eius humanitatem, & sciret salvatorem esse, quanquam deum nesciret. Mea sic est ratio, Ioannem hoc signum allegare, quo pater testimonium Christo filio perhibuit. Ut maxime ipse ex priore revelatione Christum agnoverit: tamen non allegat Ioannes privatam revelationem, hoc signum allegat, quod prophetae Christi signum esse dixerunt, Esaiae 61. Spiritus domini super me. Ut sit haec sententia: Ego Ioannes non noram Christum, sed revelante deo didici, & revelationis suae pater hoc testimonium perhibuit. Ostendit spiritum descendentem super Christum, quod signum vobis allego. Nam hoc signum est quo pater signavit filium. Hic est qui baptizat, id est facit novam creaturam, novum lumen, novum cor, mortificato veteri. Primum ergo hic observandum est, Christum à carne non cognosci, ut Matthaei 16. Beatus es Simon Bariona &c. Secundo, Christus signatus est & unctus spiritusancto, ut & nos ungamur & signemur, & distillet unctio eius in barbam Aaron. Et sicut pater testificatur de filio misso spiritusancto, ita in nobis spiritum arrabonem gratiae vocat Paulus. Et vicissim testificatur nobis spiritus, quod simus filii dei, clamans: Abba pater.

## 1:32
And I have seen.

This is the sign by which the Father testified concerning the Son, and I have borne witness that this is the Son of God. And this is the Evangelical confession, as appears in Matthew 16. You are Christ the Son of the living God. Furthermore, to confess that Christ is the Son of God is to confess that he is God, Savior, heir, and Lord of all. Likewise, just as he himself is heir and Lord of all, so it is necessary to believe that those who are in Christ are co-heirs of Christ. And therefore that they themselves are also lords of all, of sin, of death, of the world, in short, of the gates of hell. The Apostle speaks of this faith in Rom. 10. If you believe in your heart that God has raised him from the dead, that is, has glorified him, has made him Lord of all, etc. Likewise to believe, just as he himself is the Son, and so beloved by the Father: Thus we who believe in the Son, are beloved by the Father. Thus in Romans 8. He who gave his only-begotten Son for us, how will he not also give us all things with him?

Latin:

Et ego vidi.

Hoc signum quo pater de filio testificatus est, & testimonium perhibui, quod hic sit filius dei. Et haec est confessio Evangelica, sicut Matthaei 16. apparet: Thes Christus filius dei vivi. Porrò confiteri, quod Christus sit filius dei, est confiteri quod sit deus, salvator, haeres, & dominus omnium. Item sicut ipse est haeres & dominus omnium, ita credioportet, eos qui sunt in Christo, cohaeredes esse Christi. Adeóque & ipsos esse dominos omnium, peccati, mortis, mundi, breviter portarum inferi. De hac fide loquitur Apostolus Rom. 10. Si in corde tuo credis, quod deus suscitaverit eum à mortuis, id est glorificaverit, fecit dominum omnium &c. Item credere, sicut ipse est filius, adeoque dilectus patri: Ita nos qui credimus filio, dilectos esse patri. Sic ad Romanos 8. Qui dedit filium unigenitum suum pro nobis, quomodo non omnia cum illo donantur?

## 1:35
The next day &c.

These things are to be understood as the former ones. The Lamb of God, that is, who was given by God for our sins. And it expresses the humiliation of Christ. Who takes away sins: He calls him glorified, because he broke the gates of the underworld.

Latin:

Altera die &c.

Haec sicut superiora intelligenda sunt. Agnus dei, id est qui à deo datus est pro peccatis nostris. Et exprimit Christi humiliationem. Qui tollit peccata: Glorificatum dicit, quia fregit inferorum portas.

## 1:37
And the two disciples heard him.

Another testimony, both that the preaching of John is true, and that Christ is greater than John, and so also the Savior, because the disciples of John necessarily yield to Christ. John preaches the presence and points out Christ, who remits sins and sanctifies: Therefore they yield from the preaching of John to Christ, before repentance is preached Christ is not known. Because righteousness and life are not known, unless first the knowledge of sin, or death is known. As in the Canticle: He has filled the hungry with good things, and the rich &c. Secondly, they do not perform the office of John, who, once repentance has been preached, send consciences to satisfactions, to works.

Latin:

Et audierunt eum duo discipuli.

Aliud testimonium, & quod Ioannis praedicatio vera sit, & quod Christus maior sit Ioanne, adeóque & salvator, quia discipuli Ioannis necessario concedunt ad Christum. Ioannes praedicat praesentiam & ostendit Christum, qui remittat peccata & sanctificet: Ideo à Ioannis praedicatione ad Christum concedunt, ante praedicatam paenitentiam non cognoscitur Christus. Quia iustitia & vita non cognoscitur, nisi prius cognitio peccato, seu cognita morte. Sicut in Cantico: Esurientes implevit bonis, & divites &c. Secundo non agunt Ioannis officium, qui iam praedicata paenitentia, conscientias mittunt ad satisfactiones, ad opera.

## 1:38
Having turned, what do you seek?

Moved by the preaching of John, they go to Christ. But what kind of person does Christ show himself to them? The words and deeds of Christ are promises of grace, furthermore also beyond words, the deeds of Christ. He describes the Gospel in such a way that you may discern in them the grace being offered. He gently compels, he rejoices to save, he confirms. Paul writes to the Philippians that he was found in appearance as a man, so that you may recognize that he wishes to be a brother, that he does not wish to be feared, that he wishes to be trusted. Having turned, he is clearly of a countenance of mercy. 2. Corinth. 3. Paul distinguishes the countenance of Moses and the countenance of Christ. The countenance of Moses terrified. The descent of God to the people also terrified, Exodus 19. The countenance of Christ is a countenance of mercy and grace. What do you seek? that is, why do you follow me? who do you believe me to be that you should follow? First he asks what they seek: because this one thing Christ requires from those following, that they believe, according to that saying of Isaiah: His knowledge shall justify many.

Latin:

Conversus, quid quaeritis?

Ioannis praedicatione commoti, ad Christum concedunt. Sed qualem se illis exhibet Christus? Verba et facta Christi sunt promissiones gratiae, praeterea etiam praeter verba, facta Christi. Sic describit Evangelium, ut in illis cernas exhibentem gratiam. Suaviter compellat, gaudet servare, confirmat. Ad Philippenses scribit Paulus, habitu inventum esse, ut homo, ut agnoscas velle fratrem esse, non velle se metui, velle sibi confidi. Conversus scilicet vultu misericordiae. 2. Corinth. 3. discernit Paulus vultum Moysi, & vultum Christi. Vultus Moysi terruit. Terruit et descensus dei ad populum, Exodi 19. Vultus Christi est vultus misericordiae et gratiae. Quid quaeritis? id est cur me sequimini? quem me esse creditis ut sequamini? Primum interrogat, quid quaerant: quia hoc unum requirit Christus à sequentibus, ut credant, iuxta illud Esaiae: Cognitio eius iustificabit multos.

## 1:38
Where do you dwell?

Where do you stay in Greek, that is, what is the lodging to which you have turned aside? The disciples do not yet testify with their voice, but they confess the affection of faith.

Latin:

Ubi habitas?

Ubi manes in Graeco, id est quod est hospitium quò divertisti? Discipuli nondum voce testificantur, sed affectum fidei confitentur.

## 1:39
Come and see.

When consciences ask where Christ stays, it cannot be shown, except by his word alone, therefore he himself says: Come and see. as if he were saying, I myself show you.

Latin:

Venite & videte.

Cum interrogant conscientiae, ubi maneat Christus, monstrari id non potest, nisi solo ipsius verbo, ideo ipse ait: Venite & videte. quasi diceret, ipse monstro vobis.

## 1:39
But it was about the tenth hour.

I think mention of the hour was made, because Christ came in the last time.

Latin:

Hora autem erat quasi decima.

Horae mentionem factam puto, quia novissimo tempore venit Christus.

## 1:41
We have found &c.

Now they also confess Christ with their voice, it was enough to have responded with affection, here they testify with their voice before others.

Latin:

Invenimus &c.

Nunc & voce confitentur Christo, satis erat affectum respondisse, hic apud alios voce testificantur.

## 1:42
You are Peter son of Jonah.

It is an argument of divinity, that he foresees, and that he knows the name, and who the father of this one is. Then it is a much stronger argument, that he signifies the future, so that Peter may persevere.

Latin:

Tu es Petrus filius Iona.

Argumentum divinitatis est, quod praevidet, & quod nomen, & quis sit pater huius. Deinde multo potius argumentum est, quod significat futurum, ut perseveret Petrus.

## 1:46
Can anything good come from Nazareth?

What Nathanael takes offense at here, the same below cap. 7. offends certain people, saying: Does Christ come from Galilee? For they knew the prophecy about Bethlehem, But Christ always appears to the flesh in other forms, in another way than the flesh thinks.

Latin:

Ex Nazaret aliquid boni?

Quod hic Nathanaël offendit, idem infra cap. 7. offendit quosdam, dicentes: Nunquid à Galilaea venit Christus? Prophetiam enim norant de Bethleem, Sed Christus semper apparet carni aliis formis, alio modo quàm cogitat caro.

## 1:47
Behold a true Israelite.

That is, not according to the flesh only &c. Fig tree signifies the synagogue. Nathanael, the people chosen from the synagogue, which people is the true seed of Israel. And therefore Christ says: Truly an Israelite, that he may distinguish Israel according to the flesh from the elect Israel, whose is the promise, etc., which the people say concerning the increase of faith Rom. 5. Probation works hope, hope does not confound. And 12. That you may prove what is the good, acceptable, and perfect will of God. Christ wants the same thing here, when he says: Because I saw you under the fig tree, believe you will see greater things than these. Faith grows through the experience of the good will, it produces hope from that which is thus proved. You will see the vision of Jacob fulfilled, concerning which Genesis 28.

Latin:

Ecce vere Israëlita.

Id est non secundum carnem tantum &c. Ficus significat synagogam. Nathanaël, populum ex synagoga electum, qui populus verum semen Israël est. Ideoque Christus ait: Vere Israëlita ut discernat Israelem secundum carnem ab electo Israël, cuius est promissio &c. quod populus de incremento fidei dicit Rom. 5. Probatio spem operatur, spes non confundit. Et 12. Ut probetis quae sit voluntas dei bona, beneplacens, & perfecta. Idem hic vult Christus, cum ait: Quia vidi te sub ficu, crede maiora his videbis. Crescit fides per experientiam bonae voluntatis, de sic probato spem efficit. Videbitis impletam visionem Iacob, de qua Genesis 28.

## 1:51
You will see heaven opened.

These things are taken from Genesis 28. concerning the ladder of Jacob. Christ is the ladder, God leans upon the ladder, because Christ is God. The ladder reaches from heaven to earth. For Christ therefore ascended into heaven, that he might fulfill all things. Angels ascend and descend, that is, all believers in Christ ascend and descend. First, that Christ is the ladder erected from earth into heaven, and it signifies Christ is not more in heaven than on earth, ruling and governing among us. Second, that in it we ascend and descend, is the very bosom to which we are carried. Thus Isaiah 46. You who are carried from my womb, I have made, I will bear and I will carry, and I will save. Thirdly, they do not ascend or descend on the ladder who are ruled by their own judgment, by their own light, or who live in the righteousnesses of the flesh, or who walk in any way according to the flesh. Fourthly, to ascend is first, because it is necessary first to ascend to God through faith. To descend is later. Moreover, the fruit of the ascent is the preaching of the word, and every work of charity. He says the heaven is opened according to Isaiah 24. Oh that you would tear open the heavens, etc. Moreover, it is that there is an approach for us to the Father through this high priest Christ.

Latin:

Videbitis caelum apertum.

Haec accepta sunt ex Genesi. 28. de scala Iacob. Christus scala est, deus incumbit scalae, quia Christus deus est. Eascala à caelo ad terram pertingit. Nam Christus ideo ascendit in caelum, ut impleret omnia. Angeli ascendunt & descendunt, id est omnes credentes in Christo ascendunt & descendunt. Primo, quod Christus scala est à terra in caelum erecta, et significat Christum non magis in caelo esse, quàm in terra, inter nos regentem & gubernantem. Secundo, quod in ea ascendimus & descendimus, est ipsum esse sinum quò ferimur. Sic Esaiae 46. Qui portamini à meo utero, ego feci, ego feram & portabo, & salvabo. Tertio, non ascendunt aut descendunt in scala, qui suo dictamine, suo lumine reguntur, aut qui in iustitiis carnis vivunt, aut quomodocunque secundum carnem ambulant. Quarto, ascendere primum est, quia oportet primum per fidem ad deum ascendere. Descendere posterius est. Est autem fructus ascensus, praedicatio verbi, & omne opus charitatis. Caelum apertum dicit iuxta Esaiam 24. Utinam dirumperes caelos &c. Est autem, aditum nobis esse ad patrem per hunc pontificem Christum.

## 2:1 Argument
CHAPTER TWO.

Latin:

CAPUT SECUNDUM.

## 2:1
Weddings were taking place in Cana of Galilee.

It signifies weddings, not the weddings of Christ, but of the people with the law. This people lacks wine, that is, the Gospel. It hears the law, and seeks righteousness from the works of the law, which is mere hypocrisy, ignorant of the righteousness of the heart, which is the righteousness of the spirit. Israel the elect, who awaits redemption, asks for wine to be sent. Christ delays his advent, and he allows the people to be afflicted before he comes. The six water jars represent the doctrine of the law. Therefore the Evangelist adds, according to the purification of the Jews. Into these is poured water which becomes wine, that is, the Gospel, which is the doctrine of mortification. Likewise, the servants pour in the water, because ministers do not sanctify, but Christ does. The master of the feast represents the apostles and preachers, who dispense the word. And they marvel that wine is made from water, that is, that those who believe are saved by the foolishness of preaching; they discern the Gospel from the wisdom of the flesh. The desires of wisdom and of the flesh will perish, but the righteous will not be moved. Likewise, the Father gave the people of Israel good wine to drink, namely, the law. But because it was a carnal people, they made from the law masculine images, traditions, and carnal works. Christ, however, abolishes all these things when he mortifies, and gives the mortified to drink of a sweeter wine. His brothers represent his kinsmen. By the money-changers and bankers, he understands those who exchange money. Zeal is indignation, which proceeds from love, and is an affection of the greatest spirits. For thus the Father is angry with the son, who is so angered, that he would nevertheless prefer that he ought not to be angry.

What sign do you show?

Namely, that these things are done by you by divine authority.

Destroy this temple, that is, this body. Because Christ did this by divine authority, therefore John cited this passage.

And they believed the scripture.

Namely, testifying concerning the death and resurrection of Christ, etc.

Latin:

Nuptiae fiebant in Cana Galilaeae.

Nuptiae, non Christi nuptias, sed populi cum lege significat. Hic populus vino, id est Evangelio deficit. Legem audit, & ex operibus legis quaerit iustitiam, quae est mera hypocrisis, ignorans cordis iustitiae, quae est iustitia spiritus. Israël electus, qui expectat redemptionem, rogat vinum mitti. Christus differt adventum suum, & sinit antea affligi populum quàm veniat. Hydriae sex doctrinam legis repraesentant. Ideo addit Evangelista, secundum purificationem Iudaeorum. Iis infunditur aqua quae vinum fit, id est Evangelium quod mortificationis doctrina est. Item ministri aquam infundunt, quia ministri non sanctificant, sed Christus. Architriclinus repraesentat apostolos & praedicatores, qui dispensant verbum. Et ii mirantur ex aqua vinum fieri, id est stultitia praedicationis salvos fieri credentes, ii discernunt Evangelium à sapientia carnis. Sapientiae, carnis desyderia peribunt, iusti autem non commovebuntur. Item populum Israël bono vino potavit pater, scilicet lege. Sed quia carnalis populus erat, fecit ex lege imagines masculinas, traditiones, & opera carnalia. Christus autem haec omnia abolet, cum mortificat, & mortificatos suaviori vino potat. Fratres eius, cognatos repraesentat. Per numularios & trapezitas, mutantes pecuniam intelligit. Zelus est indignatio, quae ex amore proficiscitur, & est maximorum spirituum affectus. Sic enim pater irascitur filio, qui sic ira scitur, ut mallet tamen se non irasci oportere.

Quod signum ostendis?

Scilicet, quod haec autoritate divina à te fiant.

Solvite templum hoc, id est, hoc corpus. Quod autoritate divina id faceret Christus, ideo Ioannes citavit hunc locum.

Et crediderunt scripturae.

Scilicet testificanti de morte & resurrectione Christi &c.

## 2:4
What have I to do with you, woman?

Christ rejects his mother, so that he may make her equal to other humans, when he says: What have I to do with you, woman? As if he were saying, you who are my mother, therefore you seem to be about to obtain something from me by the merit of some prerogative. Therefore know that you are of no more value to me than either a sinful woman is, or a Syrophoenician: you will obtain as much as you believe in mercy. And thus every prerogative of the flesh is condemned, wisdom, the righteousness of the flesh, and whatever is of such a kind. Secondly: My hour has not yet come, that is It is for me to determine when help should be given, not for you. I do not help when you wish, but when the glory of the Father demands it. Third, how do they agree, that he denies his hour has come, and yet afterwards he performs the thing? I answer, this word: My hour has not yet come: this does not mean that here there was simply not the hour for performing a miracle, but it means that it is not for a mother or any human to determine the hour. As if he were saying, if it is my hour, that is, if it is the will of the Father, if it will redound to the glory of the Father, it will be done. But if it is not my hour, that is, if it is not the will of the Father, if it will not redound to the glory of the Father, it will not be done. Thus Christ does not indeed deny, but he represses our affection as we seek, and signifies that what we ask must be committed to the will of the Father. And because he will do it afterwards, he signifies that he does not desert anyone, although he may seem not to have heard immediately.

Fourth, therefore it must be seen what the mother does; she is not indignant, she does not despair, but she trusts that he will do what she had asked. She commands to wait, and to do whatever Christ was about to command. Moreover, it is an excellent example of confirming faith, exhibited in praying. First, one must come with confidence in his mercy alone, by no prerogative of our own merit. For he rejects his mother from himself for this reason, that he may make all equal, and thus even to a sinful woman, or the Syrophoenician, etc. Second, what we have asked, it must be committed to his will. As David 2. Kings 15. And as the leper says: Lord, if you will, you can make me clean. Thirdly, to commit to his will, not to despair, but to wait, and to stand in faith, however he may be about to dispose of what you ask. Fourthly, as the mother waits, and commands to hear whatever Christ shall command, so let us wait. And as the Apostle says: Let us persist in prayer, and let us commend our souls to a faithful creator. 1. Peter 4. Fifthly, as he complies here even when he seems to have refused, so let us believe that he will be present with us when necessity shall have demanded it, even if he seems to have abandoned us. Thus says Isaiah 57. I will not be angry to the end. Beyond the allegory, this history teaches many things. First, that weddings are approved by the presence of Christ. Secondly, the concern of Christ for our sustenance etc. You have a similar example 4. Kings 4.

Latin:

Quid mihi tecum est mulier?

Christus matrem reiicit, ut aequet aliis hominibus, cum inquit: Quid mihi tecum est mulier? Quasi diceret, quae mater es, ideo videris alicuius praerogativae merito à me impetratura. Ideo scias te non pluris esse apud me, quàm vel mulier peccatrix est, vel Syrophoenissa: tantum impetrabis, quantum credes misericordiae. Atque ita omnis praerogativa carnis damnatur, sapientia, iustitia carnis, & quicquid tale est. Secundo: Nondum venit hora mea, id est penes me est statuere, quando succurrendum sit, non penes te. Non quando tu vis, sed quando gloria patris postulat, succurro. Tertium, Quomodo autem conveniunt, quod negat horam suam venisse, & tamen postea rem perficit? Respondeo, hoc verbum: Nondum venit hora mea: hoc non vult, quod simpliciter hic non fuerit hora faciendi miraculi, sed vult, non esse matris aut ullius hominis statuere horam. Quasi diceret, si fuerit hora mea, id est si voluntas patris fuerit, si gloriae patris referet, fiet. Sin autem non fuerit hora mea, id est si voluntas patris non fuerit, si gloriae patris non referet, non fiet. Sic non negat quidem Christus, sed affectum nostrum quaerentes reprimit, & significat voluntati patris committendum esse quod petimus. Et quia postea faciet, significat se non deserere quenquam, licet non mox exaudisse videatur.

Quarto, proinde videndum est, quid mater faciat, non indignatur, non desperat, sed confidit facturum quod petiverat. Iubet expectare, & facere quicquid imperaturus esset Christus. Est autem egregium exemplum confirmandae fidei, in orando exhibitum. Primum veniendum est fiducia solius misericordiae eius, nullius praerogativae merito nostro. Nam ideo matrem, à se reiicit, ut omnibus aequet, adeoque etiam mulieri peccatrici, aut Syrophaenissae &c. Secundo, quod petivimus, committendum est eius voluntati. Sicut David 2. Regum 15. Et sicut leprosus ait: Domine, si vis potes me mundare. Tertio, committere eius voluntati, non desperare, sed expectare, & in fide consistere, quomodocunque dispositurus sit quod petis. Quarto, sicut mater expectat, & iubet audire quicquid iusserit Christus, it nos expectemus. Et sicut ait Apostolus: Instemus orationi, & commendemus fideli creatori animas nostras. 1. Petri 4. Quinto, sicut hic obsecundat etiam cum negasse videtur, ita nos credamus cum nobis praesto fore, quando necessitas postulaverit, etiam si derelinquisse videatur. Sic inquit Esaias 57. Non usque ad finem irascar. Praeter allegoriam multa docet haec historia. Primum, nuptias esse probatas praesentia Christi. Secundo, solicitudinem Christi pro victu nostro &c. Simile exemplum habes 4. Regum. 4.

## 3:1 Argument
CHAPTER THREE.

Latin:

CAPUT TERTIUM.

## 3:1
There was, however, a man from the Pharisees, Nicodemus.

This disputation of Christ with Nicodemus is a conflict of the wisdom and righteousness of the flesh with the wisdom and righteousness of the spirit. For reason and Christ differ concerning the manner of justification. Reason thinks it justifies itself if, hearing the law, it imitates its works. Such is Nicodemus, a Pharisee man, that is, a wise man, and without crime. He himself strives by himself to fulfill the law, and simulates the external works of the law, and thinks that he is therefore righteous before God, because he has violated the law by no external work. Such was also Paul before his conversion, which righteousness he writes to Philip. 3. that he counts as dung. And Rom. 7. I was living sometime without I was under the law, that is, ignorant of the law: I thought I was fulfilling the law, when I was doing nothing of the sort. On the contrary, Christ says that justification is regeneration, that is, truly to mortify the flesh, and to be renewed in the spirit. Mortification itself is a sense of death, by which the flesh is confounded and judged. Vivification is, in death, a sense of life, peace, joy of the heart, etc.

Latin:

Erat autem homo ex pharisaeis, Nico-demus.

Haec disputatio Christi cum Nicodemo, est conflictatio sapientiae & iustitiae carnis, cum sapientia & iustitia spiritus. Variant enim ratio & Christus de modo iustificationis. Ratio se iustificare putat, si legem audiens, opera eius imitetur. Talis est Nicodemus, homo pharisaeus, id est vir sapiens, & sine crimine. Ipse per se conatur, ut legem impleat, et externa opera legis simulat, putatque se propterea coram deo iustum esse, quia nullo externo opere violavit legem. Talis erat & ante conversionem Paulus, quam iustitiam suam scribit ad Philip. 3. pro stercore ducere. Et Rom. 7. Ego vivebam aliquando sine lege, id est ignarus legis: putabam me legem praestare, cum nihil minus facerem. Contra, Christus iustificationem dicit esse regenerationem, hoc est vere mortificare carnem, & renovari spiritu. Mortificatio ipsa mortis sensus est, quo confunditur caro & iudicatur. Vivificatio est in morte sensus vitae, pax, gaudium cordis &c.

## 3:2
We know that you have come from God.

Because reason is concluded by so many miracles, and the spirit reveals the righteousness of the flesh, and shows what the difference is between the righteousness of the flesh and of the spirit.

Latin:

Scimus quod à deo venisti.

Quia ratio tot miraculis conclusa est, & spiritus revelat iustitiam carnis, & ostendit quid intersit inter iustitiam carnis & spiritus.

## 3:4
How can a man be born again?

How can nature be abolished? Justification is not the extinction of nature.

Latin:

Quomodo potest homo renasci?

Quomodo potest aboleri natura? Iustificatio non est extinctio naturae.

## 3:7
Do not marvel.

Because the matter is above the grasp of nature, therefore do not deny it, because you do not attain it. It is the Spirit who teaches.

Latin:

Ne mireris.

Quia res supra captum naturae est, ideo ne neges, quia tu non assequeris. Spiritus est qui docet.

## 3:8
The Spirit blows where He wills.

Just as it is not in our power either to moderate the wind or to judge it, so the Holy Spirit is not in our power.

Latin:

Spiritus ubi vult spirat.

Sicut in nostra potestate non est, vel moderare ventum, vel iudicare, ita non est in nostra potestate spiritussanctus.

## 3:10
Are you not that teacher?

That is, do you not teach the holy people, justification, and are you ignorant of what true justification is?

Latin:

Tu ne ille magister?

Id est, tu ne doces populum sanctum, iustificationem, & ignoras quae sit iustificatio vera?

## 3:13
No one has ascended into heaven.

After he taught that it is necessary to be born again, he now shows through whom we are born again, and whose righteousness is imputed to us for salvation, when he says: No one has ascended into heaven. That is, no one's righteousness is accepted and approved before God, except Christ's. Therefore, because he himself descended from heaven, and is in heaven, therefore his righteousness is perfect before God, because he is God etc. who descended from heaven to sanctify the flesh, and to communicate his righteousness to believers. Then he adds how the Son communicates his righteousness, when he says: As Moses lifted up the serpent etc. The history is taken from Numbers 21., where, when the people were being killed by the fiery serpents that were sent in, it was commanded by God that a bronze serpent be erected, which those who looked upon would live. Which history beautifully represents Christ, and all things which we have received through him. The fiery serpent is properly the sting of death, that is sin, which the Apostle calls so 1. Corinthians 15. Where is your sting, O death? The sting of death, however, is sin. The power of sin is the law, that is, the law shows sin, sin known kills.

Thus Psalm 49 A fire shall blaze in his presence, etc. That the figure of a serpent is erected signifies that the figure of sin is erected, that is, Christ, who was made sin for us. This dead serpent represents the death of sin. And just as Christ died for the sake of mortifying sin in our flesh: so it is necessary for us also to die for the sake of mortifying sin in ourselves. But this is the consolation, that we know death is no longer death: because he himself has risen, and has extinguished sin. Thus it must be believed, because by the death of Christ sin has been mortified for us, sin will not be harmful, etc. This is what the prophet says: Death is swallowed up in victory, etc. The serpent healed by being looked upon: Thus Christ heals by being looked upon, that is, by faith. Christ explains this looking when he says: Everyone who believes in him may not perish. And Isaiah: His knowledge shall justify many. And here you see how he attributes nothing to works against the infernal regions and the judgment of God. There those very good works will be for us fiery serpents, because in the purity of good works it will be revealed. In sum, no one's righteousness is accepted by God except the Son's righteousness. The Son communicates his righteousness to those who believe in him, so that they too may be accepted by God. No righteousness of ours will be accepted before God; indeed, our very righteousnesses will be fiery serpents. Therefore faith is to believe this, that Christ is exalted, so that being looked upon he may deliver from death.

Latin:

Nemo ascendit in caelum.

Posteaquam docuit renasci oportere, iam ostendit per quem renascamur, & cuius iustitia nobis imputetur ad salutem, cum inquit: Nemo ascendit in caelum. Id est nullius iustitia coram deo accepta est & probatur, nisi Christi. Ideo quia ipse de caelo descendit, & est in caelo, ideo eius iustitia coram deo perfecta est, quia deus est &c. qui descendit de caelo ad sanctificandam carnem, & communicandam iustitiam suam credentibus. Deinde addit quomodo suam iustitiam communicet filius, cum inquit: Sicut Moyses exaltavit serpentem &c. Historia ex Numerorum 21. accepta est, ubi cum, ignitis serpentibus immissis, necaretur populus, praeceptum est à deo, ut erigeretur aeneus serpens, quem qui aspexissent, victuri essent. Quae historia pulchre repraesentat Christum, & omnia quae per eum accepimus. Ignitus serpens proprie est aculeus mortis, id est peccatum, quod ita vocat Apostolus 1. Corinth. 15. Ubi est mors aculeus tuus? Aculeus autem mortis, peccatum est. Virtus peccati lex, id est lex ostendit peccatum, peccatum cognitum occidit.

Sic Psalmo 49. Ignis in conspectu eius exardescet &c. Quod figura serpentis erigitur, significat erectum esse figuram peccati, id est Christum, qui pro nobis factus est peccatum. Hic mortuus serpens, mortem peccati repraesentat. Et sicut Christus mortuus est propter mortificandum peccatum in carne nostra: ita necesse est & nos mori propter mortificandum peccatum in nobis. Sed haec consolatio est, quod scimus mortem iam non esse mortem: quia ipse surrexit, & peccatum extinxit. Ita credendum est, quia Christi morte peccatum mortificatum est nobis, peccatum non obfuturum &c. Hoc est quod ait propheta: Absorpta est mors in victoria &c. Serpens in aspectu sanabat: Ita Christus aspectu, id est fide sanat. Sic exponit Christus aspectum, cum ait: Omnis qui credit in eum non pereat. Et Esaias: Cognitio eius iustificabit multos. Et hic vides quàm nihil tribuat operibus adversus inferos & iudicium dei. Ibi illa ipsa bona opera erunt nobis igniti serpentes, quia in munditia bonorum operum revelabitur. Summa, nullius iustitia est accepta deo praeter filii iustitiam. Filius iustitiam suam communicat credentibus in se, ut & ipsi sint accepti deo. Iustitia nostra nulla coram deo accepta erit, immo ipsae iustitiae nostrae eruntigniti serpentes. Itaque fides est, hoc credere, quod Christus sit exaltatus, ut conspectus à morte liberet.

## 3:16
So God loved the world.

He adds the reason why it behooves the son to be lifted up, because God loved the world, not because we deserved it. That presumption of the love of God is properly faith; to this Paul inflames us at Rom. 5. God commends his charity to us. And 8. If God is for us, who is against us? Who also did not spare his own son, etc. In this chapter, justification is most plainly described. For first it is proposed that the birth of the flesh is altogether uncleanness, and cannot be justified unless they are reborn. That regeneration is done by water, that is, by mortification, and by the spirit, namely, he who gives life. Then he teaches that to be reborn is the same as to believe in the son, whom it behooves to be exalted like the serpent. For the reborn already know that all their works are sins, therefore they believe that they are justified by the mercy of God, by no merit of their own. Finally, because he said it behooves one to believe in Christ, he therefore disputes more broadly concerning the cause why he was sent into the world. Thus, therefore, he says: Therefore, those who believe in me will be sons: because I was sent so that I might save the world, not so that I might judge it.

Latin:

Sic deus dilexit mundum.

Addit rationem quare filium oporteat erigi, quia deus dilexit mundum, non quia nos meruimus. Ista praesumptio amoris dei, proprie est fides, ad hanc inflammat Paulus ad Rom. 5. Commendat nobis deus charitatem suam. Et 8. Si deus pro nobis, quis contra nos? Qui etiam proprio filio non pepercit &c. Hoc capite planissime descripta est, iustificatio. Primum enim propositum est, nativitatem carnis in totum immunditiam esse, non posse iustificari, nisi renatos. Eam regenerationem fieri aqua, id est mortificatione & spiritu, scilicet qui vivificat. Deinde docet renasci id esse, quod credere in filium, quem exaltari oporteat sicut serpentem. Nam renati iam sciunt omnia sua opera peccata esse, ideo credunt se misericordia dei, nullo suo merito iustificari. Postremo, quia dixit oportere in Christum credi, ideo de causaecur missus sit in mundum latius disputat. Sic ergo dicit: Ideo, qui in me credunt, filii erunt: quia ego missus sum ut salvem mundum, non ut iudicem.

## 3:17
Not that he might judge the world.

How do these things agree with the fifth chapter, where it is said: He has given all judgment to the Son. Also, he has given him power to execute judgment? &c. I answer: Here he says he sent him into the world, wherefore he speaks of his first coming. Before that he does not judge, being about to judge afterwards when he shall have appeared in majesty &c. Also Christ did not come that he might judge, because he came that he might preach the Gospel. But the Law which judges, has already beforehand condemned all.

Latin:

Non ut iudicet mundum.

Quomodo conveniunt haec cum quinto capite, ubi dicitur: Dedit omne iudicium filio. Item potestatem dedit iudicium facere? &c. Respondeo: Hic dicit misit in mundum, quare de primo adventu suo loquitur. Antea non iudicat, iudicaturus postea ubi apparuerit in maiestate &c. Item Christus non venit ut iudicet, quia venit ut praedicet Evangelium. Lex autem quae iudicat, iam antea condemnavit omnes.

## 3:18
He who believes in him is not judged.

Not only is he not condemned, but he is not even judged, that is, faith is simply imputed to him for righteousness, whatever his works may be. For judgment happens when the conscience disputes about its own works, which conflict faith absorbs entirely, which trusts not in any work of its own, but in Christ alone. And in this place it is to be discerned how there is no regard for works, whether good or evil.

But he who does not believe.

Just as he said above that he does not judge believers, that is, to absorb by faith every dispute of the conscience concerning works: So here by unbelief [he absorbs] all disputes of the conscience, and simply to despair with the conscience.

Latin:

Qui credit in eum, non iudicatur.

Non modo non damnatur, sed ne iudicatur quidem, id est simpliciter ei fides imputatur pro iustitia, qualiacunque sint opera. Nam iudicium fit, cum conscientia de operibus suis disputat, quam conflictationem totam absorbet fides, quae non aliquo propriae operae fidat, sed solo Christo. Et hoc loco est cernere, quàm nullus sit operum respectus, sive bonorum, sive malorum.

Qui vero non credit.

Sicut supra dixit, non iudicare credentes, id est absorbere fide omnem disputationem conscientiae de operibus: Ita hic incredulitate omnes disputationes conscientiae, & simpliciter desperare cum conscientia.

## 3:18
Because he does not believe in the name of the only begotten Son of God.

According to that Deuteronomy 13 He who shall refuse to hear, I will be the avenger. As below: It is Moses who accuses you. But this is the condemnation, because the light has come into the world, convicting all the works of men, and showing the righteousness of faith. And men loved darkness more. That is, they did not do penance, they persisted in their own righteousnesses, which they thought to be light. For their works were evil, that is, and so they were unwilling for them to be judged and confounded. For he who does evil hates the light, that is, judgment.

Latin:

Quia non credit in nomine unigeniti filii dei.

Iuxta illud Deuteron. 13. Qui audire noluerit, ego ultor existam. Ut infra: Moyses est, qui accusat vos. Haec est autem condemnatio, quia lux venit in mundum, coarguens omnia opera hominum, & ostendens iustitiam fidei. Et dilexerunt homines magis tenebras. id est non egerunt paenitentiam, duraverunt in suis iustitiis, quas lucem esse putabant. Erant enim eorum mala opera, id est, adeoque nolebant ea iudicari, & confundi. Nam qui male agit, odit lucem, id est iudicium.

## 3:22
And he was staying there with them, and was baptizing.

Below, John explained this passage, that Christ did not baptize, but the disciples. The baptism of Christ is done by the Holy Spirit and fire. How was Christ not yet baptizing? Because he had not yet been glorified. But John and the disciples baptize, because they are ministers, with water, that is, they administer the sign, and preach penance. Christ does not administer the sign, but the reality.

Latin:

Et illic morabatur cum eis, & baptiza-bat.

Infra exposuit hunc locum Ioannes, quod Christus non baptizaverit, sed discipuli. Baptismus Christi fit spiritusancto & igni. Quomodo nondum baptizabat Christus? quia nondum glorificatus erat. Baptizant autem Ioannes & discipuli, quia ministri sunt, aqua, id est administrant signum, & praedicant paenitentiam. Christus non signum, sed rem administrat.

## 3:27
A man cannot.

I cannot take upon myself a greater honor, another office, than that which has been committed from heaven.

Latin:

Non potest homo.

Non possum mihi sumere ampliorem honorem, aliud officium, quàm quod è caelo commissum est.

## 3:29
He who has the bride.

Christ has the bride, that is, he communicates his the justice of the bride, and the bride her sins to Christ, and she is saved, because she believes in him. He is not saved who believes in the prophets. Because of the voice of the bridegroom, that is the Gospel, or the remission of sins.

Latin:

Qui habet sponsam.

Christus habet sponsam, id est communicat suam iustitiam sponsae, & sponsa sua peccata Christo, & salvatur, quia in hunc credit. Non salvatur qui in prophetas credit. Propter vocem sponsi, id est Evangelium, seu remissionem peccatorum.

## 3:31
He who is of the earth.

It is a general sentiment, signifying that Christ must be from heaven, because he announces the secret counsel of the father concerning the remission of sins, as above: No one has ever seen God. Therefore no one will know the father, except he to whom the son has revealed him. Those who are of the earth do not know the father: but he knows, who came from heaven, and he to whom he has revealed him.

Latin:

Qui est de terra.

Generalis sententia est, significans Christum oportere de caelo esse, quia secretum consilium patris de remissione peccatorum annunciet, ut supra: Deum nemo vidit unquam. Proinde nemo patrem noscet, nisi cui filius revelaverit. Qui de terra sunt, non norunt patrem: novit autem is, qui de caelo venit, & cui ille revelaverit.

## 3:33
He who has received his testimony. signs.

To sign or to testify that the Gospel is true, is to have the testimony of our heart that the Gospel is true. That testimony, however, is the holy spirit, crying: Abba father.

Latin:

Qui acceperit eius testimo. signa.

Signare vel testificare, quod Evangelium verum sit, est habere testimonium cordis nostri, quod verum sit Evangelium. Illud autem testimonium est spiritus sanctus, clamans: Abba pater.

## 4:1 Argument
CHAPTER FOUR.

The sum of this history pertains to the calling of the gentiles, concerning which Christ says elsewhere Matth. 8. Many shall come from the east and west etc. Furthermore Wherever the calling of the Gentiles is treated, this must always be kept in view, that we may raise up consciences. Because when he calls the Gentiles a people without God, and saves them without any works, by sheerest mercy: let us also constantly believe that although we are sinners, and a people without God, we shall obtain mercy. This is what is said in Hosea chapter one: It shall be in the place where it shall be said to them, you are not my people, etc.

Latin:

CAPUT QUARTUM.

Summa historiae huius ad vocationem gentium pertinet, de qua aliâs dicit Christus Matth. 8. Multi ab oriente & occidente venient &c. Porrò ubicunque de vocatione gentium agitur, semper hoc spectandum est, ut erigamus conscientias. Quod cum gentes, populum sine deo vocat, & salvat sine ullis operibus, merissima misericordia: constanter & nos credamus quanquam peccatores, & populum sine deo, misericordiam consecuturos. Hoc est quod Osee primo dicitur: Erit in loco ubi dicetur eis, non populus meus &c.

## 4:6
But Jesus being wearied.

Next, the individual parts of the history must be considered. First, because the calling of the Gentiles is treated, therefore Christ is described as being as weak as possible, first fleeing, then wearied, thirdly hungering, and thirsting. So that by that image he may console the weakness of a people without works, so that we may acknowledge that we have such a high priest, who could sympathize with our infirmity, having been tempted in all things.

Latin:

Iesus autem fatigatus.

Deinde singulae partes historiae consyderandae sunt. Primum, quia de vocatione gentium agitur, ideo describitur Christus quàm potuit maxime imbecillis, Primum fugiens, deinde fatigatus, tertio esuriens, & sitiens. Ut ea imagine consoletur imbecillitatem populi sine operibus, ut agnoscamus talem nos habere pontificem, qui compati posset infirmitati nostrae tentatum per omnia.

## 4:9
How do you, being a Jew.

It was commanded to the Jews Deuteron. 7. not to enter into covenants, or to join marriages with the Gentiles in their land. The Jews extended that law to the Samaritans as well. But that it was not permitted to eat or drink with them seems to have been a human tradition, which Christ here violates. Therefore circumstances must be considered. For Christ does not violate the law or traditions, unless necessity or charity compels it. As in Matthew 12 when the apostles plucked the ears of grain, and Matthew 8 when the leper is touched. Therefore, in keeping or violating the traditions of men, regard must be had for charity. For there are those who, by violating them, injure not only charity but also faith in their neighbors, and cause our good to be blasphemed. Rom. 14 These are they who defecate in the Israelite camp and bring infamy upon the Gospel. For since external ordinances are indifferent, they are good creatures of God, and works of reason like shoemaking and the like: yet they are to be received in such a way that neither justice nor injustice is placed in them, and they are not to be treated with contempt. The Pope treats the creature with contempt when he forbids it to be eaten. You do an injury to the creature, namely to a civil and external ordinance, when you violate it out of mere contempt, which is mere impiety.

Latin:

Quomodo tu cum Iudaeus sis.

Iudaeis praeceptum fuit Deuteron. 7. ne foedera inirent, vel connubia coniungerent cum gentibus in terra sua. Eam legem Iudaei ad Samaritanos quoque retulerunt. Sed quod nec edere, nec bibere cum illis licuerit, humana traditio fuisse videtur, quam Christus hic violat. Propterea consyderandae sunt circumstantiae. Non enim viclat Christus legem vel traditiones, nisi cogat vel necessitas, vel charitas. Ut Matthaei 12. cum apostoli spicas vellerent, & Matthaei 8. cum tangitur leprosus. Proinde in servandis vel violandis hominum traditionibus, habenda est ratio charitatis. Sunt enim qui violando non modo charitatem, sed & fidem in proximis laedunt, & faciunt ut blasphemetur bonum nostrum. Rom. 14. Hi sunt, qui in castra Israëlitica cacant, & infamant Evangelium. Nam cum externae ordinationes mediae sunt, sunt creaturae dei bonae, & opera rationis sicut sutoria, & similes: sic sunt tamen accipiendae, ut in eis neque iustitia, neque iniustitia ponatur, non contumelia afficiendae. Papa contumelia afficit creaturam, cum vetat edi. Tu contumeliam facis creaturae, scilicet ordinatione civili & externa, cum violas mero contemptu, qui est mera impietas.

## 4:10
If you knew the gift of God.

The sum of justification is the knowledge of Christ, according to that saying in Isaiah 53 By his knowledge he shall justify many, nor is the Gospel anything other than the word about Christ, from which Christ can be known, Roman. 1 and 10. Therefore here Christ first explains, if you knew the gift of God. It is the knowledge of Christ, faith, by which it is believed that Christ is the savior. Concerning this knowledge it has been treated above: The world did not know him.

Latin:

Si scires donum dei.

Summa iustificationis est, cognitio Christi, iuxta illud Esaiae 53. Scientia eius iustificabit multos, nec aliud est Evangelium, quàm sermo de Christo, è quo cognosci Christus possit, Roman. 1. & 10. Propterea hic Christus primum exponit, si scires donum dei. Est autem cognitio Christi, fides, qua creditur Christum esse salvatorem. De hac cognitione supra actum est: Mundus eum non cognovit.

## 4:11
Sir, you have nothing with which to draw.

When reason hears the Gospel, and that righteousness and life are promised through Christ, what else does it think than what Paul says: that the Gospel is foolishness to the Gentiles? Reason saw Christ being crucified and dying, wherefore it cannot believe him to be the savior. So likewise when the flesh is afflicted, it believes nothing less than that the cross is the savior etc. Thus we see here the little woman answering Christ ridiculously. Since indeed she may not have a vessel, she will not draw water. As if she were saying, how will you give me salvation, which you yourself do not have?

Latin:

Domine neque in quo haurias, habes.

Ratio cum audit Evangelium, & per Christum promitti iustitiam & vitam, quid aliud cogitat quàm quod Paulus ait: Evangelium stultitiam esse gentibus? Ratio vidit Christum crucifigi & mori, quare non potest credere eum esse salvatorem. Sic item cum affligitur caro, nihil minus credit quàm crucem esse salvatorem &c. Ita videmus hic mulierculam ridicule respondere Christo. Siquidem vas non habeat, non hausturum aquam. Quasi diceret, quomodo mihi dabis salutem, quam ipse non habes?

## 4:12
Are you greater than our father etc.

First she simply rejected as ridiculous what Christ had said. Now she also fights with an argument, as if she were saying. What need is there of other water, since Jacob gave this one? As the Jews were arguing: What need is there of other doctrine, since we have Moses? And now what need is there of other doctrine, since we have the holy fathers?

Latin:

Nunquid tu maior es patre &c.

Primum simpliciter reiecit tanquam ridiculum, quod Christus dixerat. Nunc etiam argumento pugnat, quasi diceret. Quid opus est alia aqua, cum hanc Iacob dederit? ut Iudaei argumentabantur: Quid opus est alia doctrina, cum habeamus Moysen? Et nunc quid opus est alia doctrina, cum habeamus sanctos patres?

## 4:16
Go call your husband.

Thus far Christ taught the Gospel, now he proves it by a miracle. For signs are testimonies of the word, Mark last chapter. He mentions, however, that it is done especially because the Gospel is the preaching of repentance. Therefore, sin is shown to the woman. But now, let others see what mystery there is in this, that he orders her to call her husband, etc.

Latin:

Vade voca virum tuum.

Hactenus docuit Christus Evangelium, nunc probat miraculo. Sunt enim signa testimonia verbi, Marci ultimo. Meminit autem potissimum fieri, quia Evangelium est praedicatio paenitentiae. Ideo peccatum mulieri ostenditur. Nunc autem quid sit mysterii in hoc quod virum iubet accersere &c. alii viderint.

## 4:20
Our fathers worshipped in this mountain, etc.

He joins the miracle with the preaching of repentance. Having heard the miracle, when she now begins to believe, she is immediately questioned about the worship of God and about righteousness. And since the Jews alone seem to be the people of God, it is asked whether the Gospel pertains also to the Samaritans. For this is the first question of the Gentiles and of all sinners, whether they are saved without the law, without works. These, being ignorant of the Gospel, because they do not believe that they will obtain salvation without works, live in a certain perpetual despair.

Latin:

Patres nostri in monte hoc &c.

Coniungit miraculum cum praedicatione paenitentiae. Audito miraculo, cum iam credere incipit, statim interrogatur de cultu dei, & de iustitia. Et cum Iudaei soli videantur esse populus dei, quaeritur an etiam ad Samaritanos pertineat Evangelium.
