Haec enim prima quaestio est gentium & omnium peccatorum, utrum sine lege, sine operibus salventur. Hi ignorantes Evangelii, quia non credunt se sine operibus salutem consecuturos, vivunt in perpetua quadam desperatione.

## 4:21
Woman, believe me, etc.

After the woman's conscience is confounded, which is the beginning of repentance, she soon asks about righteousness, namely whether God approves only the righteousness of the law, or also some other, since the fathers also pleased God without the law. Thus are all confounded consciences accustomed to ask whether God does not also approve of some other righteousness beyond the righteousness of the law, because we have lost this. For cursed is he who does not remain in all things &c. And this is the question which he proposes, when he asks where it is necessary to worship, that is, whether that righteousness of the law, which is preached at Jerusalem, is alone acceptable to God, or whether besides this there is some other &c. Christ answers: Woman, believe me, that is, hold this for righteousness, which I preach. Build upon my word, this is the righteousness of God which I preach, the righteousness of the law is not the righteousness of God.

Latin:

Mulier crede mihi &c.

Posteaquam mulieris conscientia confusa est, quod initium est paenitentiae, mox de iustitia interrogat, scilicet utrum solum legis iustitiam probet deus, an & aliam quandam, quando patres etiam sine lege placuerunt deo. Sic solent omnes confusae conscientiae quaerere, an ne etiam aliam iustitiam quandam probet deus, praeter legis iustitiam, quod hanc amiserimus. Maledictus enim, qui non permanet in omnibus &c. Et haec quaestio est quam proponit, cum interrogat, ubi oporteat adorari, id est, sit ne illa legis iustitia, quae Hierosolymis praedicatur, solo deo accepta, an ne praeter hanc alia quaedam &c. Respondet Christus: Mulier, crede mihi, id est hoc habe pro iustitia, quod ego praedico. Aedifica in verbum meum, haec iustitia dei est quam ego praedico, non est iustitia dei iustitia legis.

## 4:22
You worship what you do not know, we worship &c.

Thus he first consoles the woman, and infers that the righteousness and prayer of the Jerusalemites, that is, the righteousness of the law, is not that which justifies before God. Afterwards he begins to distinguish the Jews from the Gentiles. For when he rejects the righteousness of the law, it can immediately be asked whether the Jews provide nothing for the Gentiles. Thus Paul asks in Romans the third: What then is more to the Jew? Christ answers: You worship what you do not know. That is, you have neither sign nor word concerning God. We worship what we know, that is, we have both sign and word concerning God. Salvation is from the Jews. That is, to the Jews were made the promises concerning Christ, as it is said in Rom. 3.: Entrusted they have the prophecies of God, etc. There is therefore a distinction between the Gentiles and the Jews. To the Jews were made the promises concerning Christ; to the Gentiles they were not made. The Jews have the testimony of God; the Gentiles do not have it. But regarding the righteousness of God, there is no prerogative for either, as Paul also says Rom. 3. What then, do we excel them? Not at all. For we have already charged both Jews and Greeks that all are under sin, that is, that neither do good works save anyone, nor do evil works condemn anyone. But faith saves, and unbelief condemns. And these are of the divine glory and the grace of God, etc. Fourth, if there is no righteousness of the law, what then is the righteousness of God? Christ answers: The hour is coming, that is, when the Gospel will be preached, and now is: because the Gospel is already being preached, when the true worshipers will worship the Father in spirit and in truth of spirit, that is, not through the powers of the flesh. In truth, that is, not in hypocrisy, of which kind are all good intentions and good works which are done by the powers of nature, all of which are mere lies. But the Spirit, renewing the heart, kindles a new light, a new life, that is, the heart with the knowledge of God, with faith, and with the fear of God, and with hatred of the flesh and of all carnal concupiscence: these things are done by the Spirit; they cannot be done by the flesh. Therefore He says: The true worshipers will worship in spirit, etc. This is the new life of the heart, not the dead letter, not cold human intentions, but they are true.

Latin:

Vos adoratis quod nescitis, nos ado-ramus &c.

Sic primum consolatur mulierem, & subinfert iustitiam & orationem Hierosolymorum, hoc est iustitiam legis non esse eam, quae coram deo iustificet. Postea incipit distinguere Iudaeos à gentibus. Nam cum iustitiam legis reiicit, quaeri statim potest nihil ne praestent Iudaei gentibus. Sic Paulus quaerit Rom. tertio: Quid igitur amplius Iudaeo est? Respondit Christus: Vos adoratis quod nescitis. id est vos de deo nec signum, nec verbum habetis. Nos adoramus, quod scimus, id est nos de deo & signum, & verbum habemus. Salus ex Iudaeis est. id est ad Iudaeos factae sunt promissiones de Christo, ut Rom. 3. dicitur: Credita sunt illis vaticinia dei &c. Est itaque discrimen gentium & Iudaeorum. Ad Iudaeos factae sunt promissiones de Christo, ad gentes non sunt factae. Iudaei testimonium dei habent, gentes non habent. Sed ad iustitiam dei neutris aliqua praerogativa est, ut & Paulus ait Rom. 3. Quid ergo, praecellimus eos? nequaquam. Causati enim sumus iam ante & Iudaeos, & Graecos, omnes sub peccato esse, id est neminem neque bona opera salvant, neque damnant mala. Sed salvat fides, damnat incredulitas. Atque haec sunt divinae gloriae & gratiae dei &c. Quarto, Si nulla est iustitia legis, quae ergo est iustitia dei? Respondet Christus: Venit hora, id est cum praedicabitur Evangelium, & nunc est: quia iam praedicatur Evangelium, quando veri adoratores adorabunt patrem in spiritu, & veritate spiritus, id est non per vires carnis. Veritate, id est non hypocrisi, cuiusmodi sunt omnia bona proposita, & bona opera quae naturae viribus fiunt, quae omnia sunt mera mendacia. Spiritus autem renovans cor, novum lumen, novam vitam, hoc est cor cognitione dei, fide, & metu dei, & odio carnis, & universae concupiscentiae carnalis, accendit: haec spiritu fiunt, carne fieri non possunt. Ideo dicit: Veri adoratores adorabunt in spiritu &c. Haec nova cordis vita est, non mortua litera, non frigidae intentiones humanae, sed vera sunt.

## 4:24
The Spirit is God.

For the natural man does not perceive the things that are of the Spirit, that is, he does not know the Spirit, unless by the Spirit.

Latin:

Spiritus est deus.

Nam animalis homo non percipit ea quae sunt spiritus, id est spiritum non cognoscit, nisi spiritus.

## 4:25
I know that the Messiah is coming.

That the little woman says she knows that Christ is to come, pertains to the fact that Christ was promised also to the Gentiles, although the promises were made to the Jews alone.

Latin:

Scio quod Messias venit.

Quod muliercula dicit se scire Christum venturum, eò pertinet quod & gentibus promissus est Christus, quamquam ad solos Iudaeos factae sint promissiones.

## 4:27
The disciples came and were wondering.

That the disciples were wondering that he was speaking with the woman, figures here that the Jews also were wondering that the Gentiles were being taken up. And that the disciples do not understand him speaking with the woman, signifies the Jews compelled by scripture, and by the signs of God, to preach the Gospel to the Gentiles.

Latin:

Venerunt discipuli & mirabantur.

Quod discipuli mirabantur cum muliere colloquentem, figurat hic, quod & Iudaei mirabantur gentes assumi. Et quod non comprehendunt discipuli cum muliere colloquentem, significat Iudaeos coactos scriptura, et signis dei, ut praedicarent Evangelium gentibus.

## 4:31
Meanwhile the disciples were asking him, etc.

Because Christ had already preached to the Samaritan woman, and the disciples were wondering that he was preaching also to the Gentiles, he teaches here that the time is pressing to preach the Gospel to the whole world: but after he himself has suffered, therefore he speaks first of his passion: I have food to eat, which you do not know. And then. My food is, that I may do the will of him who sent me, that I may finish his work. Which I accept in this way, as Lucae 12.

It is said: I have a baptism to be baptized with, and how am I constrained until it be accomplished? Secondly, he adds that the time for preaching the Gospel in the whole world is at hand, because the harvest is already white and has ripened, that is, the time predetermined in the scriptures is at hand, since even the nations were already beginning to believe. As he says elsewhere: From the days of John until now the kingdom of heaven suffers violence. Thirdly, he speaks of the office and reward of the apostles, that the apostles are only reapers, eating the food of grace, because they did not understand that it behooved Christ to suffer.

Latin:

Interea rogabant eum discipuli &c.

Quia iam Samaritanae mulierculae praedicaverat Christus, & discipuli mirabantur, quod & gentibus praedicaret, hic docet instare tempus praedicandi Evangelium toti mundo: sed postquam ipse passus sit, ideo de passione sua primum loquitur: Ego cibum manducare habeo, quem vos nescitis. Et deinde. Cibus meus est, ut faciam voluntatem eius qui misit me, ut perficiam opus eius. Quod ego sic accipio, ut Lucae 12.

dicitur: Baptismo habeo baptizari, & quomodo coarctor usque dum perficiatur? Secundo subiicit, instare tempus praedicandi Evangelium in toto mundo, quod seges iam albescat & maturuerit, id est tempus praefinitum in scripturis instet, cum iam etiam gentes inciperent credere. Sicut aliâs dicit: A diebus Ioannis usque nunc regnum caelorum vim patitur. Tertio, de officio & mercede apostolorum loquitur, apostolos messores tantum esse, cibum manducare gratiae, quia non intelligebant oportere Christum pati.

## 4:34
That I may do the will.

Concerning the passion you will observe this most especially, that just as the passion of Christ is the will and work of the Father: so the passion of all who are in Christ is the will and work of the Father. Thus Paul says that he fills up that which was wanting of the afflictions of Christ. Therefore, just as Christ was glorified through the cross, so also those who are in Christ will be glorified. There are yet four months from spring, that is, the bodily harvest is far off. But the spiritual harvest is near, that is, this very time for preaching the Gospel is already here, because from the days of John the kingdom of heaven suffers violence. Regions, that is, peoples. He commands to lift up the eyes, namely, the spiritual ones.

Latin:

Ut faciam voluntatem.

De passione hoc potissimum observabis, quod sicut Christi passio, voluntas & opus patris est: ita omnium qui sunt, Christi passio, voluntas & opus patris est. Sic Paulus dicit se implere id quod deerat afflictionibus Christi. Ergo sicut Christus per crucem glorificatus est, ita glorificabuntur & ii qui in Christo sunt. Adhuc quatuor menses sunt à vere, id est procul abest messis corporalis. Sed messis spiritualis prope est, hoc est iam illud ipsum tempus praedicandi Evangelium est, quia à diebus Ioannis regnum caelorum vim patitur. Regiones, id est populos. Oculos, scilicet spirituales levare iubet.

## 4:38
I have sent you to reap that whereon you bestowed no labor. &c.

He distinguishes the apostles from the prophets, when he calls the prophets sowers, and the apostles reapers: because through the prophets the word was first received, the apostles only execute it. And thus he distinguishes the new testament from the old, that the new is the execution of the promises made in the old testament. But that they labored, this is what is said to the Hebrews 11.: All these, being approved by the testimony of faith, received not the promise, that is, the execution of the promises of the new testament was delayed. In the old testament there was not public remission of sins. For peace was not announced, which is announced in the new testament, therefore they labored. In the new testament peace is now announced, and the end of labors, as it is said 21. Jeremiah: Their soul shall be as a watered garden, and they shall hunger no more &c. We only reap what others have sown. Likewise they preserved the promises themselves before Christ was exhibited, through many afflictions they died for the promises, when they had not yet seen Christ glorified. We now see exhibited what he says, so that we doubt nothing: but that just as he himself was raised from the dead and glorified, so also we shall be raised and glorified.

In justification it is necessary first to preach the word, according to Rom. 10. Faith is from hearing &c. Miracles are witnesses of that word, this is what the little woman in this place preaches to her fellow citizens, and what she adds that many believed because of this word of the little woman: Because she told me all things that I have done.

Latin:

Ego misi vos metere, quod vos non labo. &c.

Distinguit apostolos à prophetis, cum prophetas seminatores vocat, & apostolos messores: quia per prophetas primum verbum est acceptum, apostoli tantum exequuntur. Atque ita novum testamentum discernit à veteri, quod novum sit executio promissionum factarum in veteri testamento. Quod autem laboraverint, hoc est quod ad Hebraeos 11. dicitur: Hi omnes testimonio fidei probati, non acceperunt repromissionem, id est promissionum novi testamenti executio dilata est. Veteri testamento non fuit publica remissio peccatorum. Non enim annunciabatur pax, quae novo testamento annunciatur, ideo illi laboraverunt. Novo testamento iam annunciata pax est, & finis laborum, sicut 21. Hieremiae dicitur: Erit anima eorum quasi hortus irriguus, & ultra non esurient &c. Nos tantum demetimus quod alii seminaverunt. Item ipsae promissiones ante Christum exhibitum conservaverunt, per multas afflictiones mortui sunt pro promissionibus, cum nondum vidissent glorificatum Christum. Nos iam videmus exhibitum quod ait, ut nihil dubitemus: quin sicut ipse resuscitatus est à mortuis & glorificatus, ita & nos resuscitemur & glorificemur.

In iustificatione necesse est primum praedicare verbum, iuxta Rom. 10. Fides ex auditu est &c. Miracula sunt illius verbi testes, hoc est quod muliercula hoc loco civibus suis praedicat, et quod subiicit multos credidisse propter hoc verbum mulierculae: Quia dixit mihi omnia quae feci.

## 4:42
Now we do not believe because of your speech.

It is necessary what she adds: Now we do not believe because of your speech, that is the miracle which was done with you: because miracles which are done outside of us do not yet certify hearts concerning the will of God toward us, as we saw above in Nicodemus. But those which are done with us, and in our hearts, those are what certify the hearts, such as those of which he says Psalm. 30. How great is the multitude of thy sweetness O Lord? And 55. In whatever day I shall call upon thee, behold I have known that thou art my God. These things however are done when through the cross he teaches us to know and believe him. Just as it is also said Rom. 5.: Tribulation worketh patience, patience probation, that is the knowledge of the divine will toward us, probation hope. For they cannot hope, or stand by faith, whose hearts are uncertain concerning the will toward them. He teaches the same at Romans 12. Be reformed in the newness of your sense, that you may prove, that is, that you may know what is the good will of God, etc. Thus here they believe no longer because of another's miracle, but because they themselves have seen and heard. There is no Christian life where there is a lack of certain knowledge of God and of faith.

Latin:

Iam non propter tuam loquelam cre-dimus.

Necessarium est quod addit: Iam non propter tuam loquelam, id est miraculum quod tecum factum: quia miracula quae extra nos fiunt, nondum certificant corda de voluntate dei erga nos, sicut supra vidimus in Nicodemo. Quae autem fiunt nobiscum, & in cordibus nostris, illa sunt quae certificant corda, qualia sunt, de quibus dicit Psalm. 30. Quàm magna multitudo dulcedinis tuae domine? Et 55. In quacunque die invocavero te, ecce cognovi quia deus meus es. Haec autem fiunt quando per crucem docet nos se cognoscere & credere. Quomodo & Rom. 5. dicitur: Tribulatio patientiam, patientia probationem, id est cognitionem divinae voluntatis erga nos, probatio spem. Non possunt enim sperare, aut fide consistere, quorum corda de voluntate erga se incerta sunt. Idem docet ad Romanos 12. Reformamini in novitate sensus vestri, ut probetis, id est ut cognoscetis quae sit voluntas dei bona &c. Ita hic credunt non iam propter alienum miraculum, sed quia ipsi viderunt & audierunt. Non est Christiana vita, ubi cortali certa cognitione dei & fide vacua est.

## 4:44
For Jesus himself bore witness, etc.

Here John breaks off the history, for he signifies that Jesus had returned to Nazareth, and had spent a few days there. And from there he had gone away again into the neighboring cities of Galilee. He deserted his fatherland for this reason, because he was not heard there. Furthermore, this history is described in Luke 4. John also reviewed this in this place concerning the calling of the Gentiles. Fatherland signifies the Jews, so that he clearly says nothing else here than what he said above: His own received him not. And when I say the Jews, I understand all those who wish to be saved not by faith, but by their own merits. For the people of the law are rejected for this reason, so that our consciences may know for certain that Christ came to save sinners.

The faith of the nobleman is that he prays to Christ to heal his son. The unbelief of the nobleman is this, that he commands Christ to come down, that is, he does not think he can heal by his word. Also that he commands him to come down before his son dies. For the nobleman does not believe that Christ is also the lord of death. Therefore Christ censures his unbelief, when he says: Unless signs and wonders &c. Just as Matthew 12 & 16 he says: A wicked and adulterous generation seeks a sign. But what is it to seek a sign? It is to wish to be certified in another way than through the word. Such are these consciences, when they hear the remission of sins being preached, they think they would indeed believe, if they were made certain by some sensible sign. And they seek a sign from God, because they do not believe the word. But God sent his word, and healed them. Item: He who hears you, hears me. Which he says for this reason, that consciences may be certain that it is the word, which has been written and preached through the apostles. Item 1 Corinthians 1 Paul says: The Jews seek a sign. That is, the Jews because they are not overcome by a sensible sign, which the faith of the Gospel sanctifies, they do not wish to believe, and yet they think themselves to be justified through all things. Of which opinion they have neither a sign, nor the word of God, but they feel thus through the blindness of the flesh without the divine word and sign. And what Christ adds elsewhere: No other sign shall be given to this generation except the sign of Jonah the prophet. That is, among all the signs that are made, none is efficacious for salvation, except the sign of Jonah, that is the death and resurrection of Christ, by which he works the death of the flesh, and the life of the spirit.

Latin:

Ipse enim Iesus testimonium perhi-buit &c.

Hic Ioannes abrumpit historiam, significat enim Iesum rediisse in Nazareth, atque ibi pauculos dies versatum esse. Atque inde rursum abiisse in vicinas urbes Galilaeae. Ideo autem patriam deservisse, quod ibi non audiretur. Porrò haec historia descripta est Lucae 4. Recensuit autem & hoc loco Ioannes de vocatione gentium. Patria Iudaeos significat, ut planè non aliud hic dicat, quàm quod supra: Sui eum non receperunt. Et cum Iudaeos dico, intelligo omnes eos qui non fide, sed suis meritis volunt salvari. Nam ideo reiicitur populus legis, ut certo sciant conscientiae nostrae venisse Christum, ut salvos faceret peccatores.

Fides reguli est, quod orat Christum, ut medeatur filium. Incredulitas reguli haec est, quod iubet descendere Christum, id est non putat verbo curare posse. Item quod iubet descendere priusquam moriatur filius. Non enim credit regulus, etiam mortis dominum Christum esse. Taxat itaque incredulitatem eius Christus, cum inquit: Nisi signa et prodigia &c. Quomodo Matthaei 12. & 16. ait: Generatio prava & adultera signum quaerit. Quid est autem signum quaerere? Est velle certificari alio modo, quàm per verbum. Sunt huiusmodi conscientiae, cum audiunt praedicare remissionem peccatorum, credituros se quidem cogitant, si aliquo signo sensibili certi redderentur. Et à deo siguum quaerunt, quia verbo non credunt. Misit autem deus verbum suum, & sanavit eos. Item: Qui vos audit, me audit. Quod ideo dicit, ut certae sint conscientiae id esse verbum, quod per apostolos scriptum & praedicatum est. Item 1. Corinth. 1. ait Paulus: Iudaei signum quaerunt. Id est, Iudaei quia non vincuntur signo sensibili, quod fides Evangelii sanctificet, credere nolunt, & tamen per omnia iustificari se putant. Cuius opinionis neque signum, neque verbum dei habent, sed ita per caecitatem carnis sentiunt sine verbo divino & signo. Et quod aliâs Christus addit: Non dabitur huic generationi aliud signum nisi signum Ionae prophetae. Id est, inter omnia signa quae fiunt, nullum est efficax ad salutem, nisi Ionae signum, id est mors & resurrectio Christi, quo mortem carnis, & vitam spiritus operatur.

## 4:48
Unless signs and wonders &c.

As if he were saying, you command that I descend, when nevertheless I heal by a word. Therefore I am compelled to perform signs for your unbelief.

Latin:

Nisi signa & prodigia &c.

Quasi dicat, iubes ut descendam, cum tamen verbo curem. Itaque cogor incredulitati vestrae signa facere.

## 4:50
And he believed this word.

After unbelief is rebuked, an example of the power of faith is exhibited, when the nobleman departs and believes, whereby it comes to pass that the son recovers.

Latin:

Et credidit sermoni huic.

Posteaquam reprehensa est incredulitas, exhibetur exemplum potentiae fidei, cum regulus abiens credit, quo fit ut filius revalescat.

## 4:52
He inquired therefore the hour.

Finally, that he asks about the hour pertains first to the declaration of faith. Then to the provoking of the whole household to faith. For he himself indeed believed because of Christ's word that it would come to pass that the son would not die. For John testifies that he had believed. But when and how the son would recover, he did not know, content that Christ had said: Your son lives. In which he teaches what the nature of faith is: namely, to conceive confidence in God, that he is about to save us: how, when, or through what afflictions, pertains nothing to us. Believe, and you will be saved, even if all creatures were to oppose all evils. Thus Abraham goes out from the promised land, not knowing whither he is to go. Thus he awaits the promised posterity, although he was commanded to kill his son. Thus Moses leads out the children of Israel, although he does not know how God is going to defend them against the force of King Pharaoh. When he stood at the Red Sea, not knowing where they were to escape to, he nevertheless commands them to stand still, that they would see the great works of God, etc.

Latin:

Sciscitatus est ergo horam.

Postremo quod interrogat de hora, primum pertinet ad fidei declarationem. Deinde ad provocandam universam familiam ad fidem. Nam ipse quidem credebat propter Christi verbum futurum, ut non moreretur filius. Ioannes enim testatur eum credidisse. Sed quando & quomodo revalesceret filius, nesciebat, contentus dixisse Christum: Filius tuus vivit. In quo docet quae sit fidei natura: nempe fidutiam de deo concipere, quod servaturus nos sit: quomodo, quando, aut per quas afflictiones, nihil ad nos pertinet. Crede, & salvus eris, etiam si omnes creaturae omnia mala obniterentur. Sic Abraham egreditur terram promissam, nesciens quorsum sit eundum. Sic expectat posteritatem promissam, quanquam filium iuberetur occidere. Sic Moyses educit filios Israël, quanquam nesciat quomodo sit deus eos adversus regis vim Pharaonis defensurus. Cum staret ad mare rubrum, nesciens quò evasuri essent, iubet tamen consistere, visuros magnalia dei &c.

## 5:1 Argument
CHAPTER FIVE.

The SUM of this chapter is this. Christ gives a reason for the violated Sabbath, but he does the same above in other Evangelists, yet he argues much differently above the example of David, who violated the law concerning the showbread in necessity. He also argues the example of the priests, because they violate the law on the Sabbath, which the law itself also requests. From which you will understand that not even by keeping the law is it kept. Here he argues the authority of the Father, and teaches that the Father and he work at the same time. Wherefore he who judges his work, judges also that of his Father. Now just as the authority of the law is from God, so it is also to be violated by divine authority. Briefly, what he says in other Evangelists: For the Son of man is Lord, even of the Sabbath. This is explained in this place, why and how he is Lord of the Sabbath. And from this place the reason for Christian liberty is to be sought.

Latin:

CAPUT QUINTUM.

SUMMA huius capitis haec est. Christus reddit rationem violati sabbati, facit autem idem supra in aliis Evangelistis, sed multo aliter supra exemplum David causatur, qui legem de panibus propositionis in necessitate violavit. Causatur & exemplum sacerdotum, quod sabbato violent legem, id quod & lex rogat. Unde intelliges, ne servando quidem legem servari. Hic causatur autoritatem patris, docetque simul operari patrem, & se. Quare qui suum opus iudicat, iudicare & patris eius. Iam sicut legis autoritas à deo est, ita & divina autoritate violari. Breviter quod in aliis Evangelistis inquit: Dominus enim est filius hominis, etiam sabbati. Id hoc loco exponitur, cur & quomodo sit dominus sabbati. Estque ex hoc loco petenda ratio libertatis Christianae.

## 5:2
Having five porches.

By the five porches he signifies the law, which since it does not justify, nay rather only works wrath, holds the languishing. The pool is the Gospel, and thus baptism. The water is troubled, because the Gospel is the word of the cross. The bearers are the doctors of the sick. That only the first were healed, seems to me to pertain to election. Christ, by his word effecting that which the water was accustomed to do, declares that his word is that which the water signified. Whence also you see in this text what liberty is. First, in it is liberty, that the sick are healed, that is, that sins are freely forgiven: because Christ, having been made a curse for us, took away the curse. Secondly, those who have been healed are led by the Spirit, so that they now acknowledge God, believe in God, fear God, which they could not do before. Thirdly, he performs external works according to the judgment of the Spirit, mortifies the flesh, serves his neighbor, and freely, that is, with a cheerful conscience, because righteousness is not first to be earned by those works, but has already been given. He also uses ceremonies and human ordinances, as charity demands. In all things, however, that he does, he has a reason for his deed, which he pleads here, when he says: He who healed me, commanded me to take up my bed. That is, he does whatever he does out of faith. And he keeps the rule of Paul, Rom. 14. Everything that is not of faith is sin. He who made me whole &c. And thus I do out of faith what I do.

Latin:

Quinque porticus habens.

Quinque porticibus legem significat, quae cum non iustificet, immo iram tantum operetur, tenet languentes. Piscina Evangelium, adeóque baptismum. Aqua turbida, quod Evangelium sit sermo crucis. Baiuli, aegrorunsunt doctores. Quod soli primi sanabantur, mihi videtur ad electionem pertinere. Christus verbo id efficiens, quod solebat aqua, declarat, verbum suum id esse, quod significabat aqua. Unde & quae sit libertas, vides in hoc textu. Primum in eo est libertas, quod sanantur aegri, hoc est quod gratis peccata dimittuntur: quia Christus factus maledictum pro nobis, sustulit maledictum. Secundo, qui sanati sunt, spiritu aguntur, ut iam agnoscant deum, credant deo, timeant deum, quod antea non potuerunt. Tertio, Externa opera pro spiritus arbitrio facit, mortificat carnem, servit proximo, & libere, id est hilari conscientia, quod iis operibus non sit iustitia primum emerenda, sed iam donata. Ceremoniis item & humanis ordinationibus utitur, ita ut postulat charitas. In omnibus autem quae facit, habet facti sui rationem, quam hic causatur, cum inquit: Qui sanavit me, iussit ut auferam grabatum. Id est ex fide facit quicquid. Servatque regulam Pauli, Rom. 14. Omne quod non est ex fide, peccatum est. Qui me sanum fecit &c. Atque adeò ex fide facio, quod facio.

## 5:13
He did not know who he was.

The Word which he had heard and had conceived in his heart, he did not know Christ according to the flesh, nor does Christ wish this.

Latin:

Nesciebat quis esset.

Verbum quod audierat & conceperat corde, non norat autem christum secundum carnem, nec hoc vult christus.

## 5:14
Behold, you are made whole.

There are two things to be considered in every affliction: judgment and mercy. Judgment is that we are punished on account of our sins, as Christ says here: Do not sin, lest something worse happen to you. Thus the whole law and the prophets teach. But besides these, it is necessary to know mercy, that is, that the one punishing does not punish in this for the sake of destroying, but for the sake of converting, provided we believe in him. 1. Corinth. 11. When we are judged, we are corrected by the Lord, that we may not be condemned with the world. And Nahum: The Lord is good, and strengthening in the day of tribulation. David 29. Call upon me in tribulation. Thus, let the confirmed conscience believe the cross to be salutary, which faith produces peace in the hearts of the afflicted, etc.

Latin:

Ecce sanus factus es.

Duo sunt in omni afflictione consyderanda, iudicium & misericordia. Iudicium est, quod propter peccata nostra punimur, ut hic dicit Christus: Noli peccare, ne quid tibi deterius accidat. Sic tota lex & prophetae docent. Sed praeter haec scire oportet misericordiam, id est, quod puniens non in hoc punit ut perdat, sed ut convertat, modo ei credamus. 1. Corinth. 11. Dum iudicamur, à domino corripmur, ne cum mundo damnemur. Et Naum: Bonus dominus, & confortans in die tribulationis. David 29. Invoca me in tribulatione. Ita confirmata conscientia credat salutarem esse crucem, quae fides pacem in cordibus afflictorum efficit &c.

## 5:17
My Father works until now.

It is nothing other than that the Father works, and works all things in all, according to the counsel of his will. As Paul Ephes. 1. says, and Rom. 11. From him and through him and in him are all things. By which sentence, if anyone measures it by reason, nothing will be more absurd. On the contrary, there seems to be no more present consolation than if one knows that life, sustenance, death, and all one's affairs are in the hand of God, ruled and disposed by a present Father. Those who do not believe this, when they must depart through death outside of all creatures, do not feel themselves creeping into the bosom of God, and thus they despair. Furthermore, this opinion and the one below prove the divinity of Christ. For if the Father does not work without the Son, and as He says afterwards: The Son likewise, that is, with equal power with the Father, works, it is necessary that the Son works as God. Since indeed He works with equal power with the Father. What then if the Father and the Son do all things in common? Why does the Son judge, and the Father does not judge? Christ answers this question below, when He says: He has given Him authority to execute judgment, because He is the Son of man. That is, the judgment of the Father and the Son is the same in the hidden realm. For God judges in the hidden realm, but the execution of the judgment is manifest in the Son, appearing openly in the flesh. And from these things you may understand what the glory of Christ is. For since judgment belongs to God alone, yet Christ judges, it is necessary that Christ be God. Therefore Christ judges here: that all may honor the Son, just as they honor the Father. The same: Just as they confess the Father to be God, so let them also confess the Son to be God. For if He does not yield His glory to another, it is necessary that Christ be God, who He says that the Son is to be honored just as the Father.

Latin:

Pater meus usque modo operatur.

Nihil aliud est quàm patrem operari, & omnia operari in omnibus, iuxta consilium voluntatis suae. Ut Paulus Ephes. 1. dicit, & Rom. 11. Ex ipso & per ipsum, & in ipso sunt omnia. Qua sententia, si quis eam ratione metiatur, nihil erit absurdius. Contrà videtur non alia praesentior consolatio, quàm si sciat in manu dei esse vitam, victum, mortem, omnia sua regi & disponi praesente patre. Qui non credunt illi, cum egrediendum est per mortem extra omnes creaturas, non sentiunt se in sinum dei irrepere, atque ita desperant. Porrò haec sententia, & inferior Christi divinitatem probant. Nam si pater non operatur sine filio: & ut postea dicit: Filius similiter, id est pari potentia cum patre operatur, necesse est filium deum operari. Siquidem pari potentia cum patre operatur. Quid igitur si pater & filius omnia communiter faciunt? cur filius iudicat, & pater non iudicat? Huic quaestioni respondet Christus infra, cum dicit: Dedit potestatem iudicium facere, quia filius hominis est. Id est, idem est iudicium patris & filii in abscondito. Deus enim in abscondito iudicat, sed executio iudicii manifesta filii est, palàm in carne apparentis. Et ex his intelligas, quae sit gloria Christi. Nam cum iudicium solius dei sit, iudicet autem Christus, necesse est deum esse Christum. Ideo hic Christus iudicat: ut omnes honorificent filium, sicut honorificant patrem. Idem: Sicut confitentur patrem deum esse, ita confiteantur & filium deum esse. Nam si gloriam suam alteri non cedit, necesse est deum esse Christum, qui sic filium, ut patrem honorificandum esse dicit.

## 5:24
He who hears my word, and believes him.

But he himself explains what it is to honor, when he says: He who hears my word, and believes him, that is, to believe is the honor which is shown to me. And then, just as this honor is shown to me, so the same honor is shown to the Father, that it be believed that just as my will is, and in this I have come that I might save: So it is the Father's will, to will to save. Likewise what he says: He who does not honor the Son, he does not honor the Father either. That is what he says elsewhere: No one knows the Son. I am the way. That is, the Father cannot be known, except through me, Christ. But to know in Christ, is to know in the word of the cross, this is that when we die together with Christ, we know the will of the Father to be, that we are to be resurrected with Christ. And these things indeed concerning the first part, by which he renders the reason for the violated Sabbath, because the Father does the same. Furthermore, for the Sabbath to be violated, is for the law to be abolished. But for the law to be abolished, is for honor to be taken away from God. Therefore, when Christ abolishes the law, he teaches that the Father is not honored by the law. How therefore is the Father honored? If you believe in the Son, this is the honor by which the Father wishes himself to be honored, not the law.

Latin:

Qui verbum meum audit, & credit ei.

Quid autem honorificare sit, ipse exponit, cum ait: Qui verbum meum audit, & credit ei, Id est, credere honos est, qui mihi exhibetur. Et deinde, sicut hic honos mihi exhibetur, ita idem honos patri exhibetur, ut credatur, quod sicut mea voluntas est, & in hoc veni ut salvem: Ita patris voluntatem esse, velle salvare. Item quod dicit: Qui non honorificat filium, ille nec patrem honorificat. Id est quod aliâs dicit: Nemo novit filium. Ego sum via. Id est pater cognosci, nisi per me Christum, non potest. At in Christo cognoscere, est in verbo crucis cognoscere, hoc est ut cum commorimur cum Christo, scimus voluntatem patris esse, resuscitari nos cum Christo. Et haec quidem de prima parte, qua reddit rationem violati sabbati, quia idem facit pater. Porrò sabbatum violari, est legem tolli. Tolli autem legem, est adimi honorem deo. Christus ergo cum tollit legem, docet lege non honorificari patrem. Quomodo igitur honorificatur pater? Si credas in filium, hic honos est, quo se pater honorificari vult, non lex.

## 5:31
If I bear witness.

A little before in rendering the reason for the violated Sabbath, he has cited the authority of the father, in which place he aims at this, that righteousness is not the carnal observation of the Sabbath, that is, that which is not from faith, but to believe. For thus he says: He who hears my word has eternal life. And these things indeed thus far. Now he proves that he was sent by the father, first by miracles, second, by the testimony of John, third, by the scriptures. This opinion seems to conflict with that which is cap. 8. And if I bear witness of myself, my witness is true. I answer as elsewhere & in this chapter: I do not seek my own will. Thus here also he teaches that he does not wish to be known according to the flesh, that which also below: The flesh profits nothing, it is the spirit that gives life. But they wish to know according to the flesh, who seek signs comprehensible to the flesh, just as the Jews were seeking, Matthaei 16. Just as all seek, who not content with the word, which Christ himself is, require the flesh of Christ. And this is the unbelief of all, to wish to know, not to wish to believe: such are they, who are offended by predestination &c. Finally. They know Christ according to the flesh, who imitate the doctrine, work, ceremonies, & whatever is of Christ, carnally. But they are not worthy, that they should be recounted here. Therefore when he denies that he is the one, who bears witness of himself, he denies that his flesh is that, from which the word proceeds. Just as he said before, that he does not seek his own, but the will of the father. And below cap. 7. My doctrine is not mine. Therefore In 8. cap. he adds: You judge according to the flesh. That is, you, because you see nothing but a man, think that the testimony which I bear to myself is human, and not rather divine. And thus when he says: And I know that my testimony is true, he signifies that he is God, because he bears witness of himself.

Latin:

Si ego testimonium perhibeo.

Paulò ante in reddenda ratione violati sabbati, patris autoritatem causatus est, quo loco eò tendit, non esse iustitiam observationem sabbati carnalem, id est eam quae non est ex fide, sed credere. Sic enim ait: Qui verbum meum audit, habet vitam aeternam. Et haec quidem hactenus. Nunc probat se à patre missum esse, primum miraculis, secundo, testimonio Ioannis, tertio, scripturis. Pugnare videtur haec sententia, cum ea quae est cap. 8. Et si ego testimonium perhibeo de meipso, verum est testimonium meum. Respondeo sicut aliâs & in hoc capite: Non quaero voluntatem meam. Ita hic quoque docet, se nolle secundum carnem nosci, id quod & infra: Caro non prodest quicquam, spiritus est qui vivificat. Volunt autem secundum carnem nosse, qui signa petunt comprehensibilia carni, sicut Iudaei petebant, Matthaei 16. Sicut petunt omnes, qui non contenti verbo, quod ipse Christus est, carnem Christi requirunt. Et haec est omnium incredulitas, scire velle, credere non velle: quales sunt, qui offenduntur praedestinatione &c. Postremo. Christum secundum carnem norunt, qui doctrinam, opus, ceremonias, & quicquid est Christi, carnaliter imitantur. Sed ii non sunt digni, ut hic recenseantur. Proinde cum neget se esse, qui testimonium de seipso perhibeat, negat suam carnem esse, unde verbum proficiscatur. Sicut ante dixit, se non suam, sed voluntatem patris quaerere. Et infra cap. 7. Doctrina mea non est mea. Propterea in 8. cap. apponit: Vos secundum carnem iudicatis. Id est, vos, quia nihil nisi hoiem videtis, putatis testimonium quod ipse mihi perhibeo, humanum esse, ac non potius divinum. Et sic cum dicit: Et scio quod verum est testimonium, se deum esse significat, quod de se ipso testificetur.

## 5:33
You sent to John.

The second testimony concerning Christ is the testimony of John, which he compares with the former, namely with the miracles, saying that John indeed testified truly, but that the testimony which the Father bears is greater. For John did no sign. Moreover, he adds that he alleges manifold testimony so that they may believe.

Latin:

Vos misistis ad Ioannem.

Secundum testimonium de Christo, Ioannis est testimonium, quod cum priore comparat, scilicet cum miraculis, inquiens Ioannem quidem vere testificatum esse, sed maius esse testimonium quod pater perhibet. Ioannes enim nullum signum faciebat. Addit autem, se multiplex testimonium allegare ut credant.

## 5:37
Neither have you heard his voice at any time.

This is a refutation by which Christ dilutes the argument by which he confirms the authority of the law, because the law was given by a divine voice, with God appearing from Mount Sinai in a visible form, as in Exodus 19. Here Christ does not deny that the law was given by a divine voice, but he denies that the Jews heard or saw God, so that it might be gathered from that why the Jews do not believe. He gathers it, however, in this manner. You have neither heard nor seen God, therefore you do not believe me. You might ask, therefore, why Christ denies that they heard God, when they certainly heard the voice of God from Mount Sinai? I answer, because By an external and bodily appearance, not God, but that appearance which is a creature, is perceived; therefore they did not see God. Thus, those who hear only an external and bodily voice, hear a creature. And since God is spirit 2. Corint. 3, He is neither perceived, nor known, nor heard, except by the spirit. For the flesh is that veil by which the face of Moses was covered, which is taken away when we turn to God. And this very history teaches this, when Israel wishes that Moses should speak, and denies that they can bear the voice of God, but Moses was entering into the mountain and bearing the voice of God and the sight of Him. Therefore, to hear the voice of God, to perceive God, is to acknowledge and hear the spirit, that is, to perceive the voice in the heart, and to be vivified by it. In this sense it is said here, that the Jews neither heard, etc., nor saw the appearance, because they did not perceive by the spirit, by which alone God is known, and His voice is perceived. But to have seen or heard corporally does not justify, because justification is to be regenerated by the spirit of God. Therefore, in this sentence nothing else is said than in the Corinthians: The letter kills, the spirit gives life. For the letter is a bodily voice, a bodily appearance, and whatever is not the living spirit in us. But the spirit is the power of God.

Latin:

Neque vocem eius unquam audistis.

Confutatio est, qua Christus diluit argumentum, quo legis autoritatem confirmat, quod lex divina voce, de monte Syna deo apparente visibili specie, lata sit. ut Exodi 19. Hic Christus non negat legem latam esse divina voce, sed negat audisse Iudaeos, aut vidisse deum, ut inde colligatur cur non credant Iudaei. Colligit autem in hunc modum. Vos deum nec audistis nec vidistis, ergo mihi non creditis. Quaeras igitur, cur negat Christus eos audisse deum, cum certe audierunt vocem dei è monte Syna? Respondeo, quia externa & corporali specie non deus, sed illa species quae creatura est, cernitur, ideo deum non viderunt. Ita qui vocem externam & corporalem tantum audiunt, creaturam audiunt. Et cum deus spiritus sit. 2. Corint. 3 neque cernitur, neque cognoscitur, neque auditur, nisi spiritu. Nam caro velamen illud est, quo Moy si facies tecta est, quod aufertur, cum convertimur ad deum. Atque haec quidem ipsa historia docet, cum Israël vult, ut Moyses loquatur, & negat se ferre posse vocem dei, Moyses autem ingrediebatur in montem & vocem dei & conspectum ferens. Proinde audire vocem dei, cernere deum, est agnoscere & audire spiritum, id est percipere vocem in corde, & vivificari per eam. In hanc sententiam hic dicitur, Iudaeos nec audisse, &c. nec speciem vidisse, quia spiritu non percepisse, quo solo cognoscitur deus, & percipitur vox eius. Vidisse autem vel audisse corporaliter, non iustificat, quia iustificatio est regenerari spiritu dei. Itaque in hac sententia nihil aliud dicitur, quàm in Corinth. Litera occidit, spiritus vivificat. Est enim litera vox corporalis, species corporalis, & quicquid non est vivus spiritus in nobis. Spiritus autem potentia dei est.

## 5:39
Search the scriptures.

For the scriptures convict you, because you cannot excel, and because they convict, therefore they show me, who justify all believers. For the law is a schoolmaster into Christ, etc.

Latin:

Scrutamini scripturas.

Nam scripturae coarguunt vos, quod non possitis vos praestare, & quia coarguunt, ideo me ostendunt, qui iustificem omnes credentes. Lex enim paedagogus est in Christum &c.

## 5:45
Do not think.

Just as he said above, God did not send his son into the world, that he might judge the world. That is the word which is announced by Christ, it is the remission of sins, not an accusation. It is the law which accuses and condemns all. It is Christ who raises up believers from the judgment of the law.

In whom you hope. For if you believed, etc.

But see in this text the contradictions. In whom you hope, and then. If you believed Moses, etc. Here Christ distinguishes feigned faith from true. Feigned faith is a carnal opinion, inconstant and uncertain about God, judging concerning the word of God according to the flesh. Thus the Jews believe Moses carnally, hearing Moses, they perform the law carnally. Thus all the impious think carnally about God, imagining for themselves a God according to carnal judgment. Some think otherwise, either that he is not such an iniquitous exactor that he demands impossibilities from us, or that he is not so savage as to condemn a man. Others on the contrary, that he is not so merciful as to have pity on sinners. This indeed is idolatry, to fashion a form for God not according to his word, but according to carnal judgment. Concerning these it is said Jer. 51.: Every man is made a fool, every founder is confounded by the graven image, because his molten image is a lie. Hence there is carnal security, hence despair. &c. But true faith is a certain and constant opinion about God, conceiving God according to His word, condemning all our things, showing mercy not because of our works, but because of His mercy for the sake of Christ. This faith is joined with the fear of God, and brings forth security, not that carnal one, but joy of heart, enduring in present afflictions: and this faith looks forward to afflictions, and beyond them, to God who will deliver and rescue &c. Thus they did not believe Moses, that is, the word of God promulgated through Moses, but the Jews believed according to their own carnal opinion, as it seemed to them. All things which Christ taught, Moses wrote concerning Him. Wherefore if the Jews had understood Moses in the spirit, that is, that all our things are condemned by the law, that no one is justified by his own works (as Moses Exod. 34. Innocent &c. and Deut. 9. Not because of your righteousnesses. &c. He also testifies that Christ is to come who will save, not indeed with respect to works, because through Him even the nations are to be blessed &c.) If they had understood these things in the spirit, they certainly would have believed in Christ. Now because they understood Moses carnally, namely that they themselves were righteous before to God, if they were doing the works of the law, and they thought that Christ was about to reign carnally, they wish to believe their own sense, they condemn the sense of God and Christ.

Latin:

Nolite putare.

Sicut supra dixit, non misit deus filium suum in mundum, ut iudicet mundum. Id est verbum quod à Christo nunciatur, remissio peccatorum est non ac cusatio. Lex est, quae accusat, & condemnat omnes. Christ est, qui iudicio legis erigit credentes.

In quo vos speratis. Si enim credere-tis &c.

Sed vide in hoc textu pugnantia. In quo vos speratis, & deinde. Si crederetis Moysi &c. Hic Christus distinguit fictam fidem à vera. Ficta fides, carnalis opinio est, inconstans & incerta de deo, iudicans de verbo dei secundum carnem. Sic Iudaei credunt Moysi carnaliter, audientes Moysen, carnaliter faciunt legem. Sic omnes impii de deo carnaliter sentiunt, imaginantes sibi deum tuxta carnale iudicium. Alii aliter, vel quod non sit tam iniquus exactor, ut à nobis impossibilia postulet, vel quod non sit saevus ut hominem damnet. Alii contra, non tam misericordem ut peccatorum misereatur. Haec vero idololatria est, deo formam affingere non secundum verbum eius, sed se cundum carnale iudicium. De his Hiere. 51. dicitur: Stultus factus est omnis homo, à scientia confusus est omnis conflator in sculptili, quia mendax est conflatio eius. Hinc securitas carnalis est, hinc desperatio. &c. Fides autem vera, est sententia de deo certa & constans, concipiens deum iuxta suum verbum, damnantem omnia nostra, miserentem non propter opera nostra, sed propter misericordiam suam propter Christum. Haec fides coniuncta est cum timore dei, & parit securitatem non carnalem illam, sed gaudium cordis, durans in afflictionibus praesentibus: atque haec fides afflictiones prospicit, & ultra eas, deum liberaturum & erepturum &c. Sic non credebant Moysi, id est verbo dei per Moysen promulgato, sed Iudaei credebant pro sua carnali opinione, sicut eis videbatur. Omnia quae docuit Christus, de eo scripsit Moyses. Quare si Iudaei intellexissent Moysen spiritu, hoc est damnari lege omnia nostra, neminem iustificari suis operibus (ut Moyses Exodi 34. Innocens &c. & Deut. 9. Non propter iustitias tuas. &c. Testatur & Christum venturum esse qui salvet, non quidem operum respectu, quod per eum etiam gentes benedicendae sunt &c) Haec si spiritu intellexissent, certe credidissent Christo. Nunc quia carnaliter intelligebant Moysen, scilicet se iustos esse coram deo, si facerent opera legis, & Christum carnaliter regnaturum putabant, volunt suo sensui credere, sensum dei damnant & Christum.

## 5:46
For he wrote about me.

It does not only pertain to that, which Moses testified concerning Christ ( Genesis 3. and 12. In your seed shall all nations be blessed. And 49. The sons of your father shall worship you. Then: He is the expectation of the nations. And Deuter. 18. I will raise up a prophet etc.) But also, because the law was given only for this reason, that sin might be known, and we might know that we cannot be justified by works, and that we have need of grace, as Paul says: The end of the law is Christ. Likewise, the law is our schoolmaster unto Christ.

Latin:

De me enim ille scripsit.

Non solum eò pertinet, quod Moyses de Christo testificatus ( Genesis 3. & 12. In semine tuo benedicentur omnes gentes. Et 49. Adorabunt te filii patris tui. Deinde: Ipse est expectatio gentium. Et Deuter. 18. Prophetam suscitabo &c.) Sed etiam, quia ideo tantum lata lex est, ut peccatum cognosceretur, & sciremus operibus iustificari nos non posse, & habere opus gratiae, ut Paulus inquit: Finis legis, Christus est. Item, lex paedagogus noster in Christum.

## 6:1 Argument
CHAPTER SIX.

What the use of this history is, appears sufficiently from the other Evangelists, namely that it may confirm those who are anxious about food. Thus Matthew 16. Christ reminds those anxious about bread of this deed. Why do you think among yourselves, O you of little faith? Furthermore, he heals this temptation with many histories and promises, Deuteronomy 8. I gave you the food of manna, that I might show you not by bread alone etc. And Matthew 6. Do not be anxious to be, saying, what shall we eat? etc. And of Proverbs: The blessing of the Lord makes rich. And Psalm 103. When you give to them they will gather etc. Moreover, it teaches that those who have should know it is a gift of God, and give thanks for the gift of God. For to eat with thanksgiving is to perceive that what we eat is a gift of God etc. And those who do not have, let them trust that they will receive from the hand of God.

They abound indeed who believe, according to that Mark 10. They will receive a hundredfold even in this time. But those in whom the word has grown abound much more in spiritual nourishment, which at the beginning seemed thin and moderate to the flesh, but afterwards is felt to be incomprehensible. And to this the allegory properly pertains, according to that: To everyone who has, it will be given.

Latin:

CAPUT SEXTUM.

Qui sit huius historiae usus, satis apparet ex aliis Evangelistis, nimirum ut confirmet eos qui de victu soliciti sunt. Sic Matthaei 16. Christus solicitos de panibus, huius facti commonefacit. Quid cogitatis inter vos modicae fidei? Porrò huic tentationi multis historiis & promissionibus medetur, Deuteronomii 8. Dedi tibi cibum manna, ut ostenderem tibi non in solo pane &c. Et Matthaei 6. Nolite soliciti esse, dicentes, quid manducabimus? &c. Et Proverbiorum: Benedictio domini divites facit. Et Psalmo 103. Dante te illis colligent &c. Docet autem, & ut qui habent, sciant donum dei esse, & agant gratias pro dono dei. Nam manducare cum gratiarum actione, est sentire dei donum esse quod edimus &c. Et qui non habent, confidant se de manu dei accepturos.

Abundant quidem qui credunt, iuxta illud Marci 10. Accipient centuplum etiam in tempore hoc. Sed multo magis abundant spirituali alimonia illi, in quibus verbum crevit, quod principio tenue & modi cum carni videbatur, postea sentitur incomprehensibile esse. Et huc allegoria proprie pertinet, iuxta illud: Omni habenti dabitur.

## 6:6
For this he was saying, testing him.

For thus the saints are tested, so that they may become known to themselves, and God may become known to them, Rom. 5. Testing produces patience, patience probation etc. A denarius makes two groats and a half. For thus Philip answers according to the flesh, feeling that they could not be fed otherwise, or rather, could not even be fed for less.

Latin:

Hoc enim dicebat tentans eum.

Sic enim tentantur sancti, ut ipsi sibi innotescant, & innotescat eis deus, Rom. 5. Tentatio patientiam, patientia probationem &c. Denarius facit duos grossos & dimidiatum. Sic enim respondet Philippus secundum carnem, sentiens eos non aliter posse pasci immo nec minoris posse pasci.

## 6:20
It is I, do not be afraid etc.

The only consolation in every affliction is to trust that we are tempted by the good will of God. Therefore, by way of consolation, he says: I am, that is, I who am yours, who save you, who do not allow those who believe to perish.

Latin:

Ego sum, nolite timere &c.

Unica consolatio est in omni afflictione, confidere quod bona voluntate dei tentemur. Propterea argumento consolationis inquit: Ego sum, id est ego qui vester sum, qui vos salvo, qui perire non sino credentes.

## 6:26
Amen amen I say to you, you seek me, not &c.

Because the flesh savors or seeks nothing but carnal things, even in Christ it sees and looks at nothing but the flesh, when Christ even wishes his own flesh to be crucified and to die, so that we may know that life, wisdom, justice, glory, kingdom, and whatever is carnal, is condemned and cursed. On the contrary, that the cross, death, &c., by which the flesh is abolished, are blessed. Thus he speaks of himself John 12. Unless the grain of wheat falling into the ground, shall have died, it remains alone &c. They look at the flesh of Christ, who imitate or do whatever work or precept of Christ, and wish to justify themselves through it, in short all those who do not wish to mortify themselves. Thus the Jews follow him when he fed them, they do not follow when he was being crucified. They follow Christ for a while, until they are tempted, they believe as long as he feeds, they desert when he has ceased to feed. This is the occasion for Christ, after he had fed them, to speak about flesh and spirit, as if he were saying: Behold this people of the law, they seem to themselves to be the people of God, and they pretend to believe, they pretend the law, so that they may be preserved by God in the kingdom, riches, etc. Now when he hears the Gospel being preached, and that the worldly kingdom is to be ended, and that all things of that people are to be condemned, he does not hear the voice of God, etc. He believed for so long as God gave carnal things. Now when He does not promise carnal things, he curses, etc.

Latin:

Amen amen dico vobis, quaeritis me, non &c.

Quia caro nihil nisi carnalia sapit, aut quaerit, etiam in Christo nihil nisi carnem videt & spectat, Cum Christus etiam suam carnem crucifigi & mori velit, ut sciamus vitam, sapientiam, iustitiam, gloriam, regnum, & quicquid est carnale, damnatum & maledictum esse. Econtra, benedictam esse crucem, mortem &c. quibus caro aboletur. Sic ipse de sese loquitur Ioannis 12. Nisi granum frumenti cadens in terram, mortuum fuerit, ipsum solum manet &c. Carnem Christi spectant, qui quodcunque opus vel praeceptum Christi imitantur aut faciunt, & per illud se iustificare volunt, breviter omnes illi, qui se mortificare nolunt. Sic Iudaei sequuntur eum cum pasceret, non sequuntur cum crucifigeretur. Christum sequuntur tantisper, lonec tententur, credunt donec pascit, deserunt ubi pastere cessaverit. Haec occasio est Christo, post quàm pavisset hos, dicendi de carne & spiritu, quasi diceret: Ecce hic populus legis, dei populus sibi videtur esse, & simulat se credere, simulat legem, ut à deo conservetur in regno, opibus &c. iuxta legis promissionem. Nunc cum audit Evangelium praedicari, & regnum mundanum finiendum esse, & omnia istius populi damnanda esse, non audit vocem dei &c. Tantisper credidit, dum dedit deus carnalia. Nunc cum non promittat carnalia, maledicit &c.

## 6:27
For Him God the Father has sealed.

He gives the reason why He is about to give the food which does not perish, etc. To seal the Word is to testify, because He says, the Father has sealed the Son, that is, He has anointed Him with the Spirit sanctifying and vivifying those who believe, etc.

Latin:

Hunc enim pater consignavit deus.

Reddit rationem cur sit daturus cibum, qui non pereat &c. Verbum signari est testificari, quia inquit, pater filium signavit, id est, unxit spiritu sanctificante & vivificante credentes &c.

## 6:28
What shall we do, that we may work the works of God?

See, however, the people of the law asking what they should work, that they may do the works of God, that is, which justify before God, as if they did not have the law given by God. Christ answers, no longer commanding the law, but He condemns the righteousnesses of the law, saying that this one work of God is that by which we are justified before God, to believe in Him whom He has sent, etc.

Latin:

Quid faciamus, ut operemur opera dei?

Vide autem populum legis interrogantem, quid operetur, ut faciat opera dei, id est, quae coram deo iustificent, quasi non habeant legem à deo datam. Respondet Christus, non iam praecipiens legem, sed legis iustitias damnat, inquiens, unum hoc opus dei esse, quo coram deo iustificamur, credere in eum quem misit ille &c.

## 6:30
What sign, therefore, do You do?

Here, when He condemns the righteousnesses of the law, they object again for the sake of defending the law, that they have the testimony of the law, manna given from heaven. Therefore what sign do you perform? that is, because you condemn the righteousnesses of the law, and preach faith, what will be the testimony, why we may safely believe you?

Latin:

Quod ergo tu facis signum?

Hic cum legis iusticias damnet, iterum opponunt pro defendenda lege, se habere testimonium legis, manna de caelo datum. Ergo quod tu facis signum? id est, quia legis iusticias damnas, & praedicas fidem, quod erit testimonium, cur tuto possimus tibi credere?

## 6:32
Moses did not give you the bread etc.

Christ compares manna with the righteousnesses of the law, to this effect, that just as manna did not give life, so neither do the righteousnesses of the law give life: but that it is Christ who is the life-giving bread, not indeed according to the flesh, for the flesh profits nothing, but according to that which descended from heaven. Therefore he says: The bread which descended from heaven, that is, the sanctifying and life-giving word, is the bread which gives life.

Latin:

Non Moyses dedit vobis panem &c.

Comparat Christus manna cum iusticiis legis, in hanc sententiam, quod sicut manna non vivificaverit, ita nec vivificent iusticiae legis: Christum autem esse qui sit panis vivificans, non quidem secundum carnem, nam caro nihil prodest, sed secundum id quod de caelo descendit. Ideo inquit: Panis qui de caelo descendit, id est, verbum sanctificans & vivificans, panis est qui vivificat.

## 6:34
Lord, always give us this bread.

That which is said 1. Cor. 2., that to the animal man the things which are of the spirit of God are foolishness: the Jews do this in this place when they do not understand Christ's sermon at all, and mock it.

Latin:

Domine semper da nobis panem hunc.

Quod. 1. Cor. 2. dicetur, animali homini stulticiam esse ea, quae sunt spiritus dei: id hoc loco agunt Iudaei cum Christi sermonem prorsus non intelligunt, & irrident.

## 6:37
All that the father gives me.

That is, Therefore you do not believe, because you are not drawn by the father.

Latin:

Omne quod dat mihi pater.

Id est, Ideo non creditis, quia à patre non trahemini.

## 6:37
And him who comes to me, I will not cast out.

Against free will nothing could be more open to be said, than that only those come to Christ whom the Father gives to him, or whom the Father draws, as he says afterwards, or whom the Father teaches. For the Father to give to the Son is to elect: for the Father to draw or to teach is to grant the knowledge of Christ, that is, to teach judgment and justice. Judgment in that the heart feels all our things to be damnable: but that we are justified by grace through Jesus Christ, whom the Father gave for us. Furthermore, that teaching is not the letter, but it is spirit and life in the heart. For the Jews also heard the letter, and now all the impious hear it. But what faith or justice may be, does not ascend into the heart of man, Esaiae 64. You see therefore that nothing at all is attributed to free will or human powers. Furthermore, if predestination offends, act like those, 2. Regum 10. Be a man of valor &c. and let us fight for our people, but the Lord will do what is good in his sight. This is to be done which God commands, not what he has forbidden, but he commanded to believe in Christ, Matthaei 3. This is my beloved Son, hear him. But he did not wish predestination to be known. 2. ad Timot. 2. The Lord knows who are his. Likewise because the will of the Father is the same as that of Christ, and by the will of Christ, the will of the Father is to be known, but Christ says: Come to me all you who labor &c. Therefore it is also the will of the Father to refresh all who labor, that which Christ also added here. These It is the will of the Father, that those who believe may be saved. Do not hesitate concerning the will of the Father, if you believe in the Son.

Latin:

Et eum qui venit ad me, non eiiciam.

Contra liberum arbitrium nihil potuit apertius dici, quod illi tantum ad Christum veniant quos dat ei pater, seu quos trahit pater, sicut postea dicit, seu quos docet pater. Patrem dare filio, est eligere: patrem trahere vel docere, est cognitionem Christi donare, id est docere iudicium & iustitiam. Iudicium in eo quod cor sentit omnia nostra damnibilia esse: iustificari autem nos gratia per Iesum Christum, quem pro nobis dedit pater. Porrò illud docere non est litera, sed est spiritus & vita in corde. Nam & Iudaei literam audiebant, & nunc audiunt omnes impii. Quid autem sit fides aut iustitia, non ascendit in cor hominis, Esaiae 64. Vides itaque nihil prorsus tribui libero arbitrio aut viribus humanis. Porrò si offendit praedestinatio, fac ut illi, 2. Regum 10. Esto vir fortis &c. & pugnemus pro populo, dominus autem faciet quod bonum est in conspectu suo. Hoc faciendum est quod praecipit deus, non quod vetuit, praecepit autem credi in Christum, Matthaei 3. Hic est filius meus dilectus, hunc audite. Praedestinationem autem non voluit sciri. 2. ad Timot. 2. Dominus novit, qui sint eius. Item quia eadem voluntas est patris quae Christi, & Christi voluntate, patris voluntas cognoscenda est, Christus autem inquit: Venite ad me omnes qui laboratis &c. Ergo & patris voluntas est, reficere omnes laborantes, id quod hic etiam adiecit Christus. Haec est voluntas patris, ut qui credunt, salvi fiant. De voluntate patris noli haesitare, si filio credis.

## 6:39
That I should lose nothing of all that he has given me.

He says this on account of the faint-hearted, who, beholding the magnitude of their sin, do not dare to believe. Likewise on account of those who, although they have already begun to believe, nevertheless waver in temptation, where they think themselves to be forsaken, and therefore about to perish. And this is the consolation which we seek from predestination, to know that we are in the divine hand. Thus the apostle consoles: Rom. 8. If we are in the hand of God, we cannot fall.

Latin:

Ut omne quod dedit mihi non perdam ex eo.

Propter pusillanimes dicit, qui conspicientes magnitudinem peccati, non audent credere. Item propter eos qui, quanquam iam coeperint credere, tamen in tentationem labascunt, ubi se putant deseri, atque ideo perituros. Atque haec est consolatio, quam à praedestinatione petimus, scire quod in manu divina simus. Sic consolatur apostolus: Rom. 8. Si in manu dei sumus, cadere non possumus.

## 6:40
And this is the will of my Father.

He says this for this reason, lest consciences should doubt, since it is the very will of the Father to desire to save as many as believe. We know this to be the will of Christ.

Latin:

Haec est autem voluntas patris mei.

Hoc ideo dicit, ne conscientiae dubitent, quoniam ipsissima voluntas patris sit, velle salvare quotquot credunt. Scimus hanc voluntatem esse Christi.

## 6:49
Your fathers ate &c.

Four things are treated in order: what the power of the bread is, how it is to be used, whence that power is, why he names flesh. First, Christ distinguishes in this discourse the law from faith, the letter from the spirit, the righteousnesses of the law from the righteousness of the spirit, when he says: Your fathers ate manna etc. that is, the law does not make you alive. Just as corporal manna is only food, so the works of the law are only corporal and external righteousnesses. But Christ is the bread making alive unto eternity. Manna or the righteousness of the law saves no one from death, because by the works of the law shall no flesh be justified before God. The judgment of God confounds all our works. But the living bread, which descended from heaven, saves from death, because it descended from heaven. For no one has ascended into heaven, except he who descended. That is, in whom the spirit of Christ is, they cannot die, just as neither can Christ die. Faith is that which is not judged, and subsists before God; works do not subsist.

Latin:

Patres vestri manducaverunt &c.

Quatuor ex ordine tractantur, quae sit vis panis. quomodo utendum, unde sit ea vis, cur carnem nominet. Primum Christus distinguit hoc sermone legem à fide, literam à spiritu, iustitias legis à iustitia spiritus, cum inquit: Patres vestri manducaverunt manna &c. id est lex non vivificat vos. Sicut manna corporalis tantum cibus est, ita opera legis, corporales tantum, & externae iustitiae sunt. Christus autem est panis vivificans in aeternum. Manna seu iustitiae legis neminem servant à morte, quia ex operibus legis non iustificatur coram deo omnis caro. Iudicium dei confundit omnia opera nostra. Panis autem vivus, qui de caelo descendit, servat à morte, quia de caelo descendit. nemo enim ascendit in caelum, nisi qui descendit. id est in quibus spiritus Christi est, ii non possunt mori, sicut neque Christus mori potest. Fides est quae non iudicatur, & subsistit coram deo, opera non subsistunt.

## 6:50
If anyone shall eat &c.

Secondly, how it is to be used. To eat is otherwise to convey food into all parts of the body, which is not sewn on, but grows onto it. So here, to eat the heavenly bread is to convey it into the heart, and to become the life of the heart. To discern, to handle with hands, to devour bread, is not to eat. But it is another thing to eat: thus to think of Christ with feigned intentions, to imitate works, is not to eat Christ, but to be reborn in the heart, and to be made alive by the spirit of Christ, to fear in the heart, to believe, that at last is truly to eat. But that to eat is to believe, he himself explains at the beginning of the sermon. He who believes in me, has life. And below: The flesh profits nothing.

Latin:

Si quis manducaverit &c.

Secundo quon utendum. Manducare aliâs est traiici cibum in omnes corporis partes, qui non assuatur, sed adnascatur. Ita hic manducare panem caelestem, est traiici eum intra cor, & fieri cordis vitam. Cernere, tractare manibus, vorare panem, non est manducare. Aliud vero est manducare: ita Christum cogitare simulatis intentionibus, imitari opera, non est manducare Christum, sed corde renasci, & vivificari spiritu Christi, corde timere, credere, id demum vere manducare est. Quod autem manducare sit credere, ipse exponit principio concionis. Qui credit in me, habet vitam. Et infra: Caro non prodest quicquam.

## 6:50
He who descended from heaven.

Thirdly, from where the power is, he explains in these words: He who descended from heaven. And then: As the living Father sent me, so I also live because of the Father. That is, it is not the flesh that gives life, but God. Just as he said above: As the Father raises the dead and gives life, so the Son gives life to whom he wills, so that we may not doubt that the power of God is the power of Christ, although the flesh may seem to be weak.

Latin:

Qui de caelo descendit.

Tertio, unde sit vis, id exponit his verbis: Qui de caelo descendit. Et deinde: Sicut misit me vivens pater, ita & ego vivo propter patrem. id est caro non est quae vivificat, sed deus. sicut supra dixit: Sicut pater suscitat mortuos et vivificat, ita filius vivificat quos vultut ne dubitemus potentiam dei, esse potentiam Christi, quanquam infirma caro esse videatur.

## 6:52
The bread which I will give, is my flesh.

Fourthly, why therefore does he speak of flesh and blood, if indeed it is not the flesh that gives life? I answer that it is what the Apostle says 1. Corinth. 1.: Because the world did not know God through wisdom in the wisdom of God, it pleased him through the foolishness of the cross to save those who believe, etc. Therefore the flesh of Christ was exhibited, so that we might believe in Christ crucified, that is, so that we might both know that it is necessary for us to be mortified, and believe that through death we shall receive life, just as Christ also was glorified through death. Likewise, because he satisfied the law with his flesh, and died for us, let us believe that this is our satisfaction. And this is what he says: The bread which I will give is my flesh for the life of the world. Therefore he wishes the flesh to be eaten so that we may be mortified, and yet he says this bread is living, so that we may know that there is life in death.

This is a hard saying, etc.

Christ concluded in a long discourse that the manna did not give life, that he is the one who gives life, and that the eating of him is life. This spiritual doctrine offends carnal men, not only for this reason, that they heard his flesh was to be eaten, but rather for this, that he denied there was life unless his flesh was eaten, and that he preferred his bread to the manna. For the flesh thinks that its own good works are life, nor does it see sin in them. For a veil is drawn over their heart. 2. Corinth. 3. Therefore it thinks it can stand before the judgment of God. When Christ condemns all these things, he offends the Pharisees, teaching that he is the one who must be eaten, that is, that one must believe in Christ crucified, that we must be mortified, humbled, and that all our good works must be condemned, and that we must believe that we are saved by the righteousness of Christ alone: the mortification of which is life for us in Christ. When the Pharisees, ignorant of the righteousness of the spirit, hear these things, they condemn them, because those who wish for righteousness to be saved by the law do not arrive at the righteousness which is by faith, but the Gentiles arrive at it, believing that they are justified without regard for works.

Latin:

Panis quem ego dabo, caro mea est.

Quarto, quare igitur dicit carnem & sanguinem, siquidem caro non est quae vivificat? Respondeo id esse quod 1. Corinth. 1. dicit Apostolus: Quia non cognovit per sapientiam mundus deum in sapientia, ideo placuit ei per stultitiam crucis salvos facere credentes &c. Ideo caro Christi exhibita est, ut in Christum crucifixum credamus, hoc est, ut & sciamus oportere nos mortificari, & credamus, per mortem nos vitam accepturos, sicut & Christus per mortem glorificatus est. Item quia sua carne legi satisfecit, & mortuus est pro nobis, credamus eam satisfactionem nostram esse. Et hoc est quod dicit: Panis quem ego dabo, caro mea est pro vita mundi. Itaque carnem manducari vult ut mortificemur, & tamen ait hunc panem vivum esse, ut sciamus in morte vitam esse.

Durus est hic sermo &c.

Longa concione peregit Christus, manna non vivificasse, se esse qui vivificet, manducationem sui vitam esse. Haec spiritualis doctrina carnales homines offendit, non hoc tantum nomine, quod audirent carnem eius manducandam esse, sed hoc potius quod negabat vitam esse, nisi carnis sui manducationem, quod suum panem mannae praeferebat. Caro enim, vitam esse putat, sua bona opera, nec videt in illis peccatum. Velum enim obductum est cordi eorum. 2. Corinth. 3. quare putat se posse consistere coram iudicio dei. Quae omnia cum damnat Christus, offendit pharisaeos, docens se esse quem manducare oporteat, id est credi oportere in Christum crucifixum, oportere nos mortificari, humiliari, & damnari omnia nostra bona opera, & credere quod solius Christi iustitia salvemur: Quorum mortificatio nobis in Christo vita sit. Haec cum audiunt pharisaei, ignari iustitiae spiritus, damnant, qui quiae iustitiam ex lege salvam esse volunt, ad iustitiam quae ex fide est, non perveniunt, perveniunt autem gentes, credentes se iustificarisine operum respectu.

## 6:63
If therefore you shall see the son of man.

It is an imperfect sentence, which you will be able to complete thus: If you shall see the son of man ascending, you shall know that it is the power of God which quickens you, of which I have now spoken. For in this the Jews were offended, that through mortal flesh he had promised eternal life. For the wisdom of the flesh cannot understand how life is hidden under death, and under the infirmity of the flesh of Christ, the power of God. Thus therefore he says: My resurrection or ascent to the Father will make plain that my flesh, which is now infirm, is the life-giving bread: because when I shall have ascended to the Father, although now infirm, yet I will fill all things etc. Therefore the resurrection will testify that this bread is the power of God. For the resurrection is the glorification of Christ. Nonnus refers it to the judgment, as if he were saying: Your incredulity will be declared one day when I am judging etc.

Latin:

Si ergo videritis filium hominis.

Imperfecta oratio est, quam sic poteris absolvere: Si videritis filium hominis ascendentem, cognoscetis dei potentiam esse quae vivificet vos, de qua nunc dixi. In hoc enim offendebantur Iudaei, quod per mortalem carnem promiserat vitam aeternam. Non potest enim intelligere sapientia carnis, quomodo sub morte vita sit abscondita, & sub infirmitate carnis Christi, potentia dei. Sic ergo dicit: Resurrectio mea vel ascensus ad patrem palàm faciet, carnem meam, quae nunc infirma est, esse panem vivificum: quia ubi ad patrem ascendero, quanquam nunc infirmus, tamen implebo omnia &c. Itaque resurrectio testabitur, potentiam dei esse hunc panem. Nam resurrectio glorificatio Christi est. Nonnus ad iudicium refert, quasi diceret. Declarabitur olim me iudicante incredulitas vestra &c.

## 6:64
It is the spirit that quickens.

He teaches that this is the power of God, with these words: It is the spirit that quickens: and finally: The words that I speak, are spirit and life. Christ is eaten by the word, and therefore by faith, not by carnal eating, apparition, sign etc. For before the power of the word, which is the power of God, has renewed us, we are not yet justified.

Therefore the flesh profits nothing: for it does not renew hearts.

Latin:

Spiritus est qui vivificat.

Potentiam dei esse, docet, hanc suam potentiam, iis verbis: Spiritus est qui vivificat: & demum: Verba quae loquor, spiritus & vita sunt. Verbo manducatur Christus, ideoque fide, non carnali esu, apparitione, signo &c. Priusquam enim verbi potentia, quae dei potentia est, renovarit nos, nondum iustificati sumus.

Proinde nihil prodest caro: nam ea non renovat corda.

## 6:65
But there are some of you who do not believe.

A discourse concerning predestination in carnal men produces contempt of God and blasphemy. For thus the flesh judges: Why should I serve when I do not know whether the service is accepted? The spiritual man, however, is comforted, who resigns his own will to God, and has known that the secrets of God ought not to be known but ought to be believed, and they know that they, believing, are saved.

Latin:

Sed sunt quidam ex vobis qui non cre-dunt.

Sermo de praedestinatione in carnalibus, efficit contemptum dei & blasphemiam. Sic enim iudicat caro: Cur serviam qui nesciam, utrum sit acceptum servitium? Spiritualis autem consolatur, qui suam voluntatem deo resignat, & scivit secreta dei non debere sciri sed credi debere, & sciunt se credentes salvari.

## 6:71
Have I not chosen you twelve? etc.

What he adds concerning Judas indicates that not even all those who still stand will persevere: so that he may teach us fear, lest we boast in anything in ourselves. Perseverance is the gift of God, not our merit. For it is He who works both to will and to perform.

SUMMARY OF THE CHAPTER.

In this chapter you will especially observe what it is to eat the flesh of Christ, namely to believe in Christ crucified, to be mortified at the same time, and to trust that He gives life in death. These things concerning that chapter etc.

Latin:

Nónne ego vos duodecim elegi? &c.

Quod subiicit de Iuda, indicat, nec eos omnes perseveraturos, qui stent adhuc: ut doceat nos timorem, ne quid gloriemur in nobis. Dei donum est, non nostrum meritum perseverantia. Ipse enim est, qui operatur & velle, & perficere.

SUMMARIUM CA-PITIS.
