Confirmat autem & hic conscientias sanctorum, ut sentiant nihil posse regnum mundi adversum eos, praeter id quod pro sua benevolentia pater admittit, ut experiamur eius praesentiam, & fides nostra confirmetur. Sicut Zachariae 2. Ego ero ei, ait dominus, murus igneus.

## 19:11
Therefore he who delivered me to you.

Therefore, that is to say, the father permitted these things to the gates of hell. Then, the matter is so little in your power that you are only a slave of Satan, who impels both the Jews and you.

They have the greater sin: because they know the scriptures to whom Christ is promised. If you let this man go, you are not a friend of Caesar. That is, you do not favor Caesar, and you will be held as one who conspires against Caesar, and you approve of his sedition, etc. We have no king, but Caesar. In this place they testify against themselves, that is, we do not acknowledge the kingdom of Christ.

Latin:

Propterea qui me tradidit tibi.

Propterea, scilicet permisit pater haec portis inferorum. Deinde adeò non est penes te res, ut sis tantum mancipium satanae, qui & Iudaeos & te impellit.

Illi maius peccatum habent: quia illi norunt scripturas quibus promissus est Christus. Si hunc dimittis, non es amicus Caesaris. id est non faves Caesari, & habeberis pro eo qui coniures adversus Caesarem, & probas huius seditionem &c. Non habemus regem, sed Caesarem. hoc loco adversum se testificantur, id est non agnoscimus regnum Christi.

## 19:23
The soldiers therefore, when they had crucified him, took his garments.

There is no doubt that the garments signify the doctrine of Christ, and the soldiers the heretics. All the things that follow seem to have been added to this, so that they might signify the resurrection of Christ and the reborn church: just as from Adam put to sleep, Eve was made. That sleep of Adam undoubtedly signified the death of Christ. This is the humble and dry wood, which he makes to flourish. Ezechiel. 17.

To divide the garments is to tear apart the scripture. To cast lots for the garments signified those who would come who would play with scripture, and twist it in whatever direction according to their own lust.

Latin:

Milites ergo cum crucifixissent eum, acceperunt vestimenta.

Non dubium est quin vestes, Christi doctrinam significent, & milites haereticos. Quae sequuntur omnia videntur in hoc adiecta, ut significarent resurrectionem Christi & renascentem ecclesiam: sicut ex Adam sopito, Eva facta est. Significabat haud dubie ille sopor Adae, mortem Christi. Est hoc lignum humile & aridum, quod florere facit. Ezechiel. 17.

Partiri vestes, est discerpere scripturam. Sortiri de vestibus, significabat futuros qui luderent scriptura, & detorquerent quorsumcumque pro sua libidine.

## 19:29
I thirst. Now a vessel was set there full of vinegar.

This passage argues that these things are recounted so that the afflictions of the saints might be signified. For in this state of the church there is no one who consoles the saints, there is no one who does not mock etc. These are they who give Christ vinegar to drink, when they ought to console him, they mock him. As in Psalm 68. And I looked for one that would grieve together with me, and there was none &c.

Latin:

Sitio. Vas autem erat positum aceto plenum.

Hic locus arguit, haec recenseri, ut significentur sanctorum afflictiones. Nam hoc statu ecclesiae nemo est qui consoletur sanctos, nemo est qui non subsannet &c. Hi sunt qui aceto Christum potant, cum consolari debeant, illudunt. Sicut Psalmo 68. Et sustinui qui simul contristaretur, & non fuit &c.

## 19:34
And immediately there came out blood and water.

Signs are set forth here also, both of the resurrection or life of Christ, and of the ecclesiastical state. For when Christ, being dead, gushed forth blood and water, he testifies that life is believed to be in him. Indeed, then at last his life began both to be hidden in God, and truly to vivify others. Just as the Apostle also says Rom. 6. that we are planted in the likeness of Christ's death, that is, inserted as a plant into him, so that we may be vivified there. And to the Colossians 3. Your life is hidden with Christ in God. Therefore he confirms those who are dying, so that they may feel that there is life even in death, and that our life is hidden in God, provided we believe in Christ, even when he seems to die.

You shall not break a bone of it.

And from this passage it appears that John did not wish so much to recount the history, as to warn what this history signified, namely that this Christ was the true passover, which was signified in the law. For this passage concerning not breaking the bones is taken from Exodus chapter 12. concerning the passover. Now indeed this sentence also confirms the saints that the bones of the Passover cannot be broken, that is, the powers of darkness can rage against Christ only to the extent that they snatch away his life and glory before the world: furthermore, they cannot snatch away his bones, that is, his life and glory before God. The same happens in all the saints; let the wicked rage as much as they please, yet they will not break the bones, that is, no force will be so great that faith does not overcome it: because it is the fruit of God unto salvation to every one that believeth.

Latin:

Et continuo exivit sanguis & aqua.

Sunt & hic aedita signa & resurrectionis seu vitae Christi, & status ecclesiastici. Nam cum Christus mortuus, sanguine & aqua scatuit, testatur vitam in se creditam esse. Immo tum demum coepit eius vitae & abscondi in deo, & vere vivificare alios. Sicut & Apostolus ait Rom. 6. plantatos esse nos in similitudinem mortis Christi, id est insertos tanquam planta sibi, ut ibi vivificemur. Et ad Colossenses 3. Vita vestra abscondita est cum Christo in deo. Proinde morientes confirmat, ut sentiant & in morte vitam esse, & vitam nostram in deo absconditam esse, modò credamus in Christum, etiam cum mori videatur.

Os non comminuetis ex eo.

Et ex hoc loco apparet, Ioannem non voluisse tam historiam recensere, quàm monere quid significaret haec historia, quod scilicet hic Christus verum pascha fuit, quod in lege significatum est. Nam hic locus de non conterendis ossibus exceptus est ex Exodo cap. 12. de paschate. Iam vero & haec sententia sanctos confirmat, non posse ossa paschatis conteri, id est potetas tenebrarum saevire in Christum potest eatenus ut vitam, gloriam coram mundo eripiat: porrò ossa hoc est vitam, gloriam coram deo eripere non potest. Idem fit in omnibus sanctis, saeviant quantumlibet impii, tamen ossa non conterent, id est nulla vis tanta erit, quam non fides superet: quia fructus dei est in salutem omni credenti.

## 19:40
But they took the body.

Thus far Christ has suffered. The Jews and Pilate feel that they have dispelled every danger, when the totality of things begins to proclaim Christ through various miracles, and the Centurion begins to proclaim him with a public voice: for because he had resigned vengeance to God, it comes to pass that God testifies concerning his Son. To this pertains the fact that certain ones also bury him, that is, they have honor for the infirm Christ, they proclaim and acknowledge his death, but they do not yet know his life, etc.

Latin:

Acceperunt autem corpus.

Hactenus passus est Christus. Sentiunt Iudaei & Pilatus omne se periculum discussisse, cum universitas rerum variis miraculis, & Centurio publica voce praedicare Christum incipiant: quia enim deo vindictam resignarat, fit ut testificetur deus de filio. Huc pertinet, quod & sepeliunt quidam, hoc est habent honorem infirmo Christo, mortem eius praedicant & agnoscunt, sed vitam nondum norunt &c.

## 20:1 Argument
CHAPTER TWENTY.

Christ rises again, and appears to those seeking him in the sepulcher and mourning, that is, to those who by the death of his and they themselves are put to death, they are the same who bury him. Furthermore, they are ignorant of the resurrection, that is, to those so afflicted nothing appears except death. On the one day of the sabbath, that is, on the first day of the week, in which they were observing the sabbath.

Latin:

CAPUT VIGESI-MUM.

Resurgit Christus, & apparet quaerentibus eum in sepulchro & lugentibus, hoc est qui morte eius & ipsi mortificantur, iidem sunt qui sepeliunt eum. Porrò resurrectionem ignorant, hoc est sic afflictis nihil apparet praeter mortem. Una die sabbati. id est prima die hebdomadis, in qua sabbatum agebant.

## 20:17
Do not touch me, for I have not yet ascended to the father.

Christ does not wish to be touched in the meantime while he has not ascended to the father, that is, he does not wish that we should boast of his flesh, nor does he wish to be worshipped carnally. The apostles were expecting a certain carnal kingdom, and they thought that this kingdom was about to begin immediately from the resurrection, as appears from the Acts. And a carnal opinion about Christ, likewise every carnal worship, which the flesh simulates by itself, is that touching of Magdalene. But he condemns this carnal worship, just as he does often elsewhere, and he submits that he is finally to be touched there, where he ascends to the father. We touch him, however, by faith, and we worship him with that worship which he effects in us. For Christ dies, rises again, and ascends for this reason, that he may fill all things, and truly reign in the hearts of all, which cannot even happen in a bodily kingdom. Thus also above he speaks of his kingdom John 12, when they too were dreaming of a bodily kingdom. Unless a grain of wheat falling into the ground etc. Likewise where I shall be exalted from the earth, I will draw all things to myself, etc.

Do not touch me. That is, I cannot be touched unless I have ascended to the Father, that is, then you will touch me by faith.

Tell them, namely that this kingdom of mine is to be, and that in this I have risen again, so that I may ascend.

Latin:

Noli me tangere, quia nondum ascen-di ad patrem.

Non vult se tangi Christus interim dum non ascendit ad patrem, id est non vult ut de carne sua gloriemur, nec vult carnaliter coli. Expectabant apostoli carnale quoddam regnum, & iam à resurrectione arbitrabantur inchoaturum esse hoc regnum, ut ex Actis apparet. Et carnalis opinio de Christo, item carnalis omnis cultus, quem caro per sese simulat, tangere illud Magdalenae est. At hunc carnalem cultum damnat, sicut & saepe aliâs, & subiicit ibi demum se contingendum esse, ubi ad patrem ascendit. Tangimus autem fide, & colimus eo cultu quem in nobis efficit. Nam ideo moritur, resurgit, ascendit Christus, ut omnia impleat, & vere regnet in omnium cordibus, quod in corporali regno ne potest quidem fieri. Sic & supra de suo regno loquitur Ioannis duodecimo, cum & illi somniarent corporale regnum. Nisi granum frumenti cadens in terram &c. Item ubi exaltatus fuero à terra, omnia traham ad me &c.

Noli me tangere. id est tangi non possum, nisi ascendero ad patrem, id est tunc fide me tangetis.

Dic eis, scilicet hoc meum regnum futurum esse, & in hoc me resurrexisse, ut ascendam.

## 20:21
Peace be to you, as the Father has sent me, so etc.

Christ first preaches the Gospel to the apostles themselves, when he offers peace and the Holy Spirit. For the Gospel is the preaching of peace, that is, which gladdens the conscience, Rom. 5: Being justified by faith, having peace, etc. Then he sends the apostles to preach the Gospel, and thus he declares why he suffered, and what kind his kingdom is to be: just as he says Luke 24: that repentance should be preached in his name, etc. As the Father has sent me, so I send you. That is, whatever the authority of my word is, let the same be yours also. Thus I send you, namely that you may remit sins, and bind the impious, that you may judge the world, and remit sins.

Latin:

Pax vobis, sicut misit me pater, sic &c.

Christus primum ipsis apostolis Evangelium praedicat, cum pacem offert & spiritum sanctum. Nam Evangelium est praedicatio pacis, hoc est quae conscientiam exhilaret, Rom. quinto: Iustificati ex fide pacem habentes &c. Deinde mittit apostolos Evangelium praedicaturos, atque ita declarat cur passus sit, cuiusmodi regnum suum futurum: quemadmodum Lucae ultimo ait: paenitentiam praedicari in nomine eius &c. Sicut me misit pater, sic ego mitto vos. id est quae mei autoritas verbi est, verbi est, eadem sit & vestri. Sic mitto vos scilicet ut remittatis peccata, & ligetis impios, ut iudicetis mundum, & remittatis peccata.

## 20:23
Receive the Holy Spirit. Whose sins you shall remit, they are remitted to them: and whose sins you shall retain, they are retained.

Why does he say, whose sins you shall remit, as if indeed it were in the power of the apostles to remit, even to those to whom God does not remit?

I respond: Christ spoke in such a way as to render consciences certain that it is certain that God forgives, when you have believed the word of the one evangelizing. He recalled the conscience to look back at His word. Let him who announces the Gospel not seek another sign or miracle by which he might be made more certain of the remission of sins, beyond the word itself. And indeed, He does not want another word to be required of him who announces the Gospel. This, therefore, is the meaning. Your Gospel is the word of God. Those who believe your word will believe God: those who believe your word, let them know that they are in no way deceived, let them know that they have certainly obtained the remission of sins. This is regarding the form of speaking. Furthermore, the Gospel embraces two things, to absolve and to bind. It absolves those to whom it remits sins, whether it is pronounced publicly or privately. Although Christ willed us to be baptized, so that faith might be confirmed by that sign. Thus, He willed the Gospel to be pronounced privately, or for us to be absolved privately, so that He might confirm the conscience of the one hearing that He is passing a divine sentence concerning him. The Gospel binds, both publicly and privately. Publicly, when it judges all the impious who despise it. But it binds privately those whom it rejects from the church, according to the rule prescribed Matthaei decimooctavo.

Latin:

Accipite spiritumsanctum. Quorum remiseritis peccata, remittuntur eis: & quo-rum retinueritis, retenta sunt.

Cur ait, quorum remiseritis, quasi vero penes apostoios sit remittere, etiam iis quibus non remittit deus?

Respondeo: Sic locutus est Christus, ut certas redderet conscientias, certum esse quod deus remittat, ubi verbo Evangelizantis credideris. Revocavit conscientiam ad respiciendum verbum eius. Qui Evangelium nunciat, ne quaerat aliud vel signum, vel miraculum, quo certior fiat de remissione peccatorum, praeter ipsum verbum. Et huius quidem non vult requiri aliud verbum qui annunciat Evangelium. Est ergo haec sententia. Vestrum Evangelium verbum dei est. Qui vestro verbo credent, illi deo credent: qui vestro verbo credent, sciant se nihil falli, sciant se certo consecutos remissionem peccatorum. Haec de forma loquendi. Porrò Evangelium duo complectitur, absolvere & ligare. Absolvit quibus peccata remittit, sive publice, sive privatim pronuncietur. Quanquam baptizari nos voluit Christus, ut eo signo confirmetur fides. Ita privatim pronunciari voluit Evangelium, seu privatim absolvi nos, ut conscientiam confirmaret audientem de se ferre sententiam divinam. Ligat Evangelium, & publice, & privatim. Publice, cum omnes impios qui contemnunt, iudicat. Privaetim vero ligat, quos ex ecclesia reiicit. iuxta regulam praescriptam Matthaei decimooctavo.

## 21:1 Argument
CHAPTER TWENTY-FIRST.

Just as above Christ appears to those mourning and anxious with fear, so here he appears to those laboring miserably. For Christ is not known, except to those who have been weaned and torn away from the breasts, as Isaiah twenty-eight says. That is, those who do not trust in any arm of flesh.

Latin:

CAPUT VIGESIMUM PRIMUM.

Sicut supra apparet Christus lugentibus & metu anxiis, ita hic apparet misere laborantibus. Neque enim cognoscitur Christus, nisi iis qui ablactati sunt & avulsi ab uberibus, sicut Esaias vigesimo octavo ait. hoc est qui non fidunt brachio ulli carnis.

## 21:3
And that night they caught nothing.

That they fish in vain all night, although Christ wished to indicate the power of doctrine, nevertheless it can be referred entirely to all things, so that whatever we undertake by faith, we may carry out trusting that with God blessing, the matter will succeed. Thus Proverbs third: In all your ways think of him, and he will direct your steps. Furthermore, he wished to indicate the condition and nature of the word: for nothing is caught, except by the word of Christ. Just as he also calls himself the door, the way, etc. elsewhere.

Latin:

Et illa nocte nihil prendiderunt.

Quod illi tota nocte frustra piscantur, quanquam voluerit doctrinae vim indicare Christus, tamen omnino ad omnia referri potest, ut quicquid suscipimus fide, geramus confidentes, quod benedicente deo res successura sit. Sic Proverbiorum tertio: In omnibus viis cogita illum, & ipse gressus tuos diriget. Porrò verbi conditionem & naturam indicare voluit: nihil enim capitur, nisi Christi verbo. Sicut & aliâs se vocat hostium, viam &c.

## 21:7
It is the Lord.

From the miracle they recognize Christ, who always confirms faith by the outcome of temptation, as he says in eighty-fifth Psalm: I have cried to you, and you have heard me, there is no one like you among the gods.

Latin:

Dominus est.

Ex miraculo agnoscunt Christum, qui semper eventu tentationis confirmat fidem, sicut octuagesimo quinto Psalmo dicit: Clamavi ad te, & exaudisti me, non est similis tui in diis.

## 21:15
Simon, son of John, do you love me more than these?

Because Peter had fallen in a particular way, he therefore had to be restored by a specific consolation. Some argue thus: Christ asks whether he loves more than the rest, therefore he entrusted more to him than to the others. I respond, because the matter concerns consoling and raising up Peter: and in this place is the express remission of Peter's sin: to this must be referred this, 'more than these.' For these things cohere rightly: 'Peter, do you love more than these, therefore more has been forgiven you.' And Christ, wishing to confirm his conscience, reminds him of his own judgment: 'To whom less is forgiven, he loves less,' Luke 7, as if he were saying thus: 'Take heart, if you love, your sins are forgiven.'

Latin:

Simon Ioannis diligis me plus iis?

Quia Petrus peculiariter lapsus erat, ideo propria consolatione restituendus erat. Quidam sic argumentantur: Christus interrogat plus'ne diligat reliquis, ideo illi plus caeteris commendavit. Respondeo, quia agitur de consolando & erigendo Petro: & hoc loco est expressa remissio peccati Petri: eò referendum est hoc, plus his. Recte enim cohaerent haec: Petrus plus diligis his, ergo plura tibi remissa sunt. Et Christus volens eius conscientiam confirmare, admonet eum suae sententiae: Cui minus dimittitur, minus diligit, Lucae septimo, quasi sic dicat: Confide, si diligis remissa sunt peccata.

## 21:17
Peter was saddened because he said to him a third time, 'Do you love me?'

He teaches also what kind of men pastors ought to be, namely those who love the Lord: also how they ought to be tested. For he questions Peter three times, and a third time he shakes his faith. For faith is to feel that we are accepted by Christ. And Christ questions him in such a way, as if he thinks ill of Peter. When he senses this, he now begins to waver, but he recovers his breath nonetheless. 'When you were younger, you girded yourself and walked where you wished,' that is, you lived in the righteousness of the flesh, and according to your own will. 'You will stretch out your hands' yours. that is, you will wait for it from another. And he will lead you where you do not wish. that is, to the cross.

Latin:

Contristatus est Petrus, quia dixit ei tertio, amas me.

Docet item quales oporteat esse pastores, nempe qui diligunt dominum: item quàm oporteat esse probatos. Ter enim interrogat Petrum, & tertio fidem eius labefacit. Nam fides est sentire quod accepti simus Christo. Et Christus sic interrogat, quasi male sentiat de Petro. Quod ubi ille sentit, iam nutare incipit, sed respirat tamen. Cum iunior esses, cingebas te & ambulabas quò volebas. id est vivebas in iustitiis carnis, & pro tuo arbitrio. Extendes manus tuas. id est expectabis ab alio. Et ducet te quò non vis. id est ad crucem.

## 21:21
Lord, but what about this man?

The example is set forth of those looking at another's calling, and another's works: and it is commanded that we follow our own calling: and indeed that we follow Christ, that is, that we look only to him, and perceive him to be the one who saves. What is that to you? namely, whatever I am about to do concerning him. You follow me. that is, do not look at others. And we know that his testimony is true. This passage is for the authority of the apostles against impious men, who boast that the apostles were also men, just as above in the fifteenth chapter: You will bear witness, because you have been with me from the beginning. etc.

THE END.

IN THE YEAR 1523. IN THE MONTH OF MAY.

Latin:

Domine hic autem quid?

Propositum est exemplum respicientium alienam vocationem, & aliena opera: & praecipitur ut nostram vocationem sequamur: & sequamur quidem Christum, id est respiciamus illum tantum, & sentiamus illum esse qui salvet. Quid ad te? scilicet quicquid de eo facturus sum. Tu me sequere. id est ne respice alios. Et scimus quia verum est testimonium eius. Hic locus pro autoritate apostolorum est contra impios homines, qui & apostolos homines fuisse iactitant, sicut supra capite decimo quinto: Vos testimonium perhibebitis, quia ab initio mecum estis. &c.

FINIS.

ANNO M. D. XXIII. MENSE MAIO.
